# Chapter 1: Nine Days of Rain

Neela came back to herself on the window ledge with rain in her mouth, and before she opened her eyes she counted.

Two.

Ammu's pouch held two smears. She did not need to look. She had counted them by feel at dusk, the way she counted exits and stair treads, and the number had not improved overnight.

Her bones were still settling. The change back always felt like being poured into a jar too small, and then the jar cracked and she was only a woman, naked and shaking, crouched on teak wet with monsoon. Grey light. The cistern chain clanked once somewhere below, and the red smell of soaked laterite rose off the whole fort.

"Here."

The cloth came over her shoulders before she had finished shivering. It was warm. Not warm from the room. Warm from the brazier, the way it had been warm every dawn since the vow, rewarmed in the night by a man who said nothing about it.

She pulled it tight and looked in. Devan stood with the shutter held back on his forearm. His other hand was closed. When he saw her looking, he opened it. A grey river pebble lay on his palm, smooth as a tooth.

"Habit," he said.

"You don't need it now."

"I know." He closed his fingers again. "Come in. The third slat's going to complain."

It did. She slid down off the ledge and her knees nearly went. He caught her elbow, then let go the moment she had her feet, which was the way he did everything now: help given, hand removed, no comment. On the floor by the brazier sat the covered dish of rice and curd, the cup, the kindi with its spout toward her side.

She ate on her knees, fast and shaking, as the hollow hunger demanded. He sat on his heels across from her and waited. When her hands steadied, she said, "Two."

"Two what?"

"You know what."

"I'd like you to say it."

Of course he would. He never put a name to her fear for her. He made her do it. "Two smears. Two nights of the other one. Then Ammu's thumb is gone."

He nodded slowly. He answered with facts, because that was what he had. "The ache comes a moon after the last night you turn. Not before. That's what you told me."

"It's what I know. Nobody's ever tested it on purpose." She scraped the bowl clean. "Two nights of the other one is all the raid gets, Devan. After that I'm a woman with split nails and a fever, climbing a wall."

"Then we plan for the two nights."

"We've been planning for nine days."

"We've been resting for nine days," he said. "It isn't the same."

That was fair, and she hated it. She set the bowl down and rubbed her right wrist. The scar there was still raw under its binding, the ridges torn by the forced resin and slow to close. Across the room, without seeming to, he pressed his thumb to his own wrist. A thin red line lay there, matching hers. It had not faded either.

They both looked at it. Neither said a thing.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The hall smelled of damp wool and lamp oil. Rain came off the eaves in ropes, and beyond the courtyard wall the whole hill country was one grey roar.

Neela went down by the east stair. She took it fast, counting turns under her breath. At the bottom she glanced across the court toward the west stair, and then she went the long way round by the kitchen yard.

Chinna was at the kitchen door with her bad hip propped on the frame. Neela stopped and asked after it, and after the wager that had no winner, and was told it was a fine thing to have a warden's wife who remembered which hip. Unni came through with a tray and she took the cloth off it, found the fish, and told him his mother's salt had been good last week. He went red to the ears. Small courtesies. They had once been the only wealth she owned.

The names kept the house standing in her head. Chinna. Unni. Gopan on the cistern wheel. Kannan, up in his loft with the white goat. And Raiu, whom she still said under her breath on the stairs.

There was one more name, and she did not say it even there. Ammu. Not two weeks dead, and her thumb of resin sat in the cord pouch against Neela's hip. Every smear Neela spent was a piece of her sister going onto her own skin, and there were two pieces left.

She had been twenty-one years a number. She had been a name for nine days.

Then there were the other names, the ones she'd put in a crown record herself, by choice, with a pen. *Neela, of Kettamala.* The last rider had carried it out of the pass before the road went to river. Her witness entry was ash. The slip with her birth name was in Chathu's keeping. No master in the world could now say *she is ours, she is vouched.* To the guild she was rogue. To the crown she was an unvouched odiyan with her name on a border writ. Both would hunt her. Neither was in a hurry. The rains had given her four months of locked road, and she knew exactly how thin that mercy was.

Devan had laid Ittan's things out on the long table. The hide strip, the oilcloth, the sums. And the small tally book from the false-bottomed chest, forty leaves in a clerk's cramped hand.

"Read what you can," he said.

She sat and turned the pages. Dates, sums, place names. A pass-village here, a ford there. Down one margin, in a column no clerk had meant her to understand, ran small marks she knew in her bones. A hook for goods delivered. A slash for coin paid. Three dots for *balance carried*. Resin House shorthand, the kind apprentices scratched on tapping sheds. Ittan, she guessed, had copied it off the guild's own receipts.

"This is guild reckoning," she said. "Not all of it. Counts and payments I can follow. Names I can't, they're set in the masters' own marks." She ran a finger down the column. "Ittan paid the house in instalments. Four of them. Look. Slash, slash, slash, slash, hook."

"Hook is?"

"A delivery. Something was sent in return."

"What?"

"I don't know. A man, a message." She turned another leaf. "Or a death. Chathu writes them all the same."

Beside three of the lines, tucked into the margin as small as a pressed seed, someone had drawn a bird. A sunbird, wings folded, long curved bill. It was not the queen's seal, whose wings spread wide in green wax. This one sat quiet, the way a clerk's tick sits quiet. She tapped it.

"These mean something. I can't read it."

"Guild mark?"

"Not one I was ever taught."

He leaned to look, and said nothing, and she watched him decide not to guess. That was the thing she was learning about him. He kept his guesses in his pocket until they were facts.

"Here is what I have," he said, and straightened. "Ittan's chit is for my death. The writ with the queen's seal says who paid. That is one murder, and it's mine, and a high court will find it hard to hear from the man it was done to." He rested two fingers on the hide. "Asha's contract is not here. It is in the guild's book. Chathu enters every contract against a page. Muthu saw a black-covered book in his arms on the cook-house step."

"The ledger."

"I need that entry. Not a story, not a confession. The contract itself, dated and entered, with the buyer's name. A court will take paper."

She had known it was coming. She had known since Muthu came back from the fodder run with a face like wet slate and said *Chathu, black book, held it to his chest like a child.* She made herself say her side flat, in the same voice he used.

"I don't want the entry. I want the book. Every page in it. Every one back in the hand of the person it names." Her hands had gone cold on the table. "Mine is in there too, Devan. Whatever's left of it. Every sister I have. If you take one leaf out and leave the rest, Chathu rewrites the house in a month."

"I need one page."

"All of them."

They looked at each other across Ittan's sums.

It would have been simple to be angry. She had expected to be. She waited for the heat, and it did not come, because he had not lied to her about what he wanted, not once, and it left her nothing to push against.

"Those aren't the same plan," she said.

"No."

"They both go through the same door."

"Yes."

"Then we go together." She heard how quickly she said it. "And argue after. On the road. In the dark. Wherever."

His mouth did something small. "I was going to say that."

"Then why didn't you?"

"I wanted to see if you would." He turned the tally book closed with one finger, the sunbird vanishing under the cover. "We take what the raid gives us. The page for Asha's contract is mine. The rest is yours to answer for. If it comes to one hand's reach, I will not choose for you."

"Will you promise that?"

"I won't answer that," he said gently, and for a moment she thought he was refusing. Then he finished. "Because I do not know what I will do with a dying man's blade at my throat and your life on the table. I know what I want to do. I'll say that much."

It was the most honest refusal she had ever been given, and it pleased her more than a promise.

She pulled the hide strip toward her and made it a map with her thumbnail. "The ridge track is quicker. It's also the one the pass-village watcher walks. So we take the wood road, by the lower ford. Half a day longer."

"And the smears?"

"The first goes tonight. I run the road ahead of you and find where the rain has cut it." She said the rest before he could. "That leaves one for inside the wall. One night to get in, find a black book and get out."

"And tonight's costs you a hollow morning, and a night nearer the ache."

"Yes."

"You've priced it."

"I counted it," she said. "It's the same thing."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Ittan's grille was a hand's width of iron in the padippura guardroom door. Unni opened the bolt for them and stood well back, not quite meeting anyone's eye.

The councillor had lost the shine off him in nine days. His clove smell had gone stale. But he rose from the cot as they came, a gracious man hosting two guests, and bowed with the ghost of the old ease.

"My friend. Warden's lady. Forgive the lamp, I'm told oil is dear in a siege."

"It is not a siege," Devan said.

"No. Of course. The rain." He smiled, and the smile slid off the end of itself. "My friend, I wonder. Has anyone thought of her medicine? The willow bark. It must be boiled slow, an hour, and she likes the window open in the rain. If the physicians let her sicken in a shut room, she coughs herself to rags."

Neela watched him. Not his words. His eyes.

Devan said, "I'll send word to the house physicians by the first rider the road allows."

"I'd be grateful. I would be very grateful."

He asked nothing else. Not for his freedom, not for his rank, not a lawyer, not a letter. In the cold guardroom he leaned on the iron and begged only for the boiling of bark, and every few breaths his gaze slid past Devan's shoulder, past her own, to the dark corner by the door. It stayed there a heartbeat. Then it dropped to the floor, careful.

When Unni's boot scraped on the flagstones, Ittan flinched like a hit horse.

She had seen men afraid of a warden. She had seen men afraid of a blade, and of the other one, once they learned what walked the hill. This was neither. This was a man who had looked at the thing that would come next and decided Devan, with his wires and his writs and his locked grille, was the kinder alternative.

Outside in the rain she took Devan's sleeve. "He's not afraid of you."

"I noticed."

"He's afraid of whoever he thinks will come for him."

"Or for what he'd say first." He pulled his hood up. "Say that again when we have the ledger. It will matter."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At dusk the rain thinned to a grey mist, and she climbed the west rampart with him because she could not sit still, and because the walls were the only place the two smears in her pouch did not feel like a drumbeat.

Two. She would spend the first of them tonight, as she had said, to run the wood road by moonrise. She would lie awake first and count the cost of it. Another night's resin, another night nearer the ache, another night in the dark with a man who would hold a river pebble in his palm that he no longer needed.

Below the wall the Monkey Pass road ran down in a pale scar, a cart road gone to a brown stream. No cart could cross it. No horse. The gorge above it ran with water and the cliffs shed it in sheets. By every rule she knew, the road was empty and would be empty until the rains broke.

All day the langurs had barked from the cliff face. The sound had gone round her head for nine days, shrill and furious, the way a house sounds when a kettle won't stop. She had stopped hearing it. Which was why she heard the silence at once.

It did not come as a hush. It came as a hole. The nearest troop stopped mid-bark. Then the next. Then the ones across the gorge, and the ones high on the shoulder, until the whole cliff held its breath and there was only rain on stone and the brown water roaring.

Devan had gone still beside her. She knew the stillness by now.

"They bark at cats," she said quietly. "They bark at fire. At a goat-boy with a stick. They go quiet for one thing."

"Strangers on the road," he said.

"Yes."

"Nobody can walk that road."

"No."

She counted, because it was what she did. Her lips moved on the numbers: the tower, the gorge turn, the length of the wet stone between them and the first bend, how many breaths a man would need to cross it. The langurs held their silence. It lasted through all her counting, and well beyond it.

Somebody was down there in the rain, on a road that did not exist, walking toward Kettamala.

# Chapter 2: The Justice at the Gate

Bhaskaran's horn cut across the kalari in the middle of the third form, and Devan stopped with one foot lifted.

One long note. Two short. Strangers at the padippura, unarmed, not many. He had taught the signal himself, and in five years it had never been blown on a day like this one.

He stood in the red earth with sesame smoke in his nose and rain running off his shoulders. The pass was a river. The road below the fort had been empty all night; he had watched it until the dark took it, and nothing had moved on it. So they had not used the road.

He took the north steps two at a time.

He had left Neela asleep. When the langurs went silent at dusk she had judged no night run worth a smear with strangers on the road, and she had kept both. She had lain down at last like a woman who'd been told she could sleep. He did not like leaving her. He liked less the thought of waking her to this before he knew what it was.

At the gate the rain came down in ropes off the roof. Bhaskaran stood under it with the horn still in his fist. Unni stood behind him, staring at the five figures in the arch as if they might be a joke someone had told badly.

Four of them stood in a line. The fifth stood in front.

She was a lean woman of fifty-eight, soaked from hem to hip, with a grey plait dripping down her cloak. Her boots were caked to the shin in red scree. She looked like someone who had spent the night climbing something she disliked.

"Warden of Kettamala," she said. Her voice was dry as a split pot. "I am Rema, justice of the high court at Kodiyur. I regret the hour."

"You walked."

"We climbed. Your goat path is not fit for goats." She pushed back her hood. "I bring the queen's warrant. May I bring it out of the wet?"

He should have said yes at once. He looked at her escort instead.

Four, in oiled cloaks, each with a green sunbird sash worn across the chest. Their packs were oilcloth, tied tight, and dry. A dull sheen showed on them, not a bead of water standing. Their boots were stiff and square at the heel. Mud reached the ankle and no higher, and not a scuff marked any sole.

He had walked that goat path first at nineteen, on a dare. It had taken the skin off his knees.

"Bring it in," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The warrant came out of a sleeve of oilcloth, folded in three. Rema handed it across without ceremony and watched him read.

*Councillor Ittan, Keeper of the Passes, charged with treason under the wartime charter. To be delivered into the keeping of the crown at Kodiyur.* Below it, in a clerk's round hand, ran a list of Ittan's offices. And below that, the wax.

Green. Crushed leaf, not clove. A sunbird with wings spread.

He did not move his thumb. He let his eye run down the left wing to the tip, and there it was: a chip no bigger than a grain of rice, the same notch as the seal on the writ that lay in a hollow behind the third slat of his chamber.

Of course it was the same. It was one seal. He told himself so twice, and kept his face where it was.

"The charter was invoked when the spring raids began," Rema said. "You were sent notice."

"I read it."

"Then you know the warrant outranks the warden's hold. I am to carry him to Kodiyur."

"On what road?"

For the first time she smiled. It was a small, tired thing. "That is the difficulty, yes."

"I have a request," he said. He made it flat, because flat was the only voice he trusted. "I mean to put Ittan before the court. Not as an accused man. As a witness to my sister's murder, six years ago, on the west stair of this fort. He sold the passes. I think the same hand bought her death."

Bhaskaran, behind him, went very still.

"You think," said Rema.

"I can show you the tallies. I can show you his chest of waxed blanks, his book, the sums. I cannot yet show you that the hand is one hand. That's what a hearing is for."

She studied him for the length of a slow breath. He had faced raiders with less sense of being weighed.

"The charter lets a warden hold a prisoner until assize when the roads are shut," she said at last. "It lets a justice sit assize where she stands. I stand here." She folded the oilcloth over her knuckles. "Three days, Warden. You will show me your proof. Ittan will answer. At the end of it I decide whether I carry a traitor out, or a witness, or no one."

"Three days."

"You have kept him well?"

"He has a cot and a lamp. He has asked for one thing."

"What?"

"That his wife's willow bark be boiled slowly."

Something moved in her face and was gone. "I shall want my First Guard at his door. A courtesy to the crown."

"Bhaskaran's men hold that door."

"Then both," Rema said pleasantly, "and you will not mind the company."

He minded it very much. He had a whole fort, sixty soldiers and the only wall for a hundred miles, and he understood now that all of it had been quietly put to one side. A woman with a plait and a sleeve of paper had walked into his gate, and the gate had opened because it was meant to.

"Both," he said.

He turned to the four. "Your names, for the garrison roll?"

"Crown escort does not give them, Warden." Rema was already shaking rain from her cloak. "They go by place. First, Second, and so on. It saves the clerks a great deal of ink."

"First," said the nearest. A woman, close to his own age, built like a rope. Her voice had no colour in it.

"Second."

"Third."

"Fourth."

Numbers. He had heard a woman say *Seventeen* in the dark a month ago, and it had settled in his ribs like a stone. This one fit the same hole too neatly.

First Guard shrugged her pack higher on her shoulder. Her cuff rode up. On the inside of her left wrist lay a single pale seam, the length of a finger, smooth and shiny in the grey light. One clean line, flat to the skin. Nothing like the thick ridges that lay layer over layer on his wife's wrists, ten years of burning laid down on each other. The guard's collar gaped as she moved, and the skin at her throat was bare, unmarked.

Lamp-burn, he thought. Or a rope. A night guard carries a lamp too long, wraps the cloth wrong. Half his own garrison wore marks like it, on hands and forearms. She tugged the sleeve down, and he looked away, and the thought went into the place where he kept things that were not yet facts.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Janu fed them in the hall, because Janu would have fed the flood itself. She scolded the mud and the hour and the queen's justice, in that order, and set a bowl of rice and fish in front of Rema twice.

Rema ate like a person who had been hungry on principle. The escort did not eat in the hall. They stood by the pillars, two and two, and Janu's girls carried their bowls to them, and the bowls came back full.

"Your goat path has a drop at the boulder," Rema said between mouthfuls. "A fall I would not want my enemies to take. My escort strung ropes at the three worst places. In the dark, mind. I would have turned back at the first."

"Good soldiers."

"The best I have ever travelled with." She wiped her fingers. "I have gone on circuit with a great many soldiers, Warden. They snore. They grumble. They sleep on their feet at the wall and wake very cross. In nine hours on that mountain I did not see one of those four sit down, let alone sleep."

She said it lightly, as praise.

He said, "I'll have the south range made ready for them."

"Kind."

He said no more. When she looked toward Janu's steaming pot, he glanced across at the First Guard against her pillar. She was looking at nothing, at the middle of the air, her hands loose at her sides. She stood very still. He watched her sash a long while and could not catch it rising.

He picked up his bowl and did not taste it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Neela was sitting on the edge of the bed with her hair half braided when he came in. She had heard the horn. He could see she had counted the doors.

"Before you ask," he said.

Her fingers stopped.

"A justice of the high court. Rema. Fifty-eight, tired, no fool. She brings a warrant for Ittan under the wartime charter, sealed in the queen's green wax. I asked for an assize, with Ittan as witness to Asha. She gave me three days. She sits here. Her escort stand at Ittan's door beside Bhaskaran's men. I agreed to that."

"You agreed."

"I had no way not to." He sat on his heels so she would not have to look up. "Four escort. They gave numbers, not names. They came by the goat path in a night of rain, and their packs are dry and their boots are new. Rema says they fixed ropes in the dark and she never saw one of them sleep."

"Everyone sleeps."

"That is what I thought."

"What else?"

"The seal on the warrant has a chip at the left wing tip. It's the same as the writ." He moved his hand once, flat. "And the First Guard, a woman, has a mark on the inside of her left wrist. One pale seam, about so long, smooth. Not like yours. Nothing at her throat. I took it for a lamp burn."

Neela said, "Which wrist?"

"Left. Inside."

"Mm," said Neela. She looked at her own right wrist, with its binding, and then at the shutters. Her next breath came a moment late. He saw it, and kept it.

"There's more," he said, because he had told himself in the passage that he would not stop at the easy end. "When I came up the stair I stopped at the corridor and told Raiu's relief, the new one, that you were not to be woken, and that if the justice's people asked, you were unwell. I was going to say the same to Janu. I was going to put you in the south range behind a bar. I thought, if she doesn't see you, she can't ask."

He heard how it sounded. The silence after it was not angry. It was attentive.

"But you came up here instead," she said.

"I came up to say it to you first. You have been shut behind my fences for a month, with good reason. I do not think it is a good reason now. I do not want you behind one that you did not choose."

She put her hands flat on her knees. "Do you know what it looks like," she said, "when a warden's wife is ill the morning a crown justice arrives? After the lord of the house has spent a month doing nothing but bar his shutters?"

"Like guilt."

"Like the only story that needs no help from anyone." She shook out the rest of her hair. "Hide me and she'll ask where I am. Hide me for three days and she'll find out, and she'll have asked it about every servant who ever carried a tray to my door. If I stand in the hall and Janu complains about my cooking, I am only a wife. A wife with a name on a crown writ, who ought to be proud of it."

"She will ask questions."

"She will." Neela met his eyes. "I'll answer them. I'll say only true things. It is more than most people do, and Chathu spent twenty years teaching me which ones to choose."

He had wanted to say *no*. He had been holding it since the gate, in the part of him that had learned at twenty-six what happens to the people behind a locked door. He turned it over and found, to his surprise, that she was right, and that the wanting was only fear.

"True things," he said.

"And you tell me everything she says. Everything. Before I ask."

"I've done that once this morning."

"You did well." Her mouth tilted. "Do it again."

"I will."

She stood. She caught his wrist as she passed, quick, the way she did at the kalari when she was about to correct his guard, and the bracelets of scar on her skin pressed hot against the pulse in his. Then she let go. "Which way do I walk into the hall?" she said. "From the east stair?"

"From the east stair."

"Then I'll take the west."

He looked at her.

"She won't have noticed which I use," Neela said. "I will." And she was out of the door before he could answer, with the third slat of the shutter complaining behind her in the draught.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Rema had moved to the warden's chair. Devan saw it as he came down the east steps: not a challenge, a convenience, the chair nearest the brazier. Janu had put a cushion on it. He found that he had no objection to the cushion.

His objection was to the man beside the chair. Muthu. Cheerful, loud, rain still on his collar, telling the justice that the fort had never seen so honoured a guest in his time and the south range had the best roof in the hill country. He laughed at his own words. He stopped laughing when Rema looked at him, and for the length of a breath his face was very quiet.

Devan marked it, and let it lie.

"A sentence of business, Warden, and then I shall sleep like your garrison," Rema said, as he came up. "The closing writ you sent down to Kodiyur with the last rider. It bore a household witness. A woman's name, written in a clerk's hand and a fresh hand beside it. *Neela, of Kettamala*."

"My wife."

"I know whose wife." She folded her hands. "I should like to meet her. By name. In my experience a writ is only as honest as the person who signed it, and I would like to look at her while I read."

He had meant to say something. Some courteous, unhelpful, dilatory thing.

"She is here," said Neela from the foot of the west stair.

She stood with one hand on the newel, on the parrot's smooth broken beak, and a face as unremarkable as a wall.

# Chapter 3: Numbers, Not Names

"Ward of Kunjan Chirayil," Rema said, reading it off the marriage papers as if she were tasting it. "Of Kodiyur."

"That is the name on my marriage papers," said Neela.

She had crossed the hall at an even pace, with her shoulders down. She had taken the west stair on purpose, and the parrot's beak was still smooth under her palm in memory. Now she stood before the warden's chair with the whole hall listening. Janu hovered with a ladle. Muthu leaned on a pillar. Devan had come to stand at the end of the long bench, one step behind her left shoulder, where a man stands when he means to say nothing.

"Where were you raised?"

"In a house with a great many girls. A master kept the accounts. I learned to count there."

"A warehouse household," Rema said. It was not a question. She had filled the gap herself and looked pleased with the fit.

Neela let her. She said nothing false, and she did not say what Rema had heard. Twenty years of Chathu had taught her that the best lie was a true sentence with room around it.

"You met the warden before the wedding?"

"I met him at the wedding."

"A council match."

"Yes."

"And you signed the closing writ of your own will?"

"Nobody held my hand," Neela said. "The pass was closing and someone had to."

Rema's eyes went to her wrist. The binding showed under the cuff of the day cloth, a strip of clean linen over the torn ridges. The justice's gaze rested there as lightly as a fly, and moved on.

"A burn?"

"Yes. It's healing."

Neela did not look at the pillars. She did not need to. At the edge of her sight, First Guard stood square and still, and her eyes had gone to the binding a moment after the justice's. They stayed there a breath longer. Then they dropped, and her right hand drifted across and closed on her own left cuff.

Neela kept her face where it was. She counted the pillars. Eight. She filed the hand away.

"Are you content here, Lady Neela?"

*Content.* A clever word, with a trapdoor in it.

"I'm alive," Neela said. "I have a name in this house. Ask Janu how many others I've learned."

"Eleven," said Janu, from behind the ladle, in the voice of a woman defending a debt. "And she got Chinna's hip right the first time."

A few of the soldiers laughed. Rema smiled, and it reached her eyes by a hair. "You will be sworn at assize, you understand. Wife of the warden, household witness to the writ. You will answer what I ask."

"Yes."

"Good. I find the truth sits best on people who are tired of the other thing." She rose from the warden's chair with one hand on the small of her back. "Go to bed, all of you. I shall begin with the tallies in the morning."

Muthu came off his pillar and offered his arm to the justice, laughing again. Neela watched his mouth. It was a good laugh. It stopped a hair too soon.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The escort had not gone to bed.

The south range was lit and ready, Janu's girls had seen to that, but the four stood where they had stood at supper, two and two by the pillars, packs at their feet. Neela took a jug of water from the kitchen girl at the door and went down the hall with it, because she asked everyone.

It was the rule she had built her whole self on. A servant, a guard, a goat-boy. You ask the name, and you say it back once so it knows you meant it. A person who has a name can't be walked past.

She stopped before the first pillar and held out the cup. "Water? I'm Neela."

"Thank you, no." The woman had a rope's body and a face with the colour taken out. "First."

"First what?"

"First Guard."

"Has your mother a name for you?"

"I am First."

It was said without heat. There was no insult in it and no joke. Neela had expected a wall, a person standing behind the word. There was no one behind it. It was only flat, and it was worse than a wall.

She went on to the next. The man at the second pillar glanced at First before he spoke. It was a short look, the look of a dog waiting for the hand. "Second."

The third said, "Third," to the air above her head. The fourth, the youngest by the look of the throat, wet his lips and said, "Fourth," and added nothing, and lowered his eyes.

Twenty-one years she had answered to *Seventeen*. She had thought she knew every way a number could sit in a mouth. She had never heard four people say it as if their mothers had given it to them.

The jug was heavy. She poured a cup for herself and drank it to give her hands something to do.

The warrant had not frightened her. A warrant was paper, and paper could be read. These four did not read. Not one of them had asked for a chair, or a boiled egg, or where the privy was. They had taken a night mountain in a rain that had sent the langurs silent, and their packs were dry.

Who empties four people out, she wondered, and then sends them up a mountain at night with a queen's sash on?

She did not know. That was what chilled her. A man with a hidden name had something to lose, and she could always work out the cost of losing it. These four seemed to have nothing hidden at all. They had been hollowed out and set on guard.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The yard was black and wet. She went out by the kitchen door because she could not yet face the bed, and found the hounds loose.

For nine days of rain they had been shut in the long kennel behind the stables. Devan had let them out at dusk that day, without saying why. All fourteen ran the yard now, as the fort's hounds had always run it, big rough brindled animals who did not trouble anyone who belonged.

They did not trouble her. One shouldered her thigh as she passed. They had always taken her scent as if she had been born in it. But when she looked toward the south range, where the escort's lamps burned yellow in the shutter-cracks, there was not a dog on that side of the yard.

They stood in a half-ring at the edge of the lamplight, thirty paces off. Heads low. One whined, high and thin through its nose. Another turned in a tight circle and sat down with its back to the south range, like a man pretending not to hear.

"They've been like that since the gate."

Kannan was on the ladder of his loft, a small shape against the stable wall, with the white goat nosing at his ankle. Pepper bit at the rung. He kicked her gently.

"Since dusk," Kannan said. "They wouldn't go near when the justice's people came in. My mother Bindu's dog did that, once. When a snake was in the thatch."

"Did it find the snake?"

"It found it." He considered. "Bhaskaran says they're only wet."

"Wet dogs don't whine like that."

"No," said Kannan, with a nine-year-old's contempt for the sergeant.

She stood a while with her hand on a hound's rough head. It leaned into her palm. She should have found that comforting, and she told herself that she did.

"Go to sleep," she said. "Take Pepper."

"Pepper doesn't go to sleep. She plans."

"Then plan quietly."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The dispensary smelled of camphor and old paper. Eravi sat bent over a mortar with his lamp turned low, a stooped man who always looked as if the ceiling were coming down on his shoulders. He did not ask why she had come at that hour. He took her hand and turned it to the light.

"Three nails," he said. "Split to the quick. This one has bled into the bed."

"I slept badly."

"You sleep like a hunted deer, my lady, and your husband has the same trouble." He reached behind him for a pot of green clay, thumbed off the lid and sniffed it. "This is good for splitting. I had it from the justice's people only this evening. The young one, the fourth, came for his blisters. He left me a pot for thanks."

"Fourth Guard came to you?"

"He had a bad heel. They had climbed all night in new boots, whatever else you say." He worked a smear on her first nail with the tip of his little finger. "Bark salve. Their own. It will draw the heat out."

It was warm. Then the smell reached her.

Bitter. Scorched tamarind, and sap that had been burnt on a stone, and under it a thin sweet note of pine.

Night had fallen an hour ago. Resin on a wrist after sunset meant a turning inside a minute, here, under a physician's lamp. Her mind went cold and quick, the way it did on a wall.

She looked. The clay sat on the nail and on nothing else. Not the quick of the finger, not the wrist, not the pulse in it. She kept her hand where it was, because snatching it back would have been a sentence. Then she took the edge of her cloth from her lap, as if to dab at a stray smear, and wiped the nail clean under Eravi's eyes. He only grunted and reached for the linen.

She counted the minute. Sixty, slowly, against the tick of the lamp. Her throat stayed her own. Her skin stayed skin.

She had smelled that bitterness every night of her adult life. She had smelled it on her own wrists. On the day Chathu first held her hand over the clay cup she had smelled it for hours, in her hair and on her pillow, and spent a year trying to wash it out.

"What bark is it?"

"I never asked. A hill bark of some kind." He bandaged the nail. "It will be a good deal for the price."

Many barks were bitter. That was all it was. Half the tanning yards in Varanad smelled of scorched something. The justice's party had come over the mountains, and in the mountains there were many trees.

*Many barks are bitter*, she told herself, and counted the nails on her left hand, and got to four, and lost the number, and counted again.

"You've gone quiet," said Eravi mildly.

"It's late."

"It is." He put the pot in her palm. "Keep it. I have no use for what I don't understand."

She walked out with the lid pressed down under her thumb. She did not open it again.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The hall was dark but for the brazier and one lamp at the far end. She had come down to leave the pot in the strongbox with the rest of the things she did not want to carry, and she stopped in the arch because someone was talking.

Devan stood alone before the long bench.

The bench was empty. He had set a stool at the head of it, perhaps to be the justice, and he faced it with his hands clasped behind his back and his shoulders square, like a man about to be shot.

"My sister Asha, who was twenty-two," he told the stool, "was killed on the west stair of this fort by a creature that could not have entered by any gate, and I have reason to believe, and have long believed, that the Keeper of the Passes, in concert with persons I am not yet able to name, did—" He stopped. His jaw worked. "No."

He began again. "In the sixth year before this one."

He stopped again.

"The tally," he said, to the stool, with great force. "Bhaskaran passed a cart. The wax on the tally is council wax, and I submit, I submit in the strongest—" He stood there. The lamp flickered. He took a breath that reached the roof, and let it go, and said, in a voice she had never heard from him, very low, "I can't make it come out in order."

She had seen him with a raider's arrow in his arm. She had seen him not flinch while Eravi bled him twice. She had never seen the warden of Kettamala afraid of a stool.

She had come down to leave a pot and go. She stepped out of the arch instead, into the edge of the lamplight.

"That was bad," she said.

He turned. He did not startle. He did, for a moment, go the colour of the wall. "How long were you there?"

"Since *creature*."

"Ah."

"You used *I submit* twice. Nobody has submitted anything in your whole life. Your face is going to do that thing in front of a hundred people." She came down the hall with the pot in her hand. "Also you said *reason to believe*, *long believed* and *in concert*. That is three ways of telling her you aren't sure."

"I am not sure."

"Then don't say so in three ways. Say the part you are." She set the pot on the bench. "What can you prove?"

"Asha is dead."

"Prove it. How?"

"Janu found her. The stair. The wounds."

"Good. That's one. What else?"

He thought. She watched him lay it out the way he laid out a patrol, in a line, nothing wasted. "The tally. The tally was passed by Bhaskaran on the strength of council wax. It matches the satchel on the narrows. It matches the token Asha kept. The dot and the split are the same."

"Say that."

"I did say that."

"You said it with *submit*." She took the stool away and stood where it had been, and put her hands behind her back, and made her face a justice's: patient, tired, rather bored. "Warden. Tell me about the wax."

He looked at her. His mouth did something odd. "The wax is council wax. It carries the dot-and-split mark. Bhaskaran passed a tally of that wax at the new moon before my wedding. The tally number matches a satchel taken at the narrows. It matches a token my sister hid before she died." He stopped. She waited. He stopped some more.

"Good," she said softly.

"That's all?"

"That's all. Don't add the rest."

"The rest is the point."

"The rest is her point. Let her get there herself. If you hand a judge your conclusion she'll take it for a thing you want. If she finds it, she'll think it is true." She let the justice drop from her face. "You've commanded sixty men for five years. You say *go* and they go. This is the same, and it is harder. You're not asking for obedience. You're asking to be believed."

"They are not the same?"

"One is a rope. The other is a bridge." She heard how it sounded and shrugged. "Chathu used to have us practise in the long room. Girls who were sent into a rich house had to be taken for what they said they were. I was good at it."

"Of course you were." He said it plainly, the way he said everything, and there was no knife in it. "Again?"

They did it four times. She asked, and he answered in order, and each time something fell off the end of his answer. *In concert* went on the second. *Creature* went on the third, and she did not tell him why she was glad. By the fourth he stood with his hands loose at his sides, the wax and the tally and the date lined up in his mouth like men on parade, and no more.

"Raiders don't take notes," he said, near the end, to the empty bench.

"What?"

"I was thinking that I would rather face raiders."

"I'd rather you did, too. You'd cheat less."

His laugh was a short dry thing, mostly breath, and it startled him as much as it startled her. He rubbed his jaw. In the lamplight the tiredness stood out on him like bone.

"You do this well," he said.

"It's the only thing I do well that I'm not ashamed of."

He didn't answer that. He took the stool from the floor and set it back at the head of the bench, carefully, like a man putting away a borrowed tool. Then, without turning, he said, "May I ask you one?"

"Ask."

"I'm not spending a pass on it." He faced her. "If the court asks you what you are. Not your name. Not your house. What you are. What will you say?"

The brazier ticked. Somewhere above them the cistern chain clanked the hour, slow, iron on iron.

She opened her mouth. She had a cover, and it was good. She had true sentences with room around them, a ward of Kunjan Chirayil, a girl raised among girls, a wife who learned names. All of them were walls. He knew it. He had asked for the thing on the other side.

*A leopard*, said the part of her that had been twenty-one years a number. She could not say it. It was the one word in the world she could not give away, in any room, to any ear.

*An odiyan*, said another, and her skin went cold. That was a rope for her own neck.

*Seventeen*, said a third, and that was the only answer she had ever been taught.

"Ask me something else," she said.

"No," said Devan, very gently. "I'll wait."

"Then that is my pass." Her voice came out thinner than she meant. "One refusal each, and you said you'd let it lie. I'm spending mine."

"It lies," he said. "I didn't say I'd stop wondering."

She stood in the lamp's small ring, with a pot of bark salve on the bench beside her, and understood that the justice had set the oath for the morning after next, and that she did not know what she would swear to.

# Chapter 4: Ask Your Captain

For the first time since the gate, no green sash stood at Ittan's door.

Devan saw it from the far end of the passage. Rema had called her escort to the hall to carry the tally chest. Bhaskaran's men held the guardroom alone, and the man holding it was Unni, thin as a spear shaft, with his mother's salt-fish smell still in his jerkin.

"Sir. The justice said two of hers at the door at all times."

"The justice is counting wax," Devan said. "Shut this behind me. Knock when the chain strikes."

Unni looked at the iron cistern chain through the passage slit, then back at him. He was not much past twenty and he had a good face for being afraid in. "Yes, sir."

The guardroom smelled of wet wool and lamp oil. Ittan sat on the cot with his writing case on his knees and his hands folded on the lid, like a man waiting for a ferry. He had been a round man. Since the sealing he had lost something from the cheeks.

"My friend," he said. "You look worse than I do."

"I haven't been given a cot."

"No. They only give those to traitors." The smile came and went. "You want something. You have your sister's face on. It is a very good face, it has never once learned to hide."

Devan took the stool by the door and kept the lamp between them.

"Rema sits assize for you. You will be heard as witness. If you swear before her that the hand that sold the passes also bought Asha's death, she has to carry you out as a witness, not a traitor. I need you sworn. I need names."

"Do you?"

"A witness gets a hearing in Kodiyur. A traitor gets a rope on the road."

Ittan looked at his hands. "Do you know what an oath is, Warden? It is a man choosing which person to be afraid of. I am a long way past choosing." He lifted his eyes. The lamp stood in them, bright and full, and nothing fell. "I will swear to nothing. Not the day of the week. I ask only one thing."

"Ask."

"My wife has a cough. It's bad in the damp, and this is the damp season." The voice was still genial. The words came slow, one stone at a time. "There is a brown jar in my case. The physicians make it, honey and root, the sweet kind, to take after the willow bark. She won't take the bark without it. If you would send it on with the next rider, and not forget that she likes it warmed."

Devan had expected a bargain. A name for a name, or a door left unlocked. He had braced for that all morning.

"I'll send it," he said.

"You will. You're a man who does." Ittan shut his eyes. "That is why they—" He stopped himself.

"Give me the names of her physicians," Devan said. "I'll write to them myself, in my own hand, with my seal. Tell them where she's to be looked after, what dose, who pays. The bill will be Kettamala's. She'll not be left."

He had not planned to say it. It came out as plain as a ration count, and he saw it land.

Ittan's mouth worked. His fingers closed on the case so hard the knuckles went the colour of tallow. For a moment the whole round, easy face was gone, and what looked out was a frightened man of forty-nine who had a wife.

"You would," he whispered. "Salt and pepper, you would. That is the trouble with you."

"Ittan."

"Ask your captain." The words fell out low and fast. "Ask your captain what he brought me."

Devan did not breathe. "When? The morning after the vow?"

Ittan opened his mouth. Then he closed it, and Devan watched him fold himself away. The hands settled on the lid. The eyes went to the lamp and stayed there. He sat four feet away, and he was not in the room.

"Ittan."

Nothing. A drip in the corner. The chain struck the hour, iron on iron, and Unni's knuckles rapped twice on the door.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Muthu was at the padippura, laughing.

He stood with Bhaskaran under the gate roof, arguing the price of a bad axle with a pepper carter who had walked up in the rain to complain. He had one arm round the carter's shoulders. The carter, who had come to shout, was grinning. That was Muthu's gift. People gave him their anger and walked away holding something warmer.

Devan stood in the arch until the carter went. Then he said, "Ittan says you brought him something."

"Brought him?" The laugh came up, huge and rolling, the one that turned heads in the lower market. "Warden, I brought him the fort's whole patience. Two meals a day and a blanket, and a lecture on the price of cloves from Bhaskaran, who'll—"

He stopped.

It was not a pause. A pause had breath in it. This was the stillness of a man set down in a room with no air, the big hands hanging, the face gone smooth. Devan had known that stillness since they were boys in the red earth, when Muthu had sworn he hadn't touched the sweets.

"That's all," Muthu said quietly. "Ask Bhaskaran. It's on the roll."

Bhaskaran, who had been studying the rain, said nothing. He had the sense not to.

Devan could have spoken then. He could have said *the packet*, *the grey wax*, *what you carried into that room at first light*. He weighed it, with the justice's escort a hundred paces off and the hearing a day away.

If he named it now, he lost Muthu. A man named stops being a captain. He becomes a prisoner, or a stranger, and either way he leaves the gate. And Devan needed sixty soldiers who believed their warden trusted every one of them. He needed the padippura held by a man the garrison loved, with Rema's eyes on it, until the hearing had been heard.

A man you suspect is safest on the wall, where you can see his hands.

"Hold the gate through the hearing," he said. "Nothing in or out without your mark."

Muthu's face came back, in pieces, the grin first. "As if I'd be anywhere else."

"No," Devan said. "You wouldn't."

He walked back through the rain with his shoulders square. Behind him Muthu's laugh started up again, loud and warm, and Devan thought it the worst sound he had heard in years.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"He told you to ask your captain," said Neela. "In those words."

"In those words. Then he shut like a door."

The lamp was low. They had not lit the second. The shutters were closed, the cotton curtain behind them hung still, and Devan sat on the edge of the bed with his sword across his knees because he had nothing else to do with his hands. Neela stood by the window with her arms folded. She had not slept. It showed in the way she held her left hand away from her body, the three bandaged fingers curled.

She had taken his report in order, as she'd asked in the morning. He had told her about Ittan before she could ask. It was the first thing out of his mouth in the door.

"Muthu is the guild's man," she said.

"Muthu is the queen's."

"You've decided."

"I've watched him write grey-wax letters for six years. They go with the palace rider." He turned the sword. "The queen's household. Rema looked at him in the hall and he went quiet for a breath. He did not do that for anyone else. He knows what she is."

"He goes quiet when he lies. You told me. He'd go quiet in front of any justice who looked at him straight." She came away from the window. "Think of the cart. I gave Muthu an order out of your hearing, and by that dusk Chathu knew to have Vellan at his gate. Who else knew?"

"You were followed."

"On a grey mare, a night and a day ahead of any hunter? No. Someone told them." Her eyes went to the floor. "A house pays its debts in news. Chathu said it to me with his sweets in front of me. And Ittan bought my contract through that house. A man who brings Ittan things is a man who answers to Ittan's creditor."

"Or the packet came from the palace and Ittan was its last stop."

"Then why would Ittan point at him?"

"To spite the one who sent it." Devan heard it as he said it, and did not like it. "A man who's lost everything tells you who to hate. It does not have to be true."

"No." She sat on the sill and the wood creaked. "Nor does yours." She looked at him. "The packet is in the hall strongbox. One cut under the wax and we'd know whose it is. Why don't we break it?"

"Because Muthu would know he is read." He had turned that over on the stair. "The day before the hearing. He'd see the seal was lifted, and he'd know by whom, and the gate goes with him. Sixty men watch the man who holds it. Whichever of us is right, I'd lose the only place I can see his hands."

"So the wax stays whole."

"The wax stays whole. We argue it out the long way."

"Then it's a choice, not a proof." She nodded slowly. "I'll take that."

"He held the gatehouse arch for me at the Resin House," Devan said. "He took spears for that."

"A man can hold a gate for you and write to someone who wants you dead. I did both for a month." The words were quiet. "He may love you. Most people who sell you do."

He had no answer for that. He looked at his hands. He had known Muthu since he was nine and Muthu twelve. He knew how the man took his tea, and which knee ached in the rains, and how he had sat up three nights after Asha with a lantern, saying nothing, drinking Janu's bitter tonic without complaint. If Muthu was lying, it was a thing built so long and so well that Devan would have to unbuild a good part of himself to see it.

"You think I'm blind," he said.

"I think you're a man who has kept one friend for more than twenty years and doesn't mean to lose him in a night." She did not smile. "I'm not arguing so I'm right. If I'm wrong, I wish to be. Muthu laughs at my jokes."

"Everyone laughs at your jokes."

"Not Rema's guards."

It almost made him smile. It did not reach his mouth.

"We have one night of it," he said. "Tomorrow, the hearing. Rema wants the tallies, the satchel, the chest, Ittan. And you sworn. What else?" He did not need to say *what else do I have that she does not.* Behind the third slat, in the hollow of the shutter frame, lay an oilcloth bundle tied with red thread. He had not looked at it in three days. He knew its weight exactly.

Neela turned her head toward the slat, and then did not.

"The payment writ," she said.

"Yes."

"Seven weeks before the wedding. The queen's green wax, with the chip."

"Yes."

"If you hand it to Rema, she reads it."

"She is a justice of the high court. It's her office to read it. The charter says a warden shall lay all evidence before a justice sitting assize." He kept his voice flat, because flat was the only way he could say it. "It's the law I've held for five years."

"And she serves the queen."

"She does."

"Then what does she do," Neela said slowly, "with a writ that proves her mistress paid for your death?"

He had asked himself that on the stair, in the passage, in the rain at the gate. He had not liked any of the answers, so he had gone on asking.

"She reads it," he said. "She goes the colour of ash. She says it's a forgery. Or she says it's real and she's grateful, and she carries it to Kodiyur in the sleeve of her coat, with Ittan in front of her on a rope." He let the sword lie. "A justice of the queen's court can try Ittan. She can try me. She cannot try the queen. There's no bench in Varanad above the queen's bench, and if there were, who would sit it?"

"So the only proof you have goes to the one person it can hurt."

"And never comes back." He stopped. "Neela, if I withhold it, I break the charter. A warden who sits on evidence at assize is a warden the queen can strip, with the law on her side. I have never broken it. Not once, in five years. It's the only wall I've built that I'm proud of."

She was quiet for a time. She did not tell him it didn't matter. He was grateful for that.

"I'm sworn at the hearing," she said at last. "As household witness. She asks me what I know. I tell her true things." Her mouth moved. "I've told you what I'll say to a justice. A true sentence with room around it. But the room is yours, Devan. If she asks me what's behind the slat, I won't know what you want me to say."

He had been about to say *I'll decide it*. He heard himself, and did not say it.

"Tell me what you want," he said. "Not what you'd bear. What you'd do."

"I'd keep it." No hesitation. "I'd keep it behind the slat until we've found a bench above hers. I don't know where one is. I'd keep looking."

"It's my neck."

"It's my name on the closing writ. If you break the charter, I'm the witness who stood beside you. I'll share that." She put one hand over her bandaged fingers. "We could be wrong. She could be better than I think. I've known three justices, and none of them was good."

"And if she asks me whether I hold such a writ?"

"Then you'll say what you say."

"I'll say I won't answer."

"She'll know there's something."

"She will know there's something," he agreed. "That's different from knowing what." He looked at the shutter, at the third slat with its small, complaining creak that he had never fixed. "I won't lie to her. I won't lie to you. I can refuse."

"Then refuse." Her voice was dry. "I'll be beside you, being a wife."

He set the sword on the floor. He stood, and she stood, and for a moment neither of them touched the other. It was not the kind of moment that wanted touching. They were two people who had weighed a thing and put it in the same pan.

"Together," he said.

"Together." She held out her good hand, and he took it as one takes the hand of a man who has agreed to a contract, quickly and hard. Her palm was cold. Then, because there was no one to see it, she did not let go for a breath.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Neither of them slept. In the last dark hour the lamp burned to a thread, the cistern chain struck the turn of the watch, and the thin rain seemed to hold its breath against the shutters.

Neela lay on her side with her eyes open. He lay on his back. She had told him once that she counted exits, steps and breaths when she was afraid. He listened, and could hear it, the small dry sound of a lip moving on a number.

He was thinking of a brown jar, and a woman he had never met, who liked her medicine warmed.

The knock was soft. Two knuckles, then a third, then nothing, as though the hand behind it was ashamed.

Devan was off the bed with his sword before he knew he had risen. Neela was beside him with her feet bare. He opened the door a hand's width.

Unni stood in the corridor with no lamp. His jerkin was wet to the collar. He had run the whole of the west stair, and he was breathing in short pieces, like a boy who has been told to carry bad water and not spill.

"Sir." His voice cracked. "The justice. She's moved it."

"Moved what?"

"The execution. Ittan." Unni swallowed. "She has found a clause in the wartime charter, sir. A prisoner whose keeping endangers the pass. Dawn. This dawn. She says the hearing can sit without him." He licked his lips. "She says she'll hold it all the same, for the record."

Behind Devan, very quietly, Neela let out a breath.

"The escort," Devan said.

"They're already in the court. All four, in green sashes. First Guard has a block." Unni's eyes were enormous. "She had Bhaskaran's night men told to stand down, sir. Sixty of us, and they walked past us to the guardroom and nobody stopped them. They said it was the queen's sash."

He hesitated, and then it came out of him all at once. "Do we stop them, sir? Bhaskaran has the lads half up. They're only waiting on you."

Devan did not answer.

He saw it whole. One word, and sixty spears would go down the west stair and take the court from four people. And by noon every man who followed him would be a mutineer against the queen's own sash, with a rope in Kodiyur waiting for each. Neela's eyes were on his face. She did not tell him what to say.

Unni waited. The rain ticked on the shutters.

Outside, over the wet roofs, the sky had not yet begun to grey.

# Chapter 5: Dawn Writ

Devan walked out into the rain without his cloak and stopped ten paces from the block.

Neela stopped a pace behind his left shoulder, where a wife stands when she has been told to be one. The court was red mud and standing water under a sky the colour of a pot lid. Someone had brought the chopping stump from the wood yard and scrubbed it pale. The four green sashes had gone dark with wet. First Guard stood beside the stump with her hands folded, as if she waited for a ferry.

Sixty men of the garrison lined the walls and the gallery with their spears grounded. Not one of them spoke.

Justice Rema stood under the hall roof, where the water ran off the thatch in a bright fringe. She held an oilcloth case against her coat.

"Justice," Devan said. He did not raise his voice, and it carried to the gate anyway. "I enter an objection under the assize charter, before the garrison, and I ask that it be written."

"Say it, Warden."

"A witness named to a sitting is held for the sitting. You granted an assize. You named Councillor Ittan to it. You cannot put him to the sword at dawn and sit at noon on his evidence."

"Your reading of the charter is correct." Rema said it without heat. Water dripped from her hood onto the case. "The queen's warrant outranks the hearing. The sitting will hear what it hears, and the man has no need to be there to be heard of. Your objection is entered. I shall write it myself."

Neela watched the wall, because she could not watch Devan's back. Bhaskaran had a hand shut on his spear until the knuckles stood out. Unni's mouth was open. Along the gallery men glanced at one another, then at Devan, and each glance held a question every one of them already knew the answer to. Sixty spears. Four people. Muthu stood under the gate arch with his big hands hanging, and he was not laughing.

"I have sixty men on these walls," Devan said.

The rain made the only sound.

"I could stop this before the stroke. Any man here knows it, and so do you, Justice. I won't. A man who stands against a sealed warrant is a traitor in Kodiyur, and I will not make traitors of sixty for one who would not swear to the day of the week." He drew a breath. "Let it be written that I could."

Rema looked at him for a long moment. Something crossed her face that might have been regret, and might have been a thing she had trained out of herself years ago.

"It will be written," she said.

Neela's throat hurt. He had stood behind that lock for eight years, and now he had stood in the rain and called it by name so that no man on the wall could mistake it for fear. It looked like strength. She thought it was probably both, and that he did not yet know the two could be one thing.

They brought Ittan out of the guardroom between two of Bhaskaran's men, who let go of him at the edge of the stone as if they were returning a borrowed coat. He had no writing case. Without it his hands did not know what to do. He looked at the sky, then at Devan, and found a smile somewhere.

"My friend. The jar?"

"It goes down with the first runner," Devan said. "Warmed, as you said."

"Good. Good." Ittan nodded as if a price had been settled. His eyes went past Devan and found nothing in particular. "Tell her to keep the window open in rain. She likes to hear it."

Nobody answered. There was nothing to answer. Second and Third took his arms, gently. He went with them, still nodding.

Neela turned, walked to the wall stair by the gate, and climbed it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The west rampart ran above the warden's window, where the sentry used to stand. Here the wall bent toward the padippura, and the whole court lay below her like a dish. She leaned her forearms on the wet stone.

She had seen men die. She had learned young that you do not watch the block, because the block tells you nothing. You watch the hands. The hands are where the order lives.

The hands of the escort did not wait for an order.

Second and Third set Ittan on his knees at the stump. They let go of his arms in the same instant, though no one counted. First Guard drew her sword in a single motion and held it in both hands. Fourth stood behind her with his hands open at his sides, and the rain ran off his fingers. Nobody spoke. Nobody looked at anybody else. When the stroke came, Second and Third had already stepped back, and Neela could not have said who had moved first.

She had seen hands move like that once. Twenty girls at the long table in the Resin House, all reaching for the bowl at the first note of Chathu's bell.

Afterwards Fourth's hands began to shake, and he put them behind him. First Guard wiped the blade on a cloth, folded the cloth, and put it away. Then her left hand went to her left cuff and closed on it, and stayed there.

Neela did not move. The wind had swung round off the hills, from the south range across the court and up the wall, and it brought her the rain and the mud. Under them came something else, thin, scorched and bitter.

Tamarind burnt on a stone. Pine sap, and old.

It was not salve. It was a jar opened long ago and shut again, a smell worn into leather and cloth and hair. She breathed through her mouth and still it came. Down in the court a hound began to whine, high through the nose. Then another. The whole loose pack drew back toward the kitchen door in a body, heads low, and sat.

Three times now. The salve on Eravi's thumb had been the first. She had told herself many barks were bitter, and she had counted her nails to prove it. There was no counting this. It came off all four of them, and it had been on them a long time.

Chathu's hunters carried resin in oiled leather for one purpose. A rogue taken in the day must be turned by dusk, whether she would or no. These four had no hooked poles and no nets. But poles and nets were things you left at the foot of a mountain. They did not come up the road in a queen's sash.

Her name was on the closing writ. She had put it there herself, of her own will, in clean ink, as the warden's wife. Any clerk in Kodiyur could lay it beside a list of unvouched odiyans.

*They are here for me*, she thought, and stood very still with her ruined nails pressed into the wet stone, and watched them carry the body out.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The pit was a place she had been in once before, at dawn, with her fingers on a man's neck. She came down the north steps in the afternoon with the rain thinned to a drizzle, and the sesame-oil lamps burning in their niches round the rim.

Devan could not stop moving.

He went from one form to the next without a gap between, and each was faster than the last. She had seen him do it once at dawn, quick and deadly, but that was practice. This was a man trying to run out of the end of himself. Red earth flew up from his heels.

A stinging line opened across the back of her right knuckle, thin and bright. He had scraped the pit wall on a turn. The welt rose on her skin and then paled, no wider than a thread.

She stepped down onto the earth. It was cold and wet and it came up through her toes.

"Don't," he said, without turning. "If you've come to tell me I did the right thing."

"I wasn't going to."

"Or that it was not mine to carry."

"That either."

He stopped. His chest went in and out. There was a smear of blood on the heel of his right hand and he looked at it as if it belonged to someone else. "Then what?"

"A round."

He stared at her.

"Janu told me in the first week. Eight years. Muthu asks every seventh day and you say no." She kept her voice level. "I can't comfort you, Devan. I haven't the trade for it. You would thank me and carry on being alone with it. You can't stop moving, and I would rather you moved at someone."

"I would hurt you."

"You'd mean not to. That's the usual way."

"Your hand."

"The left. I'll keep it behind me." She tucked it into the small of her back, three bound nails and all. "Two points. Light touch, no marma. You are not to pull so hard you insult me."

He looked at her for a long time. Then he went to the rim, picked up the oil lamp that stood nearest her, and set it on the far side of the pit. It was a small thing. It took the heat off her bad hand.

"Two points," he said.

The first bout was nothing. He was careful, and she hated it. He circled to keep her on his right, where her bandaged fingers lay away from him, and he never once swung at her left side. She gave ground and read his feet. She tapped the air a hand's width from his collarbone, and he stepped out of it and did not strike back.

"Who were they?" he said, circling. "Your guess."

"Hunters. Guild hunters, carrying resin."

"They carried no poles."

"Hunters leave the poles at the foot of the pass. The ones who come in leave their faces." She slipped a cut at her ribs. "They hadn't a face between them."

"The queen has a company that goes where she wants no questions asked. A standing company, sashed, trained to do the thing and say nothing." He parried her wrist. "They'd look hollow. That is how you train a man to obey."

"First Guard touched her cuff when she saw my binding."

"She has a lamp-burn on that wrist. I saw it in the hall."

"She touched it when she saw mine," Neela said. "She touched it again after Ittan."

He did not answer. He was thinking, and she took the opening. It was a clean one, the left side of his guard. She had told him it dropped there once, at the end of a long night, with her fingers at the back of his neck. It had been mended. It had mended in a week.

It was open again now.

She went in under it and laid two fingers against his left ribs, light as a leaf, and stepped out.

"One," she said.

He looked down at the place she had touched. Then up. "You saw it open."

"You opened it."

"Yes."

"That one you gave me."

"Yes." He did not say it like an apology. "You'd have found it by the third bout. I saved you the trouble."

"Don't." Her voice came out rougher than she meant. "Do it again and mean the rest. I don't want one he gave me."

"Then you'll have to earn the next one."

"I will."

She did. It took longer, and it cost her a bruise on the shoulder that rose as a faint welt on his. He came at her in earnest at the end, fast and without waste, and for three breaths she saw what the dawn drills were for. She saw too what he had spent eight years refusing to do. Then she dropped under a sweep, rolled across the red mud, came up inside his reach, and tapped the notch under his ear with one finger.

She let him see her pleased. She did not hide it. It spread across her face before she could stop it, a plain stupid grin, the first she had worn since the wall.

Devan stood in the middle of the pit and looked at it like a man reading a map he had been missing a sheet of. Mud ran down his face. Slowly, from the corner out, his mouth gave way.

"Two," he said.

"Two."

"Muthu will want it told."

"Muthu can ask." She wiped her palm on her skirt and her voice did not shake. "Same time tomorrow?"

"Yes," said Devan.

He said nothing else. He picked up the oil lamp from the far side of the pit, carried it back to its niche beside her, and set it down where it had been.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At dusk the escort walked out.

Rema had shut herself in the south range with the tally chest from noon and sent word that the sitting would open at lamp-lighting, once her people were away. The pass was closed to carts, but a walker with a good pair of boots could take the goat path left of the split boulder. Neela had never said how she knew it. She stood in the shadow of the padippura post and watched the four go by in file, packs high on their shoulders.

Muthu sat at the gate table with the roll open and his pen in his hand. He wrote four marks and did not laugh. He did not look up either. Neela marked it and kept it.

The rain had thickened again. The light was going out of the court.

"Walk safe," she said, because she said it to everyone.

Second and Third went past her as though she were a post. Fourth glanced at her and away. First Guard, last in line, slowed.

Neela had her bad hand on the wet gatepost. She had forgotten it. The bandage had come loose on the middle finger, and the nail showed under it, split to the quick and brown at the edge.

First Guard's eyes rested on it. They were the colour of nothing at all. She did not stop walking, and she did not seem to know she was saying it.

"It passes."

Then she was through the arch and into the dusk, and the rain took her.

Neela stayed where she was until the last green sash went out of sight over the first bend. Beside her Muthu capped his ink. He had not heard, or he had chosen to be a man who hadn't.

*It passes.* Two words. Nobody knew about the nails except Eravi, Devan and herself. She had split them in her sleep, and they would split again, and the only people in Varanad who could say those words and mean them were people who had waited out the ache.

She went in out of the rain, and did not look at the sky.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Rema had taken the warden's chair again. The lamps were lit down the long hall and Janu had put out a plate of boiled eggs that nobody touched. The chest of tallies stood at the justice's left hand, with the lid up.

Devan stood before the bench with his hands loose at his sides. Neela came to his shoulder. She counted the pillars, eight, and the doors, three, and found she did not need to.

"I have buried a man this day on my own warrant," said Rema. "I will not do it twice without cause." She rubbed her lower back with one hand. "The pass is shut. I mean to sit until it opens. I shall hear the warden's case, in its order, when the tallies are counted. And any other the crown has sealed."

She reached into her coat and took out a second case.

It was leather, oiled, the size of the one that had hung from her hip at dawn. The first had held the warrant. She set the second on the table next to the chest and laid her hand flat on its lid. The wax at the knot was dark and the lamp was behind it, and Neela could not read the seal. The justice's thumb rested on it, lightly, as on a pulse.

Rema had carried it up a mountain in the rain. She had stood in the court at dawn with Ittan on his knees and had not opened it.

"Lady Neela," said Rema pleasantly. "You shall be sworn when I call you."

Beside her Devan said nothing. His hand moved the width of a finger, and the back of it touched hers.

Neela did not move her hand away.

# Chapter 6: The Ledger He Needs

"Bring me the guild's paper," said Rema, "and leave your grief at the door."

She said it to Devan, across the tally chest, with the lamps burning down the length of the hall. Neela stood at his shoulder and counted the eight pillars again, because her hands wanted something to do.

The sitting had run two hours. Rema had counted wax, weighed the satchel, and made Bhaskaran swear to the cart that passed on a council tally at the new moon before the wedding. She had been exact and dry and fair, and Neela had hated every minute of it. A fair justice is the hardest kind to lie to.

"You have a dead man's tally book," Rema went on. "It shows payments to a house in the Blackbark Wood and a mark I'm told means *delivered*. You have a slip in resin-ink that your wife says came off a courier. A slip is a slip. A sister's drawn parrot is a story." She did not look at Neela when she said *wife*. "But a house that sells deaths has to bill for them. Someone writes down who paid, who died and who was sent. Bring me that. Ink I can swear to, laid on a bench where a bench can see it."

"The guild keeps its contracts in one book," Devan said.

"Then it keeps them where I can reach them, or I have heard a great deal of grief for nothing." Rema folded her hands. "I've seen yours, Warden. It doesn't weigh anything in court. The paper might."

She dismissed them with a nod. The case with the second seal stayed unopened under her palm.

Neela walked out beside him and said nothing until the hall door shut. Then she said, "Contract books."

"Yes."

"She thinks they're only that."

"What else would she think?" He looked at her sideways. "What else are they?"

Neela did not answer. She was already counting the ways it could go wrong.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Devan wrote the letter at the table in the warden's chamber, by the one lamp, with the shutters barred behind him.

She had expected a short one. A man who answers questions with exact facts does not write long. But he had been at it a quarter of an hour, and the pen kept stopping. The brown jar stood by his elbow. It had come out of Ittan's writing case that afternoon with the lid still sticky. Kesu's grandson would carry it down the goat path at first light, wrapped in wet cloth against the cold.

Neela sat on the bed with her bandaged hand in her lap and watched him.

"You can read it," he said, without turning.

"I'd rather not."

"Then watch the wax instead."

She watched. He wrote to the household of the Keeper of the Passes, in Kodiyur. She caught that much over his shoulder, and a line near the foot: *He asked that you keep the window open in rain. I have promised it will be remembered.* He did not soften the first line, which said her husband had died at dawn by the queen's warrant. He did not blame anyone for it. At the end he wrote the words *the account of Kettamala*, and signed.

The wax was plain black, not the warden's red. He pressed the iron disc into it, a hill and a gate, and held it a breath longer than it needed.

"He bought my death," Devan said. "He bought hers." He did not say whose. "And he loved his wife. All of it was in the same case."

"You said that as if it were a sum."

"It is." He set down the disc. "I don't know how to carry the other thing, so I'm doing the arithmetic."

Neela thought about that. She thought about Chathu, who wept when members died and sold their pages afterward, and how she had spent twenty years unable to see that this was one man and not two. She doubted she could do what Devan had just done, with a pen, for Ittan's widow. She was not sure she would try.

"What if she hates you for it?" she said. "The letter."

"Then she'll have a jar and a roof, and she can hate me with a cough medicine in her hand." He turned the paper to dry. "Ask me the real question, Neela."

"I don't have one."

"You always have one. You ask the small ones so you don't have to answer mine."

It stung because it was true. She let it sting.

"What do you want from the book?" she said. "Not what Rema wants. You."

He took his time. The chain struck the hour, iron on iron, from somewhere below.

"I want the ledger in front of the high court before the monsoon breaks," he said. "Every contract in it. The Asha leaf, the pass-seller's leaves, the dates, the hands. Rema is a start. If she carries the paper to Kodiyur, there's a bench that has to look. I've spent six years being told I had nothing. I want to be the man who laid it down."

"And the rest of the book?"

"What rest?"

She had known it would come to this, and still her tongue felt thick. "The ledger isn't contracts, Devan. It's two kinds of leaf sewn into one cover. The front half is ink. Ordinary ink, no magic in it, in Resin House cipher. Who paid, who was sent. You couldn't read a line." She laid her left hand flat on her knee, the three bound nails curled. "The back is pages. Mine was in it. Every odiyan's is. Resin-ink, one true witness's hand, and whoever holds a page and reads it aloud at night has the power of the one who wrote it. It passes with the page."

"I know what a page is."

"You know it as a rule. I know it as a kitchen drawer I was kept in." She breathed. "If you give the court the book, you give it all of it. The court becomes the holder. It holds forty lives, and forty more in the kennels who can't be helped by anything now. And a court that holds a page becomes a master, whatever it swears. It doesn't have to want to. It only has to keep the book."

"The court isn't Chathu."

"No. It's better at it. It has a seal and a law to say why."

He was silent. She watched his jaw work, not in anger. He was looking for the hole in what she'd said, and he was a fair man, so he looked hard and did not find one.

"Then give me the other side," he said. "What happens if I don't take it to the court? If you hold it, or I hold it, and no bench ever sees a leaf of it? Asha stays a thing I believe. Ittan is dead with his mouth shut. Whoever bought her death keeps the name." His voice stayed level. "It's my sister, Neela. I want the name of the hand set down in ink. I want to hear it read out. Do you know how long I have waited to be told one true thing about that night?"

"I know."

"Then you know what you're asking me to set down."

She did. It was the worst of it. He needed the book and she feared what the book would become, and the space between them had a person's shape.

"I'm not asking you to set it down," she said. "I'm asking you not to hand the whole thing over for the sake of the half you need."

"That's what the court is for."

"That's what the court *says* it's for." She stood. Her legs were not steady. The nails, she thought, or the hollow place under her ribs that was past her last proper resin and getting worse. "And you can't read the front half either. Not a leaf. I'll have to read every contract aloud to you, in my own voice, and you'll hear what I was in each one. Ask me the next question. A question for a question. You've earned it."

"What's on yours?"

She had not expected that. "My page?"
