"The one that burned. The witness entry went. You told me something stayed."

"A slip." The word came out low. "Sewn behind it. *Intake, sold at six years.* Two lines I couldn't read in the mud. Chathu put his thumb on it and took it." She heard her own voice and did not like it. "My name's on it, or my village. I think. I don't know what I'd do with it if I had it. I'd read it alone, maybe, with the door shut. That's the whole of what I want from that book, Devan, and it's small, and I would not trade it for a bench in Kodiyur."

He had gone very still. Then he rose from the table and stood in front of her and did not touch her.

"I did not know it was that small," he said.

"It's the smallest thing I own. It's why I'm frightened."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They settled it standing up, which was how both of them settled things.

"Say it as a bargain," Devan said. "I'll not agree to what I don't understand."

"The contract leaves come loose at the stitching. They're ordinary. They can't cage anyone. You take them to Rema, to the high court, to anyone who'll hear them." She counted on her fingers, as she did. "The pages go back to the people they belong to. By daylight, read aloud in full, to her face."

"By the holder."

"By whoever holds the book. I won't give that part away to the queen."

"Everyone in the room hears the night-shape."

"Everyone in the room becomes her witness." She did not flinch from it. "That's the price of handing back a page. Your own court clerk, if you bring one. Your captain, if you bring him."

He took it in. She watched him do it. "Then there are no clerks in the room. Only the holder and the one whose page it is, and whoever she chooses."

"That's a better rule than mine." It cost her something to say. "Add it."

"And the rest of the book? Anything neither of us thought of?"

"I'd burn it. I'd burn all of the back half, one at a time, by the owner's hand. But some of them have been rogue in the eyes of the crown since before I was born." She spread the good hand. "I can't promise that either. Not yet."

"Contract books to the court," Devan said. "Pages to their owners."

"A first term."

"A first term." He held out his hand and she took it, as she had taken it in the rain, quick and hard. Then he said, in the flat way he had of saying things that cost him, "I don't think you're right about the court."

"I don't think you're right about the dead."

"Then neither of us is wrong." He let go. "I'd rather that than a silence."

It was the oddest compliment she had ever been paid. She took it with both hands.

"There may be more in the contract leaves than you want," she said, careful with it. She thought of a hill fort she had seen blindfolded, and of nothing she could say. "Whatever's written, I'll read it to you the way it's written. It'll be written true."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"No." He looked at the shutter, at the third slat that creaked. "But I won't shut the book because it might hold a thing I can't bear. That's how I lived for six years. I'd like to try the other way."

She said nothing. She could not. The only answer she had was a stair she could not place, a woman she never saw, and a number.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At dusk the next day he went to close the shutters, and she stopped him.

"Not tonight."

He turned with his hand on the bar. There was a river pebble in his cheek. She had not noticed it until the words came out round it. He took it out and held it in his palm, and looked as if he had been caught praying.

"You're not—"

"I'm not." She sat on the window ledge with her back to the dark, where the dusk burn had begun faintly in the scars at her throat, and did not turn from it. "There's one smear of Ammu's resin left in the pouch. I spent the other the night of the execution, on the ridge, to see who walked the road. I'm keeping this one."

"For what?"

"For inside the compound." She pulled her bangle higher on her wrist and let the scars show, pale and ridged. "I can't spend it on the road to the groves. And I can't have the ache take me before then. It's coming. My nails split on the stair this morning, and the night the justice came I woke in a sweat that wasn't the rain." She did not say how near. She had counted. "A moon with no proper resin, and I'll be on a floor."

Devan set the pebble on the sill, beside the other one.

"Then what do we do?"

"We?"

"I asked it as a question."

She nearly smiled. "We do the oldest thing a rogue odiyan does." She looked past him at the shutters, and the dark beyond them, and the wood a day's hard ride off, in the rain. "I have to rob Chathu's groves before I can rob Chathu."

He did not ask if she was sure. He said, "Rema will call you to swear."

"I know."

"She's sat on that second case since the road. She'll open it when she's counted the tallies, and you're named in the closing writ whether you stand there or not." He said it flatly. "If you're gone when she calls, she'll read it as flight."

"Then she'll read it." Neela made herself hold his eyes. "And I'll have missed whatever's in the case. If it names me, I'll hear it second-hand, from you, with no chance to answer. That's the cost. I've counted it."

"And the alternative?"

"Sit in this room while the ache takes my hands, and then I can't swear to anything, or turn, or hold a knife." She held up her wrapped hand. "Resin is the only thing I own that the guild doesn't. I'd have to steal it anyway. Better now, while I can still climb a wall."

"We ride tonight," he said.

"At full dark. The wood is a day's hard ride, so we reach the trees by noon. Nightbark can be tapped at noon, with a knife and a flask. It only burns like fire if you wear it on bare skin. And the book I came to steal is in the same compound as the trees."

"They'll have walls on the groves."

"They'll have hunters." She heard how it sounded. "I know the ridges. I know a drain that isn't a path. I'll go over as a woman and keep the smear for the night."

"And I'll ride with you."

"You have a fort to hold. You have a justice in your chair."

"I have a captain for the fort." He said it without smiling. "And Rema asked for the guild's paper. Muthu can tell her at first light that my wife and I have gone to fetch it. She can hardly call that grief." He paused. "I have a wife who is going to cut a tree the guild owns, and a knife wound of hers will open on me wherever I am. I'd rather be near enough to hand her a cloth."

She looked at him. He looked back, steadily, as a man does who has made a plain statement of fact.

"You're a nuisance," she said.

"Yes."

"I'll need you to stay behind a tree."

"I'll try."

She turned her face to the dusk. The burn in her throat eased, as it did when she did not take the resin. A breath, and it was gone, and she was only a woman on a ledge with the dark going blue behind her.

She took the flask from the sill, the one she had carried out of Kodiyur and drained weeks ago, and thumbed out the cork. It was dry inside, and it smelled of almost nothing at all.

"Then we'd better fill it," she said, and held it out into the last of the light.

# Chapter 7: Blackbark in the Rain

The knife slipped on the third cut and went into her palm instead of the bark.

Neela did not swear. She counted. One for the sting, two for the heat, three for the bright line that welled across the heel of her right hand and ran down to the wrist. The tree went on dripping black into the flask below it, unbothered.

Thirty paces back, behind the dead stump where she had told him to wait, someone sat down hard in the wet fern.

They had ridden since full dark. The wood road ran under blackbark and wild jackfruit, red mud to the horses' fetlocks, and the rain came in sheets and stopped as if someone had shut a door on it. By the time grey light showed through the canopy she had stopped feeling her feet. By noon they had left the horses tied in a ravine and climbed the ridge above the Resin House groves.

This tree was a stray. The wind had seeded it on the high ground, outside Chathu's fenced rows and his count. Its trunk was black and plated like dried mud. Nightbark can be tapped at noon, and she had known that since she was nine. A tapper's wife had taught her on a slope like this one, closing her hands over Neela's and cutting for her, singing while she did it.

She was humming the tune now without noticing. The words had never come back to her whole, only the first verses and the last, which Ammu had sung her once.

The knife had gone because of the tune. She had been listening for the next note.

A shadow fell across the fern. Devan had crossed the thirty paces without a sound. He took a square of linen from inside his shirt, already folded, and held it out.

She stared at the cloth. Then at his right hand, held a little out from his side, palm up. Along the heel of it ran a thin red line the length of hers. The welt stood up as she watched, hot and weeping at one edge, though it did not open.

"I said behind a tree," she said.

"It was a tree. It was four paces further than the one you meant." He still did not look at his hand. "Take the cloth."

She pressed it into her palm. He closed his own hand around nothing, and for a moment they stood there, two people holding the same wound. She had cut herself a hundred times. She had never had a witness to it.

"Don't wipe the flask rim with that," he said. "Tamarind and pine. It will burn the cut."

"I know." She did not know. A black drop on the edge of her palm was already burning like a spark, and she shook it into the fern with a hiss. "Now I know."

"Does it hurt on you?"

"It hurts on both of us." She nearly smiled. "That's the arrangement."

He looked at his palm at last. "Then it's a good thing I don't need it for anything."

He went back to his stump. She wrapped the linen under her bangle, tied it with her teeth, and finished the cut one-handed, slow and careful. When the flask was full to the neck, it sat so strangely in her left hand that she held it a moment before corking it. Twenty-seven years old, and she had never once held a full flask that was hers.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The ridge looked down on the Resin House like a hand on a map.

They lay flat on wet leaf-mould under a bank of fern. Devan watched with his chin on his folded arms, the way he watched the pass from the wall. She gave him the counts without being asked, low, as she would have to Ammu.

"Forty in the yards. Mostly women, and men of every age down to boys. The dust-coloured wraps are first-year. The ones in grey have a mark, a shape and a number." She knew where each shed door opened. "Eight hunters on the walls. Hooked poles, nets, iron rings on chains. Two at the main gate, two at the drain, and four under the wall."

"Under it?"

"The wall tells you where a man is not."

He gave a small grunt. It was the nearest he came to praise.

On the practice ground a girl of perhaps fourteen was being taught to fall. Her teacher folded her wrist into her chest and made her do it again. Two women ran the length of the east wall and back with stones in their cloths, quick and silent.

"Twenty years ago that was me," she said. "I thought it was a school."

Below them a man limped across the yard, big-shouldered, bear-slow, dragging his left leg a half-step behind the right. He stopped at the edge of the pit and everyone nearest him went still. She had not breathed since he came into the open.

"That's him," Devan said.

"Yes."

"He's lame."

"You did that."

"I struck his shoulder."

"He took it in the thigh coming down." She counted his steps. Eleven to the well, a pause, eleven back. The pause was longer than the leg needed. "Or the ground did. I was a cat, and then a woman, and then I was running."

Devan's fingers had gone white on the fern. She felt it as a tightness in her own hand. He was watching Vellan with eyes that were not afraid and that she could not read.

"He carved Ammu a bird once," she said. "He leaves them on the mats of the little ones. I don't know what that makes him."

"A man who kills with his hands full of birds," Devan said. "I won't answer that."

"I didn't ask you anything."

"You were going to. I'd have said it badly."

She turned her face back to the compound. Below the cook-house, behind a shuttered well, stood the oldest roof in the Resin House, long and low and tiled in black. She knew what it covered. Forty-one cages and one empty. A fox. A hare. A civet with a silver anklet.

In her head, she did not draw it for the plan. She looked just long enough to see that no one went in or out, and looked away.

Devan's left hand moved six inches through the fern and lay beside hers, not touching.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dusk came early, under cloud. The yards emptied in a line towards the long table. Smoke rose from the cook-house.

The door on the far side of the main court opened and Chathu came out.

She had not seen him in the daylight in three weeks, and her stomach turned over before her mind caught up. He was smaller than she remembered. Soft grey hair, soft grey coat. He walked with the uneven step of the old break that had never knit. Under his right arm, held close as a man carries a sleeping child, was a book bound in black.

"That's it," Devan said, very low.

"Yes."

"It's smaller than I thought."

"It doesn't have to be." Her voice sounded strange to her. "He doesn't shift it. Forty lives, and he carries it like an accounts ledger he's late with."

Chathu crossed the court. A hunter nodded; he nodded back. He went round the corner, behind the kennel roof, and out of sight.

Neela counted two hundred. At a hundred and eighty he reappeared, unhurried, and both his hands were free.

"The well," she said. "There's a house over it, behind the kennel roof, with shutters. I've never been inside. Nobody goes in but him."

"You're sure."

"I'm sure he came out without it. I'm sure it isn't in his room, because I was in his room at eleven with a grievance, and I looked." She wet her lips. "Hunters at the kennels guard the cages. The well house has no one."

"Because it needs none."

"Because he's the lock." She heard how that sounded. "Or something is."

Her hand rose to the ridged scars under her collar. The faint burn had begun there on its own, as it did at every sundown now.

"I can see it better from the wall," she said.

"You mean from four feet."

"I mean from the ground."

She had said all this to herself on the ride up. She still had to say it to him. She took Ammu's pouch from her waist and held out the last thumb of resin, the one smear she had kept for inside the compound. It was no larger than a cherry stone, and it shone black in the grey.

"The cache is empty, the horses are half a day down. I'm leaving you a cold camp and no fire." She breathed. "I'll keep to the dark."

"I'll take the pebble," said Devan.

He had already put one in his cheek. She had not seen him do it. When he spoke round it the words came soft. "I know the rule. I'll hold it until you're back."

"You don't have to."

"I know." He turned his face to her, in what was left of the light. "Go on."

She smeared the resin. It burned the throat first, then the wrists, the old fierce bite of scorched tamarind. Within a minute the world went wide and soft and full of smells. The last thing she knew as a woman was his hand, steady, not on her, just near, and his face turned away toward the dark, as she had asked.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The night was cold and wet and perfect.

The other one went down the slope without a sound, along the drain, flat and fast under the bitter fern. The wall had a gap at its foot where the water ran. Hunters passed above, hooked poles ticking on the stone, and she lay in the runoff and let them go by.

She could smell everything. Forty bodies at the long table. Sesame oil. Smoke. Under the kennel roof, a bitter, sweet, familiar reek that tightened her flanks: deer and hare and mongoose and a thin musky animal she did not let herself name. Hounds stirred there, close to the cages, and one lifted its head toward the drain. She froze until it lay down again.

The well house stood at the end of a flagged path. Four posts, a roof of old tile, shuttered windows. She put her nose to the sill and it was there, paper and resin-ink, dry and sour, as if the whole building were a single page.

The door was a plank door with an iron hasp, a farm lock as big as her skull, and a bar of teak that dropped into two brackets. A lock needs fingers. A bar needs hands that lift.

She pushed her shoulder against it, once. It shifted a hair and settled with a small knock.

On the far side of the kennel roof, a hunter's head turned.

The other one flattened against the stone and did not breathe. The man stood listening, one hand on his pole. Then he scratched his neck and walked on.

She knew then what she had come to learn. The book was in a house she could reach on four paws and not open. By day, in her own shape, she would be one woman in a yard of forty.

Back up the slope she went, to the man who waited in the fern with a pebble in his mouth.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Light came grey and without sun. She lay on the ground with the burn of the turn still in her bones and the shaking hunger tearing at her stomach. Her cloth lay folded at her hand. Devan had put it there and turned his back before she woke. He sat with his shoulders to her, spitting the pebble into his palm.

Beside the cloth was flat bread, dried plantain and a gourd of water. She ate it all. She dressed with her back to him.

They went down by the old tappers' path. Rain came and went in the trees. The horses were where they had left them.

The man stepped out from behind a trunk thirty paces from the ravine.

He was tall and stooped, in a grey wrap gone to mud at the hem, with a hooked pole in his right hand. His face was long and creased, and kind in a way that had not been used much. He was looking straight at her.

Her body knew him before her mind did. She stopped.

Pachan.

The hook came down level with her collarbone. The iron point stopped a thumb's width from her throat. It was daylight and she was a woman, and there was no resin anywhere to help her.

Devan was in front of her before she had finished the thought. He did not draw. His hands were open and a little away from his sides, and he stood with his weight low and his eyes on the iron, not the man.

"Don't," Pachan said. "Please. I'm not going to shout." He said it to Devan, but his eyes had not left her face. "I only want to say it."

"Say what?" Neela said.

"You were ten, and Chathu had you at the long table with your feet not touching the floor. You wouldn't eat. I gave you a sweet out of my sleeve." His throat moved. "That's the only kind thing I ever did in that house. And three weeks ago I held a net in that yard the day he bound you. I held it, and I looked at my boots."

"I saw," she said. "Everyone looked at their boots."

"Yes." The hook trembled. He looked at the man in front of her, at his open hands, at the wet black seam of the ridge behind them. "I should shout."

"You should," she said.

"They'd pay a year of resin."

"They would."

Rain began again, soft, on the leaves. She held very still. The part of her that always counted exits found that every one of them was a door in her own head, and behind each stood a man who could have shut it.

The hook lifted past her face, past her shoulder, until the iron pointed at the sky.

"I gave you a sweet," said Pachan, "and then I held the net. I owe you more than a sweet. I can't pay it. This is what I have."

He stepped off the path into the fern and turned his face away, like a man who has seen nothing and means to say so.

She went past him without looking back. She mounted without feeling her legs, and Devan's hand was on her stirrup a moment, as if to steady a horse that was already still.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They rode all day between downpours. She kept the flask inside her shirt against her ribs and felt it there like a second heart.

Near dark she took out Ammu's pouch to tie it to the saddle and opened it out of habit, the way she had every night for weeks. It was light, and loose in her fingers.

She had known it was empty. She had emptied it herself on the ridge. But she had not yet held it empty, in daylight, with nothing left to spend. She turned it inside out and smelled it. Scorched tamarind, faint, and under it the soap-and-sweat smell of a girl who was twenty-five.

Then she folded it flat, as if it might hold something, and put it back in her cloth.

"You'll want to throw that away," Devan said, without turning.

"I'll keep it."

"It's empty."

"I know what it is." She heard the roughness in her own voice. "I'm keeping it anyway."

He nodded once, as a man does when something has been said that does not need his help. The road went red under the horses' feet.

She rode with her mind on a flagged path and a plank door, and a teak bar that wanted hands.

"Say it," she said at last.

"I could go in," Devan said. He had been working it through as long as she had. "Hands at night. I have those."

"You'd have eight hunters under and on the wall, and four of them are where the wall says they're not. You'd have hounds at the kennels that know every boot in that yard, and yours isn't one. Your scent on the flagged path, your boots on the stone." She shook her head. "The other one can reach the door and not open it. You can open it and never reach it."

"Then someone opens it from inside."

"Or we make a way for the hunters to look elsewhere. And someone pays for that." She looked at the road. "Chathu put it where hands and dark can't be in the same body. He didn't need a lock. He needed us to be two people."

Devan was quiet a long time.

"We are two people," he said.

The rain began again. She did not answer. At dusk she would be a woman or an animal, and the book was behind a door that wanted both.

# Chapter 8: Six Years of Letters

Everything on the board was in its place except one roof, and the only person who knew where it stood would not draw it.

The tack room beside the kalari had two leaks and two buckets. Devan had set the tally board on a trestle between them, and rain ticked through the thatch into tin while Bhaskaran bent over it with a lump of charcoal. Neela sat on an upturned pail with her bandaged right hand in her lap. The left did the work. In four strokes it had made the Resin House wall, crooked as a dog's hind leg.

"North side bows in," she said. "Where the drain runs. Don't straighten it. A man who thinks that wall is straight walks into it at night."

Bhaskaran grunted and did not straighten it.

"Main gate. Two hunters. Drain, two. Four under the wall, here, here, and the two corners." She tapped each place with the charcoal. "Forty in the yards by day, and they're all out of the sheds by first light. Cook-house door is barred from inside once the long table has eaten. The well house." She drew a small square, and a bar across it. "Hands and dark. We haven't solved that."

"We have not," said Devan.

Muthu was on the gate, where Devan had put him that morning. Devan had not asked himself why.

The charcoal came to the black-tiled roof behind the cook-house. It stopped.

She laid it on the trestle. She put her hand flat on her knee, and her thumbnail began to drag along her forefinger, slow, the way it did when someone asked her a question she had decided to count instead of answer.

"Roof," she said. "Long. Behind the well."

"How long?" said Bhaskaran.

"Long."

"My lads said forty-odd cages in a row. They carried water to it, after. Iron, a door at each end."

"Forty-one." Her voice was perfectly level. "Draw it as they told you."

"I'd sooner have it from the one who—"

"Sergeant." Devan did not raise his voice. "Give me the charcoal."

Bhaskaran gave it. Devan drew a roof, long and low, with a door at each end and nothing under it. Then he laid the charcoal on the far side of the trestle, away from her hand.

"A roof is a roof," he said.

Neela looked at the board, not at him. After a moment the thumb stopped. "Thank you."

"For what? I can't draw."

The roof looked like a loaf. But Bhaskaran, who had a wife and four daughters and had learned what to leave alone in a marriage, studied the walls with great attention and asked nothing.

The well house sat in the middle of the board with its bar across it, and no arrow Devan could draw would reach it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At dusk she sat on the window ledge in the warden's chamber with the shutters wide and the flask uncorked in her lap.

"Four people walked out of Kettamala on foot," she said. "A justice's escort. Horses at the lower stable, a cart road a day's ride from the gate, and they took the goat path." She turned the cork in her fingers. "Four of them smelled of old nightbark. One put her hand to her cuff when she saw my wrist."

"I saw the cuff."

"If they went to the coast, they're nothing to me. If they went north, to the wood, they're telling Chathu that the warden's wife is alive and has friends." She said it as a sum. "I want to know which."

"Go as far as the scree."

"As far as I can read." She looked at him. "You'll be bored."

"I've been called worse."

She smeared the resin on her throat and wrists. The burn took her at once, as it always did, and he saw her teeth set. Then she turned her face to the window, as she always had, and he looked at the dark wall and listened to her breath go out of true.

The cat went through the window like water. The third slat creaked behind her.

Devan took the pebble from the sill. It was Kannan's, the white one, which she had pressed into his hand the first pebble night and never asked for back. He set it in his cheek and sat on the edge of the bed with the shutter open and the lamp unlit.

The rule had changed in the Resin House yard. A caging needed two voices now, and one of them was hers. He could have slept with his mouth open in a room full of Rema's guards and nothing would have come of it.

He kept the pebble anyway. Nobody was at his door. Rema's escort was gone, Raiu was dead, and his own men slept. If he spat the stone into his palm, no one in Varanad would know.

The chain struck the hour. He counted it, and at the last stroke his tongue pushed the stone to his teeth. He held it there a moment, then set it back in his cheek.

Near the middle watch a thin heat went down his left shin, a line as fine as a thread drawn through the skin, and stopped. He sat very still. A second one followed, shorter. After that came a long nothing. He could not tell whether the nothing was a good sign. The cistern chain clanked twice more. He held the stone and did not stand, because there was nowhere to stand to that would help her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She came up the ledge at the grey of dawn with her cloth round her shoulders and her knees shaking. The third slat creaked again, like a door in a house that was glad to see her.

Devan spat the pebble into his palm and gave her the dish he had kept warm at the brazier. She ate rice and curd on the floor with her back against the bed, in the fast, ugly way of someone paying the morning bill. He sat on the boards a pace away and said nothing until she had finished.

On her left shin was a long scrape, dark at the edges. His own shin carried its ghost, a thin red line.

"Scree," she said. "I went down it on my side."

"You lost the trail."

"In the rain. Three days of it. On the scree everything runs the same colour: water, stone, a boot, a paw." She wiped her mouth with the back of her wrist. "They went down. That much I had before the rock took it. Toward the lightning boulder and the cart road, not north. I'd have said the coast."

"Would have said."

"Because of the other thing." She put the dish down. "Something large went up the scree in the night. Up, toward the shoulder. Stones turned out of their beds, a long drag through the gravel. It was fresh. The rain hadn't filled it yet."

"Large how?"

"Heavier than I am." She did not look at him. "I followed it a hundred paces and then my nerve went. I'm not proud of it. A cat doesn't follow what it can't put a name to."

He knew the name in his own head. A stair, forty-four steps, four furrows across a boy's chest. He did not say it, and he watched her not say it. Below the window the cistern chain began to clank the hour, and her thumbnail dragged along her forefinger, and neither of them spoke until the last stroke had died.

"Don't ask me what I think," she said.

"I won't."

"You're allowed to."

"I know." He got up and set the cloth round her shoulders again where it had slipped. "I'll have Bhaskaran put a pair on the shoulder."

"He'll ask why."

"I'll tell him something large went up it." He let it be the whole of the answer. "That's true."

Her mouth moved. It was the nearest thing to a smile she had managed since the groves.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


That evening the hall was loud.

Muthu was the reason. He was halfway down the long table with a leg of fowl in one fist, telling the story of the mule and the cistern wheel, which grew in the telling every year. Gopan had once, in the fever year, been carried a hundred paces by a mule on the chain. Gopan said it was forty. Muthu said it was a hundred and ten, and slapped the board with the flat of his hand, and the whole hall laughed with him, because it was impossible not to. His laugh came out of his chest like a dropped tray.

Devan sat at Rema's right. She had the warden's chair and he had the bench beside it, which he had not minded. Neela sat at his other side with her good hand round a cup. Janu moved up and down behind them with the ladle, scolding two guards for no reason and feeding them anyway.

Rema had said little all evening. She ate carefully, in small bites, and she watched the room the way she had watched the tallies.

When Muthu reached the mule's last moment and the hall broke into laughter again, she put down her spoon and rose. The noise did not stop at once. It thinned, the way noise does when a person of rank stands, and settled into a courteous quiet.

"I have been a guest at Kettamala for some days," said Rema. "I should like to thank one man in this hall, whose duty brought him no honour, and who deserves some."

Muthu's fist was still raised, with the fowl in it. He lowered it.

"Captain Muthu." She turned her cup in her fingers. "Six years of faithful letters to the queen's household. Not one month missed, I'm told. The household asked me to say so to your face, and I am very glad to." She inclined her head to him, a small and entirely sincere courtesy. "A garrison is fortunate in an officer who writes so well."

Nobody moved.

Devan had known Muthu since Devan was nine and Muthu twelve, in the pit, with sesame oil on their hands. In all that time he had seen the man go quiet exactly when he lied. He waited for the quiet now, and for the other thing that always came after it, the laugh that rode over the silence like a man jumping a fence.

The laugh did not come.

Muthu's mouth was open. His face had gone the colour of tallow. Down the long table Bhaskaran stared into his bowl as if he had lost something in it. Janu's ladle hung over a guard's cup and did not tip. Unni, at the foot, looked from the captain to the warden and back, and understood less than anyone and more than he wanted to.

The cistern chain struck the hour. It was the only sound in the hall.

Six years. Devan did the sum without wanting to. Six years ago Asha had been found on the west stair with Janu holding the lamp. Muthu had begun to write that winter. Muthu had told him the grey-sealed letters went to his mother in Kodiyur, and Devan had believed it, and then he had stopped believing it and had thought *Ittan*, and then *the palace*. There was a sealed packet in the hall strongbox that he had not broken. He had thought he knew what kind of man had written it.

He had guessed the palace. He had not guessed six years of it, or how near those letters sat to the green wax, and the sunbird, and a payment writ dated seven weeks before his wedding.

Under the table's edge a hand closed over his.

He had not known his right fist was clenched on his knee until her palm covered it. It was her left, the nails still bound, and it came across without a glance from her, her face turned politely to the queen's justice. The welt in his palm, the twin of the cut under her bandage, pressed against his own knuckles, and her thumb found the pulse at his wrist. Until the chain's next clank it was the one place on his body where he could feel her heartbeat. Rema's eyes passed over the gesture and moved on, as one looks away from a bride's hand on a husband's.

He opened his fingers. Nothing cracked.

"Captain Muthu," Devan said, "has stood at my left for as long as I've held this fort."

It was true. It was also all he could find that was.

"So I understand," said Rema, and sat.

The hall started again, falsely, in pieces. A guard coughed and another laughed too loud. Janu's ladle came down at last into the cup it had been hanging over. Muthu sat with his hands flat on the board and his fowl forgotten, and he looked at no one.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The hall emptied slowly, the way a hall empties after a death. Devan stayed by the cold hearth with Neela at his shoulder. She said nothing. Her thumb had found her forefinger again, and he understood that she was counting the pillars.

Muthu crossed the floor to him with the walk of a man crossing a ford.

Close up he looked ten years older. Sweat stood at his hairline, though the hall was cool. He glanced once at Neela, and she looked back at him, level and without anger, and he looked away.

"Seventh day tomorrow," said Muthu.

Every seventh day for eight years he had asked it, in the yard, in the passage, once over a corpse at the ford. *Give me a round, Devan. Come on, you great lump.* Every seventh day Devan had said no. It had become their joke, a thing a man says to a friend he is certain will refuse. Devan had never once heard what was under it.

There was no joke in it tonight. Muthu's voice came out thin and steady, like a rope pulled tight.

"Give me a round."

# Chapter 9: The Seventh Day

Devan said yes in the grey before dawn, standing at the lip of the pit with the red earth cold under his bare feet, and Muthu's face did something he had never seen it do in twenty-three years.

It came apart. Then it put itself back together, badly, and Muthu went down the north steps without a word.

The sesame lamps burned in their niches. Above them on the east wall the paired sentries stood with their faces to the hill, as they always did, and did not turn. Muthu stripped to his mundu and rolled his shoulders. He made no joke about it. That was how Devan knew the round was not for sport.

"No marma," Devan said.

"No marma." Muthu came in low.

Devan let him. Bar one blow in the Resin House yard and two touches with his wife, he had struck at air for eight years, and the habit held him: take the first blow, strike no one first. Muthu's forearm cracked into his ribs, over the old white seam from the ford, and the breath went out of him in a grunt. Somewhere in the hall above, a woman he had left sleeping would be feeling the same blow bloom along her side. He hoped she'd stay asleep.

Then his hands remembered. He turned the next strike off his wrist, stepped inside, and put the heel of his palm into Muthu's shoulder hard enough to move him two paces.

"There he is," Muthu said, and his voice shook. "There's the lump."

"Talk or fight."

"Both. I'm a clever man."

They circled. Muthu feinted at the knee and Devan read it from the hip, the way he had read it at fourteen. His body was a stranger to him and also exactly his own.

"The winter Asha died," Muthu said, and came in. Devan blocked. "A man came up from Kodiyur. Household man. Polite. He had a grey cloak." Another strike, flat to the ribs, and Devan folded round it. "Said the queen grieved. Said she wanted you kept safe. Said the safest way was for someone who loved you to tell the household how you did."

"Keep going."

"I'm going." Muthu swept low, and Devan jumped it and came down, and the earth shuddered. "*To keep the warden safe.* That was the whole of the asking. A letter a month. What you ate. Whether you slept. Who came and went at the gate."

"Whether I sparred."

Muthu's guard dropped for half a breath. "Yes."

Devan hit him. A clean knuckle strike under the arm, no weight behind it, and Muthu staggered and came back wheezing.

"Six years," Devan said.

"Six years. My mother wasn't in it. She's alive, she writes to me on the ninth-day post, that part was a lie, and I'm sorry for it more than the rest." He lunged, got a hand on Devan's wrist and twisted. They went down together in the red dust, Devan on top, his forearm across the captain's collarbone. Muthu looked up at him with his teeth showing, and tears ran sideways into his ears.

"I thought it was the same thing," he said. "The crown and you. I thought if I told them everything, nobody could ever hurt you without it being seen."

Devan did not move his arm. The tell was missing. Muthu's voice had been loud, ragged, whole. Devan had stood in this pit and watched this man lie, and the silence always came first. It was not here. It meant less than it should. A man who has told one lie for six years has had time to learn the shape of the truth.

"The grey packet," Devan said. "The one you took into Ittan's cell."

Muthu closed his eyes. "A rider brought the slip off post day, a week and more before the sealing. Lathered, from the household. He gave it to me with an order that's stood ever since: if the Keeper is ever held, put it in his hand and nobody else's." He swallowed. "I never thought I'd use it. Then you held him. I read it first, because I'd never carried one I couldn't read. It was a single line. *Say nothing, and your wife is kept.*"

Devan eased his weight off the arm.

"I took it for a mercy." Muthu's chest heaved. "A pardon on terms. His wife's an invalid, Devan. They'd keep her fed and housed so long as he kept quiet. That's a kindness, I thought. I carried it in like one. I didn't know the queen would send a justice with a block. I didn't know." He opened his eyes. "I'd have thrown the packet in the cistern."

"And his wife?"

"I don't know." His mouth worked. "I don't know. Ittan read the slip and gave it back to me. I'd no one to ask what to do with it, so I put it in with my own letter and sealed the lot again in the guardroom, with the grey stick I carry."

Devan sat back on his heels. The pit smelled of sesame oil and earth, and dawn was coming grey over the wall. The cistern chain began to clank the hour, and the sentries above did not turn round.

"Get up," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The hall was empty, and Janu's girls had not yet come with the brooms. Devan lifted the cord from his neck, and the strongbox key came with it. The packet lay on top of the rest of the post, grey wax whole, thick as a man's thumb.

He set it on the long table, between the cups left from last night. Muthu stood on his right, close enough that Devan could hear him breathe. Devan did not tell him to step back.

"I had this out of your hand openly," Devan said. "In front of the yard, so you'd think it was routine. I haven't broken it. It's lain in that box since the rains began." He looked at the seal. "Today I read you, with you standing here. That's the only way I know how to do it that isn't a theft."

"Read," Muthu said.

Devan broke the wax with his thumb. Two leaves fell out, and a slip no bigger than his palm.

He took the slip first. It was in a clerk's round hand, black ink on cheap paper, with no name and no seal: *Say nothing, and your wife is kept.* He read it twice, and put it down very carefully.

The other leaves were Muthu's, square and large, in the hand of a man who writes as he talks. Devan read them aloud, because he had decided he would.

It was all there. The rain. The garrison's rice. The warden's appetite, which was *better, my lady, since the wedding.* A line about sealing day: the Keeper's five carts searched under the padippura, the Keeper held in the guardroom, the warden's wife signing the closing writ as household witness. Then the morning of the vow, in Muthu's own words: *The warden's wife spoke before the household at noon. She said she came to this house sold. She named Raiu, and the odd girl they call Ammu who died under the blackbark, and every servant down to the goat-boy. She told me, to my face, to spell her name right.*

Devan stopped. Muthu was looking at the floor.

*I do not know what she is,* the letter went on. *But I saw her bind her hand on the warden's wrist, and he did not flinch. I think she loves him. I think he is happier than I have ever seen him. I ask the household to remember that.*

There was nothing else. No mention of the Resin House, the kennels, the book, or what she was at night. No one called Chathu appeared at all. Every line was what Muthu had told him in the pit, and nothing more.

Devan laid the leaves down on top of the slip.

"I didn't know what she was," Muthu said. "I still don't. I never wrote to any guild. I don't know what a guild would want with me, or with this house. I told nobody her cart order, either. The one she gave me the night she rode out. She gave it to me alone, in the passage, and I carried it out in my own head and never said it to a living soul." His voice cracked. "Say you believe me."

"I won't answer whether I believe that."

It landed. Devan watched it land. Muthu took it the way a man takes a hit he has been waiting for, standing, with his chin up.

"Fair," Muthu said.

"Give me the post-key."

Muthu had it on a cord at his belt, the small brass key to the gate's post chest, the one thing in the fort that was entirely his. He worked the cord over his head and put it in Devan's palm. His hand shook. "Do you want me gone?"

"No."

"Devan—"

"You're my captain." Devan closed his fingers on the key. "You'll draw the watch at the sixth clank and eat in the hall, and Gopan will see you at the chain as he does every day. Nothing changes that anyone can see. The post goes through my hand." He met the man's eyes. "A captain I know is the queen's is worth more to me than a captain I don't."

Muthu looked at him for a long moment. His mouth opened. What he said, in the end, was "Salt and ash, you're cold."

"Yes," Devan said. "Go and eat."

He listened to the footsteps go. They were uneven, and then they weren't.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The tally board went up again at dusk. Devan had sent the others away, so it was only the two of them in the tack room, with the rain ticking into the buckets and the lamp low. Neela sat on the pail with her bandaged hand turned palm up. Across her ribs, under the cloth, a bruise had come up the colour of a plum. He had counted it himself in the lamplight and said nothing. She had touched his side, in the same place, and said nothing back.

"Muthu," he said.

"I heard in the yard. Everyone did."

"What did they hear?"

"That you sparred." She almost smiled. "That you hit him once and he laughed like a drain. Unni says the whole east wall turned round to look."

"They didn't turn."

"They wanted to."

He sat down on the trestle's edge. The charcoal roof lay on the board in front of him, a long loaf with two doors. He had not meant to ask. He had meant to decide it himself, as he had decided everything for eight years, alone in the dark with a lamp. Yet he was already speaking.

"I've kept a man in office who has sent six years of letters about me to the queen. I've told him I won't say whether I believe him. I don't know how to hold a man like that." He looked at his scarred knuckles. "I've never asked anyone how to hold a man."

She did not move. She went very still, the way she did at the edge of a thing she had been waiting for.

"You're asking me about command," she said.

"I'm asking you."

"I've commanded no one. I've been a number."

"You know the guild from inside. You know how a house leashes a man." He kept his voice flat. "I know it from a class and a letter. Tell me what you'd do."

She thought. Her thinking had a sound, her thumbnail going along the nail, a slow dry rasp. It stopped.

"Chathu never struck a girl who cried," she said. "He left her sweets and let her be fed and gentle until she'd learned to be grateful. It worked for years. It worked because he was always the one who decided what she was worth." She put the bandaged hand on her knee. "So don't hand Muthu a kindness. And don't hand him a cage. Either one, he'd know how to wear."

"Then what?"

"Give him a job he can't fake."

He turned that over. "Say more."

"A thing that's done in front of everyone, where the work is the whole proof. A man can lie in a letter. He can't lie in a gate that's holding or isn't." She looked at the board. "He chose a side without noticing he was choosing, six years ago. Let him choose out loud. Where it costs him, and where you can see him do it."

The rain ticked. Devan took up the charcoal, and for the first time in the whole planning he drew something with confidence: a square with a hill on it, and a gate.

"The raid goes in on the dark of the moon," he said. "I take the ridge party and Bhaskaran's best. Muthu holds Kettamala."

She said nothing for a count of three.

"With Rema inside the walls," he went on. "The queen's justice, sitting in my hall with her tally chest and the gate log. She won't be watching his loyalty. She'll be reading who goes through that gate, and when, in his own hand."

"Rema is the crown's," Neela said. "The crown sealed your death. Why would her log protect you?"

"Because she wrote against the crown." He did not look up from the board. "At the block, in her own hand, she entered her objection to Ittan's execution. Clerk's copy and all. A woman who'll put that on a roll will put a bad gate on one." He drew a bar across the square. "I don't trust her. I trust that she records."

"If he sells you, it's in the log."

"If he sells me, it's in the log, and she reads it before I'm back. He'll know that when he writes the first line. If he holds it, I'll know what he is, and so will he."

"And if it falls?"

"Then we'll both have our answer, and I'll have an hour to learn it." He set down the charcoal. "That's the cost. I'm paying it."

She leaned over the board and put one finger on the little square gate. It left a smudge. "You asked me."

"I did."

"You'll ask again."

"Probably." It came out drier than he meant. "Don't make a habit of being right."

The corner of her mouth moved. Then it went away, and her thumbnail started again along her forefinger, and he knew before she spoke that she was going back to the thing they had both walked round all day.

"The sword," she said.

"I know."

"Vellan said the watcher told him you lay it hilt toward me. A man on the hill who reads scent-posts can't see inside a barred room." Her voice was very low. "You told me what was in the packet. Muthu's letters say you eat better and you smile. They don't say where you lay your sword."

"No."

"He doesn't sleep in your chamber. Never has."

"No." Devan looked at the gate on the board, and the hall behind it, and the long passage to the south range, and the west stair, and every door in the house with a name on it. "Someone has stood where I lay it, Neela. Someone inside these walls who isn't Muthu."

The chain struck the hour. Neither of them counted it.

# Chapter 10: Every Name in the Barracks

Corporal Kandan said it twice, loud enough for the far table. "He bowed."

Neela stood on the wrong side of the barracks door with a basket of turmeric paste on her hip and her bandaged left hand tucked against it. She did not go in.

"To a woman with a paper," Kandan said. "In his own court. Ittan sat in the guardroom, a man the warden held himself, and the warden stood by while she had the stump scrubbed. Raiu not a month burned, and our lord bows."

"She carried the queen's writ," said a younger voice.

"So did the last one who came for a head. Since when does he have to?" A spoon rang on a bowl. "Since her. Look at him. Eight years he won't lift a fist, and the week she's walking the walls he's in the pit with the captain. She's put something in his cup. Bewitched. There. I've said it, and not one of you will."

Nobody answered. Neela counted the silence the way she counted anything. Fourteen men, by the breathing. One of them chewed very slowly, so he'd have a reason not to look up.

Bewitched. It was nearer the truth than the corporal knew, though no cup had done it. What bound her to the warden was a rope with two ends, and a charm would have been easier to explain. She could not answer that from behind a door. But she could do what she did in every house.

She pushed the door open with her hip and went in.

The barracks smelled of wet wool and lamp smoke. Two rows of pallets, a table down the middle, fourteen faces coming round. She set the basket between the bowls.

"Eravi says heels first," she said. "Who's hiding a blister?"

No hand moved. Then Unni's did, because Unni was twenty and could not lie. He had stood the gate since dawn in wet boots. She sat on the bench, took his foot onto her knee, and worked paste into the raw place with her right thumb. The scab across her palm, three days old from the nightbark tree, pulled at every stroke.

"Your mother still sells salt fish by the lower well?"

He went red to the ears. "Yes, lady."

"Tell her the pepper sacks are dear this year. She should hold her price."

By the time she finished his heel she had two more. By dusk she had the rest of what she'd come for.

She wrote as she went and didn't hide it. A broken roof tile in her sleeve, a stub of chalk. A name, then the village where his mother lived. Vasu, whose mother kept ducks at Kalladi. Sankaran, whose mother had gone back to Neerattu when his father died. Kuttan, who had his mother's mouth and said so, and asked whether Neela would write her a line, since he had no letters.

She wrote it. She put the mother's name at the top, twice, so Kuttan would see it spelled.

She had seen the method once, in a shut room, on a slate gone grey with six years of dust. Asha, nineteen and new, had written every recruit's name and his mother's village. Standing there in the lamplight, Neela had understood it. You stopped a crowd being a crowd by knowing the one thing about each man that was not his rank.

Bhaskaran's lads came first. On the first day they were wary, and by the third they were arguing over which of them she had learned earliest. Vasu showed her the duck-call his mother used at dusk, and half the room tried it. Unni stood up when she came in and then looked as if he wished he hadn't.

Kandan she did not win.

She tried once, plainly, on the second evening. He was cleaning a spear at the end of the table, alone. "Kandan of Mathoor. Your mother is Kalyani, and she's been lame in the left leg since spring."

The cloth stopped. "Who gave you that?"

"Unni. And the well women."

"Unni gave you my mother." He stood. He was thick through the shoulders and past thirty, with a scar cutting one brow, and he never raised his voice. "You said Raiu's name in the yard at your vow. The whole house heard you say it like it was yours to say."

"I was on the stair when he died."

"I was on the wall." He tucked the spear under his arm. "A man's mother is his own, lady. You don't pick her up off the road and keep her in your sleeve."

"Then what do you want me to call her?"

"Nothing. Justice Rema still sits in the south range taking statements for her roll." He said it mildly, the way he said everything. "I've one to give her. A bride who walks a barracks learning every man's house, and a warden who bows the week she does it. The queen's justice can make of that what she likes. Your name's on a crown writ already, I hear."

He went out. Neela stood with the tile in her good hand and his name on it. She knew what a roll was. A roll was how a crown kept a record of who might be hunted.

After a while she did not rub the name out. A man could refuse to be known and still be named. But she did not take it to Devan either. He would move Kandan to the far wall and make him a martyr, and the men who were laughing at the duck-call would stop.

At dusk on the third day she was chalking on the barracks step when she saw someone watching from the yard arch. Devan stood there with Bhaskaran's report folded in his fist. He looked at the tile for as long as it takes to read forty names. His face did not change. Then he nodded once and went in.

She could read him better than most people. She could not read that.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The kitchen was the warmest room in Kettamala, and after supper it belonged to the children and the lame.

Chinna sat on her stool by the door with her bad hip propped on a sack. Kannan lay on his stomach on the floor with the plank between them, the seven hollows worn smooth as spoons. A white goat stood in the doorway, glaring at Neela. Pepper did not approve of her. Pepper did not approve of anyone.

"Wall first," Kannan said, for the third time. "The stone has to hit the wall of the hollow, and then it drops. If it only drops, it doesn't count."

"I heard you."

"You throw like you're stabbing it."

"One good hand, and it's the scabbed one," said Neela. "Be kind."

He wasn't. At the fourth well she was behind by two, and Chinna's wrinkled hand had put a stone in the fifth that sat there like it had been born in it. Chinna ran the plank by an old rule. Rivals' stones could be flicked out. Cheats were rapped across the knuckles with the ladle that lay by her knee, and she had rapped every one of them, including a visiting priest, she said, though no one could say which priest.

Neela leaned over the plank and set her knee against its edge. The plank jumped a finger's width on the third well, and Kannan said "Hey." While his eyes went to the plank she touched Chinna's stone with the nail of her small finger. It left the fifth hollow as if it had thought better of it.

She sat back. Her own stone was already in the sixth.

"Beautiful," Kannan said, with deep injustice. "That one was beautiful."

"Beginner's luck."

"The plank jumped on the third well," said a dry voice from the door, "and Chinna's stone left the fifth by itself."

Everyone turned. Pepper bleated once.

Devan stood in the doorway with rain on his shoulders. He had been to the south range, she thought, and the gate, and a dozen other places that wanted him. He looked like a man who had lifted too much of the day and put it down badly.

"Stones do that," said Neela.

"Mine don't," said Chinna, with the ladle already in her fist.

"Left knee on the plank. Small finger at the stone." He crossed his arms. "My sister's way. I told you she cheated and I never found how." He looked at her hand, resting on her knee, the nails ragged at the quick. "I found how."

"Perhaps she was good."

"She was very good."

Chinna's ladle came down across the knuckles of Neela's right hand, not hard, and a clean sting ran up to the wrist. "Rule's the rule," Chinna said. "Even for the lady."

Across the room Devan's own right hand twitched against his sleeve. A red welt rose over the old scars on his knuckles, as if the ladle had found him too. He looked down at it. Then he looked at her.

And Devan laughed.

It came out of him as if he had forgotten it was in the house, short and startled at first, and then it kept going. He put the heel of his hand against the doorframe, welt and all. Kannan stared at him with his mouth open. In the doorway Pepper stopped glaring. Since the block, the warden had gone about the fort with a face like a shut gate, and the sound went through the kitchen like the first sun after the rains.

Neela looked at her stinging knuckles and found she was smiling at them, helplessly.

He sat down on the sack by Chinna's stool, still shaking a little, rubbing the welt with his thumb. "Go on," he said to Kannan. "Deal her out again."

"She cheats."

"She does. Somebody should keep score."

Kannan gave Neela a pebble and a narrow look. Chinna put the ladle across her own knee where everyone could see it. Neela threw her stone, struck the wall of the second hollow, and watched it drop. Devan was watching her hand, not the stone. She did not look up.

"Unni's on both," he said, low, while Kannan was busy with the plank.

She understood him a breath later. Asha's slate and her tile. "I put him first."

"I know," said Devan, and gave her nothing else, which was more than most men could have given.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She went up alone. The ladle's sting had turned to a heat across the back of her hand, and the day had a long tail to it. Devan stayed to be beaten by a nine-year-old.

The lower court was dark and wet, with a torch hissing at the arch. The cistern chain clanked the hour. She crossed toward the west stair with her bandage held against her chest, counting the flags, and had reached sixty-one when a voice stopped her.

"You walk round it like I do."

Janu stood ten paces off with a lamp in her hand and a covered dish under her arm. She wasn't looking at Neela. She was looking at the foot of the stair.

"Round what?" said Neela.

Janu didn't answer at first. The lamp shook very slightly, and the flame threw a long shadow of the parrot post across the rail, a forearm tall, the beak worn smooth as a river stone. "The flags are uneven there," Janu said at last, in the voice of a woman who had given the same excuse for six years. "Everyone says so." She shifted the dish. "Mind the wet. You've a hand to think of."

She went away toward the kitchen, her lamp swinging. Neela looked down at her own feet.

She had come across the court in a curve. It lay on the wet stone like a rope someone had dropped: a long bend out from the straight line, a wide arc around the newel.

She had known. That was the trouble. She had known since her first week, and when Devan asked why she took the turn on the eleventh step of a stair she had said *I don't know*, and her thumbnail had dragged along her finger while she said it. It had been a small thing then, a quirk, the sort a person keeps like a pebble in a pocket. She had blamed Raiu afterward, because a dead boy was a reason. But she had walked wide before he died.

Janu did it too. Janu, who had found Asha at the foot of that stair. It was not a quirk if two women walked the same curve.

Her thumbnail began to drag. She made it stop, and she crossed to the stair.

The newel stood at the foot with its shut black eye. She stopped beside it. Up close she smelled wet laterite and old wax, and the pala rail was cold under her palm. She did not mean to learn anything. She meant to put her foot on each step in turn and prove it was only a stair. *One. Two.* The steps were worn into dips by a century of boots. At nine her breath began to go short, and she let it, and counted. *Ten.*

She knew the eleventh. She had known it for weeks: a hollow, dull note, like a struck cup with water in it. She put her heel down on it anyway, on purpose, to hear it. It rang.

Her stomach turned over. That was new, too. A sound she had filed away as nothing had no business doing that.

She went down on one knee, and then on her elbow, to keep the scab off the stone. The stair met the wall there, and a slot ran along the riser, black and narrow. Beyond it, low in the stone, was an iron grate. She had seen it before and taken it for a drain.

She looked at the bolts.

The wall around them was black with age and the laterite had gone soft at the edges, but the bolt heads were clean and sharp, and they still showed the file. No bloom of rust. Only a skin of damp. Not new that spring, but newer than the stair. Someone had pulled out whatever stood here before and put this in, and not long ago.

For what? A drain that had blocked. Any fort had a hundred drains. She looked at the bolts and told herself so again.

She stood, because her knee had begun to shake, and looked up the stair. The steps rose into the dark above the torch, and the rail went with them, and her legs refused. She had never refused a stair in her life. She'd gone up a well shaft with a knife in her teeth.

She went down backward, one step and then another, with her good hand on the rail. At the bottom she turned to cross the court.

Her feet swung wide. She watched them do it, an arm and a half out from the newel, the same long curve Janu made. She hadn't asked them to.

"It's a stair," she said aloud.

The torch hissed. The chain clanked.

Neela turned her back on it and walked round to the east side of the court, to the narrow stair cut into the wall, and took the long way up. The wall-walk, the cold turn under the lamp niche, the long passage round the upper court. She counted it all. Eighty-one steps, against the forty-four she would have taken, and she did not slow on any of them.

At the chamber door she stood with her hand on the latch. The grate had not touched her. The stair had not touched her. She was certain of it.

Yet the last time, it had only been a habit she couldn't explain. Tonight it had a file mark on it.

So why did her knees remember?

# Chapter 11: Sharp-Edged Silver

Parukutty bled on the queen's silver before she had finished counting it.

She sucked her thumb and laughed, embarrassed, and set the coin back in its row on the long table. Neela, standing at the back of the hall with her bandaged hand against her hip, stopped breathing for the length of a count.

Rema had paid the death-gift early. She had said so in the same level voice she used for everything. "A widow's gift is owed from the day of the death, Parukutty of the lower village. The harvest is only when the crown remembers to pay it." She wrote the receipt herself, since Ittan's clerk sat in the guardroom, and blotted it with the side of her hand. Twelve coins for Raman the spearman, who had died in single file under the shelf. It was more than the salt factor's six and a half measures and his tally fee together.

The coins were new. They lay in the light from the high window as bright as cut fruit. Sprig on one face. A rim milled so keenly that it had opened a woman's thumb. Not a dent among them, no clipped edge, no sweat-dark from a hundred palms.

Neela had seen that edge before. She had crouched above a cold camp in the lower narrows and watched it wink in a scout's satchel.

Devan sat on the bench by the window with his forearms on his knees. He had said nothing since Rema opened the purse. His face gave nothing away. But his eyes came up once and found Neela's across the hall, and went down again.

"Take it," Rema said to Parukutty, not unkindly. "Pay what you owe before the factor hears you have it."

Parukutty gathered the coins into her mundu with the care of a woman holding eggs. She bowed to the justice, to the warden, and to the room, and went out into the court with her head high.

Neela caught her at the padippura, under the roof, where the rain came off the tiles in a bead curtain.

"Parukutty. How is Minnu's cough?"

"Better, lady. The bark you sent." She tilted her head. "Is something wrong with the silver? The justice said it was good."

"It's good." Neela took a worn panam from her sleeve, dark with years of hands, and held it out. "I've a fancy. Minnu is six, and a child under a bright coin sleeps better, my mother used to say. Let me have one for her pillow and keep this old one instead. You'd get the same weight at the factor's."

It was a poor lie. Parukutty looked at the old coin, then at her, and her mouth twitched.

"You have a funny way of asking for things, lady."

"So I'm told."

The widow picked one bright coin out of her mundu, wiped the blood off it on her wrist, and traded. "For Minnu," she said. "Chandu will say I cheated him. He wanted the best one for his father's shelf."

"Tell him the warden's wife is sorry."

Neela closed her good hand on the sharp rim. It bit her palm, in the same place as the scab, and the scab tore open.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The tack room smelled of wet thatch and bowstring wax. Two buckets caught the roof leaks, and the tick of rain on tin kept time with her pulse.

Devan had borrowed the pepper-weigher's brass scale from the granary. He set it on the trestle and laid the scouts' satchel beside it. It had sat in the strongbox since the narrows. Three bright coins came out of it, as fresh as the day they were struck.

Neela put hers on the table. Four coins now, in a row.

Neither of them spoke. He put one of the satchel coins in the left pan and the widow's coin in the right. The beam shivered and settled. He added a grain of rice to the left. The beam shivered again, and held level.

"Same weight," Neela said.

"To a grain." He lifted both and turned them against the lamp. "Look at the sprig."

She looked. It was a pepper sprig like any other, five leaves on a stem. But the lowest leaf was doubled, a faint second edge ghosting beside it, as if the die had slipped in the striking. She turned the widow's coin over. The same leaf. The same ghost.

"One die," she said.

"One die, and one run. Struck together, I'd say, and not long before the wedding." He put the coins side by side and laid his thumb across them. "The crown paid Raman's widow and the men who killed Raman out of the same box."

"A mint sells its silver to a lot of hands."

"It does."

"A lot of hands could hand it to raiders."

"They could." He did not look up. "Say it. I know you want to."

"I want you to say it." She heard how it sounded. "The thing you're thinking. Who has a mint run that no one counts, and a reason to have two kinds of coin go out?"

Devan swept the four coins into his palm and closed his fist. Neela saw the palm as it went: a thin red welt across the right one, in the exact place her own had opened. It was already beading at one end.

"Your hand," she said.

"I felt it at the gate." He did not open the fist again. "It's where the coin bit you."

"Where it bit you, then."

"Same thing, now." The corner of his mouth moved. Outside, the buckets rang in the dark. "I won't guess it aloud. A guess said in this room is a thing I have to act on. I can't act on it yet. I've a book to fetch first." He put the coins in the satchel one by one, hers last. "Remember the leaf."

"I'll remember the leaf."

She would not forget it if she tried. But he had already pulled the charcoal toward him, and the tile map of the Resin House lay waiting between them, a long square with a well drawn small behind a roof.

"The compound," he said. "You and I, going in."

"You and I are going in two ways." She drew the wall with the side of her thumb. "I go as the other one. Eight hunters on the walls, two at the drain, hounds that know every boot in the yard. They don't know a leopard. They never smell one at the long table. I'll be over the east wall with the dark of the moon on my back, and the hounds will go quiet before I've reached the flagstones."

"And the well house."

"Teak bar and a farm lock. A leopard can't lift a bar." She tapped the charcoal square. "And the book isn't in the house. It's down the shaft."

He stopped. "You know that."

"I know a thing Ammu and I did at eleven." It was easier to say flat. "We dropped a pebble down that well, through a gap in the shutter. I counted. The sound was wrong. Too soon, and too dry. A ledge, a few man-lengths down, cut into the stone, above the water." She breathed. "Chathu wouldn't keep the black book in a cellar a flood could reach. He'd keep it where fire can't and damp can't, and where it's one more place a girl can't climb with a knife."

"Then I climb it."

"You climb it. On a rope, with your hands, while I stand over you and keep eight men from looking down." She moved the charcoal. "I'll open the drain from inside. You come over the wall at the ridge party's signal, and I'll lift the bar with my teeth if I have to. After that you go down, and I stay up."

"And at dawn?"

"At dawn I turn back, and I'm human with no cloth and the hunger on me, and you're sitting on the well curb with the book."

She had said it. It settled on the table like a fifth coin.

"All night," Devan said slowly. "From the ledge to first light. Every page in my hands."

"You and an animal who can't speak or read or carry." She tried a smile. "It's not the plan I'd choose."

"No," he said. "It's the only one that works."

The rain slowed. A bucket overflowed and she rose to turn it.

When she sat again he was not looking at the map. He had the scale's brass chain wound twice round his fingers, and he held it as if he'd forgotten there was anything on the other end.

"I'll ask you something," he said. "If you'll pay a question back."

"A question for a question. It's the dark rule, near enough." She made her hands lie quiet. "You first."

"What would you do, holding it, if you were me?"

"Burn it." It came too fast. "No. Burn the wrong pages and I'd have forty rogues in a night. You'd have to read them aloud by daylight, one at a time, and every one of them would hear their own shape in front of a stranger." She frowned at the chain on his fingers. "That's my answer, and you're asking something else."

"I am." He unwound the chain. "I'm afraid of myself, with that book."

She waited. She had learned that when he was close to a thing, the best course was to be a wall for it to lean on.

"I've kept my hands clean for eight years by never using them," he said. "I told myself that was virtue. It's only what I do instead of choosing. And tomorrow night I'll hold forty lives in my lap, asleep in a yard, and nothing in this world will stop me from reading any page I like but me." His voice did not rise. "There's a name in that book I want badly enough that I don't trust my hands."

She knew whose. She also knew she could not say it, and the knowledge sat in her throat, dry and hard to swallow.

"You'd read a page and cage a man," she said. "Is that it?"

"I'd like to say no." He met her eyes. "I won't say no. I've never lied to you, and I'd rather not start by being brave."

It hurt, how clean it was. She turned her bandaged hand over on her knee and looked at the dirty gauze.

"Then it's my turn," she said.

"You don't have to."

"I know I don't." The rain had almost stopped. "It's what I owe you for the last one. Listen, because I'll only say it once." She counted to three, as she did before a drop. "If I held the book, I wouldn't free anyone first. I'd turn to my own leaf, and I'd take the intake slip Chathu stuffed in his pocket, and I'd read my birth name before I opened a single cage. I want my own name more than I want to be good." She made herself keep going. "I'd like to say I'd free forty before I looked. I've killed for eleven contracts on a promise that I'd get it back. I don't think I'm that woman. I think I'm a woman who'd read hers first."

The tack room had gone very quiet. Somewhere on the wall a sentry stamped and was still.

Devan did not look away, and he did not flinch. He took the answer the way he took a blow in the pit: seeing it, standing, and keeping his feet.

"Then that's what you are," he said. "It isn't wicked to want your name."

"It isn't good."

"No. It isn't *clean*." He almost smiled. "I'd sooner hold a book beside a woman who says that than one who says she's good. The good ones are the ones I don't trust."

"That's an odd thing to tell your wife."

"I'm an odd husband."

She laughed, once, the way she laughed when he surprised her, and was ashamed of how it went through her.

"Then let's bargain like odd people," she said. "Twenty-one of my twenty-seven years I answered to a number. You've had thirty-two years and chosen almost nothing. You've known what I was since our wedding night, and you could have buried me at the pass."

"I meant to," Devan said. "I took a bride the council gave me. I meant to hold her at arm's length for a season."

"And then?"

"And then you counted my stairs." The corner of his mouth moved. "We married twice, Neela. Once for the pepper house's price, and once because I stood in my own court at noon and said it with Janu crying into a ladle. Nobody gave you to me the second time."

"Nobody gave you to me either." She lifted her chin. "So here's my price. You choose it a third time, with a book in your hands and your own name for a thing you want. And I choose it back, with mine."

"Then it's chosen."

He did not touch her. He pushed the scale a finger's width to one side and set the charcoal on the map, and she took it to mean *later*. The roof dripped. The lamp ate the oil it had.

"The rest of the plan," she said at last. "Who's on the ridge, who's at the drain, who's got the rope."

"Bhaskaran's best. I'll write it out." He drew the well house. "And the first rule of a night I'll spend holding the book."

"What's that?"

"That if I start reading a page, for any reason, you stop me."

"With what? I'll be an animal."

"Then you'll have to think of something," Devan said, "that works on a leopard."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


On the last evening, the sky over Kettamala went the colour of old brass. Rema's tally chest stood shut in the south range. Muthu was at the sixth clank of the chain, with the gate log under his arm and the bearing of a man watched by a hawk. They would ride at first light, and by dusk they would be on the ridge above the Resin House.

Neela sat on the warden's window ledge, where she had crouched a hundred times, and held the flask in her good hand.

It was full. Black and cold and very small, with a cork she had cut herself. It smelled of scorched tamarind and pine sap, and the bitterness found the back of her tongue before she had even lifted it. Tomorrow night she would need every drop, for the ridge, the wall and the hounds.

Not tonight.

Her nails had been warning her for days. Three split to the quick, two more going. A night sweat two nights back that she had hidden by throwing the quilt off her side. The ache was a bill, and it never forgot the date. Each night she went without added a line to the account. Those other nights she had had a quiet week ahead to pay for it. This time she had a wall.

Tomorrow she had to crawl over it with eight men on top, on hands already stiff. If she went to bed human tonight, she'd climb with a fever coming up and fingers that did not close all the way. Wanting him now would be paid for in her body, on the raid.

She turned the flask in her fingers. Then she thought of the sword on the floor with its hilt toward her. Of the dish of rice and curd she had found at dawn, on mornings she'd never told him she would need one. Of his hand on the brass chain. *I'd rather not start by being brave.*

She wanted to.

Below, a spoon rang in the kitchen. Kannan was shouting at the goat. The wall behind her was warm from the afternoon, and the last of the light lay along the sill in a red bar.

She pushed the cork home and set the flask in the pouch, in the fold of her cloth, where she could not reach it without thinking. The sun still stood above the ridge. The burn at dusk was an hour off, and it would find her human.

She would have nothing to hide behind in the morning, and the ache would count it. Fine. Let it count.

She stood. The ache in her nails told her the price exactly, down to the quick. She put her bandaged hand against the shutter, felt the third slat give a little under her palm, and went to find her husband.

He was in the kalari pit, alone, with the rope for the well coiled over one shoulder and a lamp in each niche round the rim. He looked up at her step on the stair. The red line still showed across his right palm.

"Stay with me tonight," she said. "Through dusk, with no pebble in your cheek and no resin on me. All the way to the light." She came down one more step. "And then tell me what you'll let me take out of your hand."

He set the rope down on the earth.

# Chapter 12: The Bargain in the Dark

Devan dropped the bar across the shutters himself, and the third slat creaked under it, and Neela's nails itched all the way down to the quick.

It was the hour before the burn. Outside, the sky over the pass had gone the colour of old brass. She stood in the middle of the warden's chamber with her bandaged hand against her chest and counted what she could see. The shutters. The stair door, with Devan's back to it. The bed, the sword on the boards with its hilt toward her side, the kindi full, its spout toward the pillow.

On the sill lay two pebbles, the grey one and Kannan's white one. Neither would be used tonight.

"You said all the way to the light," Devan said.

"I did."

"You'll pay for it."

"I'll pay for it in the morning, and on the wall." She flexed her fingers. Two nails had split in the night. "I've looked at the bill. I want this more than I want to be comfortable."

He crossed the room slowly, as he always did when he thought a thing might spook. He stopped a hand's width from her. He was thirty-two, she was twenty-seven, and they had been married twice. Tonight was the last night in this room before the raid, and the first she had asked for with the bill read out to the nail. Neither of them pretended it was a small thing.

"Tell me what you want," he said.

"That's my line."

"Then say it."

"Sit," said Neela.

He sat on the edge of the bed, looking up at her. His quiet had a different weight than the pit-quiet she knew. She unbound the bandage from her left hand because it was in the way and laid it over the bedpost. Then she stood between his knees, took his face in her hands, scab and all, and kissed him.

It was nothing like the nights before. Those had been stolen: from Vellan's count of days, from the new moon, from the grey at the shutters. Tonight she had paid in advance, and there was no clock to run down. His mouth was slow under hers. When she opened it he made a sound low in his chest, and his hands came to her waist and stopped there, flat and waiting.

"Is this all right?" he said against her jaw.

"Yes. Is it for you?"

"Yes." His thumbs moved once over the cloth. "Tell me if it isn't."

"I will. You do the same."

"I always do."

That made her laugh into his neck, and he laughed too, and her wariness went out through it.

She undid his shirt herself. She went over him the way she learned a house: the seam of white at his ribs, puckered and old, the brow scar she could feel with her lips, the long muscles of his back that flinched when she touched a place she had not known was tender. He let her find each of them. When she pushed him back on the quilt he went, and watched her with a steadiness that slowed her hands.

When she had nothing on but the bangle, she stood a moment longer than she needed. He looked at the pale ridged scars at her throat and the layered ones on her wrists, as he had the first time and every time since. He didn't look away, and he didn't look as at a thing to be pitied.

"May I?" He lifted one hand, palm up, and stopped before he touched.

"Yes."

His fingers rested on her throat, on the old burn, as lightly as a man testing whether a bowl is hot. Then his mouth. Heat ran down through her, and she shut her eyes and let it.

She took her time. She straddled him and set the pace, and when it was too fast, he said her name, and she slowed. When it was not enough, she said his, and he moved his hands to her hips and followed where she led.
