Then she took both his wrists and pressed them to the quilt above his head, to see what he would do.

He went still at once. Entirely still, like a man who had spent eight years never striking first. His pulse jumped under her thumbs.

"Tell me," she said.

"Stay," Devan said, rough. "Don't let go yet."

So she didn't. His stillness broke by itself, slowly, the way ice takes a weight, and he turned his hands in her grip until his fingers laced through hers. It built in her. When it broke she heard her own voice go high and cracked, and under her Devan said her name like something he'd been carrying uphill, and held on.

Dusk came and went in the middle of it.

It reached her as a pull at the throat, a faint burn in the scars, as if a thread had been tugged. She stiffened on him. His hands tightened.

"Neela."

"It's the dusk." She breathed through it, the way she always did. "It's nothing. There's no resin on me, it will pass."

It passed. Her skin cooled, her shoulders came down, and she was only a woman again, breathing hard into the crook of his neck. On the sill, the two pebbles lay where they were.

"Nothing to hold in my cheek," Devan said into her hair, wondering.

"Nothing to hold in your cheek."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Later the lamp had burned low, and the dark rule came back into the room, as it did every night. In the dark you could ask anything. He answered, or he said *I won't answer that*. She lay on her back with her head on his arm, her bandage still on the bedpost, a sheet across her hips. Her nails ached. She did not mind.

"I want to make a bargain," she said.

"I thought we had one."

"We have a marriage. This is a clause." She turned her face to him. "Tomorrow night you'll hold forty lives. You said it yourself. You're afraid of your own hands."

"I am."

"I'm afraid of mine." She said it plainly, as she'd learned to. "You know what I told you. I'd read my own leaf first. And it isn't only mine I'd go through. Chathu keeps the intake slips with the pages. If I sat beside that book with the lamps low and a quiet yard, I could turn other women's leaves one after another, all night, hunting for mine, and tell myself each one was only a page on the way. Forty women's shapes under my thumb, for a name."

"So."

"So whoever holds it, and begins to read a page at night, for any reason, the other takes it from their hand." She pushed up on her elbow. "No argument. No *wait, I've a good cause*. The reason's always good. The one holding it lets go."

He was quiet. She could see the line of his throat in the last of the lamp.

"There's a hole in it," she said. "Tomorrow night I'm a leopard. I can't take anything from your hand. I haven't hands."

"No."

"So the half that's mine needs teeth." She touched his wrist, the right one, over the pulse. "If you begin a page and I'm there, I close my teeth on this. A hold, not a bite. I'd not break skin, because it would open on me too, and I'd like to have both of us whole to carry that book home. When you feel them, you shut it and set it on the well curb. Away from your hands. You don't say why."

"And you don't make me say it."

"I can't speak. It's the one mercy of the shape." Her mouth twisted. "Cat's teeth on your wrist. Remember them."

"I thought of something like that," he said. "When I said you'd stop me. I didn't think what it cost you to be the one who does."

"I'd rather be stopped by you than by anyone. And I'd rather you took it from me when I asked you to than let me be right."

"What if you're right?"

"Then I can be right in the morning." She held his gaze. "By daylight, aloud, in full, in front of the one whose page it is. That's the rule for the loophole anyway."

"Night's for letting go, then."

"Night's for letting go."

"And if I won't?"

"Then I don't go over that wall tomorrow." She said it before she could soften it. "You'd have a book and no one at the drain, and eight hunters. I'd be sitting on the ridge with my nails coming off, watching you decide. I'll not hold the other end of a wire I can't cut."

He turned his head and kissed the inside of her wrist, over the scars. "Then I'll let go," he said. "I give you my word, and I'll say it twice, so you can hold me to it. If I begin to read a page at night, you take it, or your teeth do. If you begin, I take it. Neither of us says why."

"I give mine," said Neela. "Twice."

She had expected the cost to feel like a loss. Instead it felt like a joint, the place where two timbers meet and take a weight neither could hold alone. She settled her head back on his arm.

The wound-share ran between them like a wire in the dark. Each of them had made the other a witness and a wound. Now each had made the other a lock, too.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Now my question," Devan said.

The lamp had guttered. Somewhere on the wall, the chain clanked once for the middle watch. She had known he would ask. She had told him in this room, the night before the vow, that there were eleven before the twelfth, and he might have them one at a time, if he asked.

"One of your eleven," he said. "I'll take them one at a time, as you said. Not the worst. I don't want the worst. The first."

She was silent so long he began to say, "You don't have to," and she cut across him.

"I do. It's the rule." She stared at the dark beam over the bed. "And it's mine to give."

She counted three breaths, as she did before a drop.

"I was nineteen. Chathu called it the first proper work. Before that there'd been errands, a lookout's turn at a market gate, a carrying. Never the knife." Her voice had gone flat. She let it. "A man named Raghu. He collected for a port enforcer in Kodiyur, a man who ran the quays behind the pepper warehouses. Raghu went to the houses of the ones who couldn't pay. He had a stick."

Devan said nothing.

"A boatman's son had stood in his way. Eleven years old. He lost the use of his arm. The boatman wrote to someone, and the someone wrote to the Resin House, and Chathu read the leaf out to me across his table with a tin of sesame sweets between us. A small leaf, in cipher. A little hooked mark in the corner, which he said was for the accounts." She swallowed. "I was given a night, an alley, and a number of paces."

"And you did it."

"I did it. With my hands, at the end of a lane behind the fish market. A strike here." She touched the base of her own skull, a thumb's width below. "The old masters call it the door. He fell the way you fall when you're drunk, and I walked away, and nobody ran. Nobody saw. It was very clean. I was sick in a drain two streets away, and then I went back and ate."

"Ate."

"I was hungry. It was the first thing I learned about myself. I'd been told for years I couldn't kill, and then I could, and then I was hungry." She turned her head. "He had a stick, Devan, and a boy with a ruined arm. I've told myself that for eight years. It's true. It doesn't make my hand any lighter."

She heard him move. His arm came across her, warm and heavy, and stayed. He did not say it was all right. She had braced for that, and was so grateful it hadn't come that her eyes stung.

"A man with a stick, and a boy with his arm," Devan said at last. "I'd have given you that one."

"Would you?"

"I'd have wanted to." He said it carefully, the way he would lay a hand on a hurt horse. "I know I'd want to. That's not the same as having a right to." His thumb moved against her ribs. "Thank you for telling it plain."

"It's the only way I know."

"I know." A pause. "Is there a second?"

Her stomach turned over. There was. It sat a long way down, a door at the end of a passage: a hill fort, a blindfold, a stair she couldn't see, and her own two feet planted at the bottom of it. She didn't know the fort. She had kept it from every telling, even to herself.

He had asked the question lightly. He had already moved on to the next thing as he spoke, she thought, the way a man steps back from a cliff edge he has only just noticed.

"There is," she said. "I can tell it. If you ask."

The dark held. She heard him breathe, in and out, and the small hitch a third of the way along the count.

"I won't ask tonight," Devan said. "After the book."

"Why?"

"Because tomorrow I'll hold a book beside you, and I want to hold it with both hands." He drew her closer. "After. Whatever it is. I'll hear it then."

"All right," said Neela, and the word was nearly steady.

He slept, in time, the way he slept now: one arm across her, the wall at his back, his breath going long and even. The lamp went out. Not a sound came from the passage but the draught by the stair.

She lay awake.

She counted the doors, because it was what she did. Two: the stair door, with the bar he had set himself, and the shutters, barred. Then, because the habit would not let her stop, she counted a third, the one she had no business counting. Beside her, an arm's length off, a man asleep.

Ten more to tell. She turned them over like coins in her pocket, trying to remember the faces. A magistrate with a bad knee. Two sisters in a rich house on the coast. A boy on a river barge, and the man who had paid to have him gone. And a stair, somewhere. A stair with a blindfold on.

She couldn't tell which one he would hate.

Nor which one she would hate herself for, in his eyes. That was a different sum, and a worse one. The chain clanked the hour. Her nails ached in the dark. Beside her Devan breathed, and hitched, and breathed, and she lay with her eyes open and counted them until the shutters began to grey.

# Chapter 13: The Wood Road

The rain broke at the lower ford, and Devan chose that moment to tell her the odds.

"Three in ten," he said, "if the hounds don't quiet."

Neela kept her eyes on the water. It ran brown and fast over the stones, and Bhaskaran's men were taking their horses across one at a time, calling low to each other. Steam rose off the horses. Behind them the wood road climbed away into wet green dark. It was the first dry hour in three days, and she could not enjoy it.

"Three in ten that I come out," she said.

"That you come out unseen. Alive I put higher." He said it as flatly as a man reading a tally. "I don't like either number."

"I like them less than you do. I'm the one going in."

"Then I'll stop giving you them."

"No." She turned her mare's head. "Keep giving them. I'd rather hear the number than have you hold it back and be kind."

His mouth moved. He swung down and she did the same, and they led the horses to the bank where the men could not hear. Eight men, and she had their names by the first mile. Vasu, whose mother kept ducks at Kalladi. Sankaran. Kuttan, who could not write, and rode closest and grinned each time she looked back, because she had written his mother a line. Bhaskaran led them with his four daughters on his mind and a face like a shut gate.

At the padippura that morning Muthu had held the gate log against his chest and laughed too loudly at nothing. "Mind the wall, lady," he had said. Then he had gone quiet, the way he did, and said to Devan, "I'll keep the fort. And the justice in it." Devan had put a hand on his shoulder and said nothing. Neela had watched Muthu's hands. They had been steady, and she had not known what to make of that.

Devan took a stick and drew in the mud of the bank. A square for the compound. A line for the east wall. A dot for the well house.

"Say it as a battle," he said. "Not as a promise."

She crouched. "Eight hunters on the walls. Two at the drain, four under the wall, two at the gate. Hounds that know every boot in the yard. I go in as the other one. I need the hounds quiet, the drain open and the bar lifted. Nothing else."

"And if one hunter looks down?"

"Then I'm a beast in the yard, and he has a pole and a net."

"And a spear." He tapped the dot. "Here is the part I don't like. Anything that opens on you opens on me. A spear in your flank is a spear in mine, a hand's breadth lower, wherever I'm standing. If I'm in that yard with a blade out, and you take a hit, I go down with you. Two bodies for one mistake."

"Then you don't go in the yard."

"No." He said it like a man setting down something heavy. "I stay on the ridge with the lamp men. Bhaskaran's shuttered lanterns, his bows. I hold the signal. I come over the wall for the shaft and nothing else, and when the shaft is done I go back out the way I came." He stopped. "I don't carry a sword into the one fight that matters."

"It's the only way it works."

"I know it is." He looked at the stick as if it had insulted him. "I've commanded men into walls for five years. I have never once sat above a wall while someone else went over it. I hate it, Neela."

"I know."

"I'll do it."

She did not say thank you. It would have been a small word for what it cost him, and he would have left the bank to avoid it. She said, "Then wrap your palms for the rope. Twice. A rope burn opens on me."

"I'd thought of that." He drew a short line beside the well. "And a cut on your pads opens on my soles. I'll walk like a man in new boots tomorrow."

"You'll limp. The men will think I beat you."

"They've thought it for weeks."

She laughed, and so did he, and Kuttan across the ford waved as if they had called him. Then the clouds came back over the trees, and the first fat drop struck the map and spoiled the compound.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


By dusk they were on the ridge.

The wood stopped at a crest of black rock, and the Resin House lay in the valley below, a square of dark with eight small lights along its walls. She had grown up inside that square. From here it looked no bigger than a tray. The wind came up the slope, and in it she smelled scorched tamarind and pine sap, so strongly that her wrists began to ache before the resin touched them.

Devan had put Bhaskaran's men three hundred paces back, at a cairn on the road, with their shuttered lanterns and an order that nobody climbed above the stones. Bhaskaran had asked no questions. He had looked at the warden's wife, then at his own boots, and gone to see to the horses. Neela marked that he looked at his boots. She would remember it later, when she had time to be grateful.

The two of them took a hollow in the rock, where a pit of coals gave out a dull red and no light. Devan laid a flat river stone beside it. Next to the stone he put a folded cloth and a leaf packet that smelled of cold rice and curd.

She watched him do it. "I never asked for the stone."

"No. You came back cold at the pass, and you came back hungry in the yard." He did not look up. "A stone's a small thing."

The last of the light went off the rim of the valley. The burn woke in her throat, as it did whenever the sun was gone, and she took the flask from her pouch and held it a moment.

"Sit where you can see my face," she said.

He sat on the rock across the coals. He had watched her turn more than ten times, and she knew it, and he knew she knew. But he had always lain still with his breath too even and his eyes a crack open. She had been a shadow at a window.

"I'm not asking you to pretend," she said. "Look. All of it."

"I never looked away," Devan said. "I only pretended I was."

"That is the least comforting thing you have ever told me."

"It's true."

"I know. That's why." She pulled the cork. In the yard at the Resin House she had turned because she had no time left and a man's life was in the mud. That had been a leap. This was a door, and she was holding it open on purpose. She had never done that for anyone, and her hands were not entirely steady.

The resin was cold and black, and it smelled of every dusk of her life. She drew it across her throat first, then each wrist, and it bit through the old scars like a hot wire.

"Neela," Devan said quietly.

"Don't help. Just stay."

The burn climbed into her jaw and down her spine, and the pull began, every bone coming untied. She did not look away from him. His face stayed on hers. He did not glance down at what was happening to her body, and he did not look as if he were watching a marvel or a horror. He looked like a man receiving something handed to him.

Her hands left her. The ground came up. The valley floor opened below her like a lit book, with every lamp-wick in the walls and every hound's breath. His smell struck her whole: sweat and wet wool, horse, iron, and underneath it the thing that was only him.

Through all of it his face held, turned toward hers.

"Evening," Devan said, to the other one.

She could not answer. She put her head down and pushed it, once, against his knee.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She kept the edges.

It was what the animal did with a strange place, and she let it. Round and round the hollow she went, soft-footed on the wet rock, past the horses' scent, which tasted of meat to her and which she took care to keep downwind of. She marked every cleft where a man could stand. She counted breaths as she went.

Devan had settled his back against the stone, hands loose between his knees.

"I'll talk," he said. "If you like. You said once the worst of the shape is that nobody speaks to you."

She had said it on the second night, in the dark, and had not thought he was listening. She slowed and came round the coals on her next circuit, and he took that for yes.

"Pepper ate the last of the wedding marigolds," he said. "Off the garland, off Kannan's neck. Kannan argued before the whole kitchen that she had been invited."

The other one's ears went flat with something that in a woman would have been a laugh.

"Janu said the goat had better manners than the bridegroom. She was right." His voice stayed low, steady, pitched under the wind. It held the camp like a rope holds a cart. "Asha drew parrots on the cistern wall when she was seven. Janu scrubbed them off. She drew them back. It went on two years. Janu says now that she let the last one stay a month before she scrubbed it, but she's lying."

Round the coals, past the stone, back. Each word fell where she could find it.

"She drew the newel at the west stair with a whole beak. Mended. She said a broken beak insulted the bird." A short pause. "I don't know why I tell you that."

The other one's pelt crawled along her back, and she put it down to the cold and kept walking. It was a wet night. Her nails would have been aching if she had had nails.

He did not ask her anything, and he did not wait for her to answer. When she came round the fourth time his voice slowed. When she came round the fifth, it stopped, and she looked up.

He had put his hand flat against his side, low on the ribs. The old seam, the white one. He was breathing in short, careful counts.

She stopped. Thirty years of rain in the hills had put that damp into him, and a long ride had worked it. He did not say a word about it. He never did.

She crossed the hollow and lay down along his side, spine against his ribs, as heavily as she could. The heat of her went into him.

Devan went still. "May I?" he said, and when she did not move he laid his hand on her shoulder, then buried his fingers in the pelt. His breath shook once and steadied.

They stayed like that. Nothing in the valley below changed. The coals ticked. Her side rose and fell against his, and after a time his ribs eased under her, one hard breath and then a long one. He leaned his head back on the rock.

"It's gone quiet," he said.

She did not move. She had asked a man once to stay with her through dusk, all the way to the light, and he had. The night had its cost. Tomorrow's hunger would take her at dawn, and the ache was still waiting in her nails. She counted what she was spending, and she spent it.

Below them, a hound complained in its sleep. Far off on the wall, a lamp moved and was still.

She got up.

Devan did not try to stop her. He drew his hand out of her fur one finger at a time and set it on his knee. "Two sawing calls," he said. "A count apart, when the drain is open and the bar is up. I move on the second. Not before."

She turned her head and looked at him.

"I'll sit here," he said. "I'll count a thousand, then stand. I'll be at the rope when you call, and not one step ahead of you." His voice did not lift. "I'd follow you down. I want you to know I would."

She could not tell him that she knew. She went to the lip of the hollow instead, where the dark began, and looked back once. He was a shape against the red of the coals with his hands open on his knees. Behind him, three hundred paces off, Bhaskaran's lanterns stayed shuttered.

Then she went down the slope.

The rock gave way to wet bracken and then to thorn, and the wall rose out of the dark with its eight lamps. The wind brought her the compound whole: tamarind scorch, hound, lamp oil, old cooking fat. A trace of musk in it, two days old and heavy. The hounds were quiet. They were quieter than they should have been.

She went on, low and alone, toward the wall, and the lights did not move.

# Chapter 14: The Shuttered Well

The drain path had been bigger when she was nine.

The other one did not mind. She flattened her ribs to the wet stone and slid, shoulders first, into the culvert behind the midden. The wall's footing had washed out there before she was born. Water ran through it, and so had two girls who were told every day they had no use for dogs. Ammu had gone first, always, humming the fishing song under her breath. Neela had gone second and counted.

She counted now. Eleven body-lengths of cold slime and root. Then the grate at the far end. It was shut with an iron pin on a short chain, and she took the pin in her teeth and drew it out as slowly as a splinter. The grate swung. She was in the yard of the Resin House, and the whole place smelled of her childhood.

Scorched tamarind. Pine sap. Cold cooking fat. Over all of it lay the bear.

The musk was two days old, thick and sour, and it lay on the yard like a hand. It explained the hounds. They were down in their straw by the kitchen wall with their chins flat and their eyes rolled white, and not one lifted its head. They knew exactly who had walked here.

She set the pin on the stone, where Devan would feel it with his boot. She left the grate ajar. The first thing was done.

Eight lamps burned on the wall. She counted them, and the men who walked beneath them. Two hunters at the gate, their backs to her. Two on the drain side, looking out. Four under the wall. None of them looked in. Nobody had looked in at this yard for twenty years, because everything inside it belonged to Chathu.

She took the flagged path along the kennel roof, hugging the shadow of the eaves.

The wind came through the slats, and she smelled the cages before she saw them. There were forty-two in a row, rank with the sour sweetness of animals that had stopped being afraid. A deer breathed in the first one. A hare. Something that had been a mongoose and now only paced. She did not turn her head. She had told herself on the ridge that she would not.

She turned it for the fourth from the end.

It was the only cage with no breath in it. The door stood shut and the straw lay pressed flat in one long hollow, the length of a woman curled on her side. Scratches ran down the iron at the height of her own shoulder. Forty-one of these cages held someone. This one held the shape of her, three weeks cold, kept for the day she was brought back.

*Nobody swept it,* she thought. *They were saving it.*

She went on, and her stride broke at the third cage from the end.

Sandal smoke, long gone, and under it fish oil, and a girl who had fallen asleep over a slate. A civet lay in the straw with its small masked face on its paws. It lifted its head.

The lamp on the wall caught something at its hind leg, a thin line of silver. An anklet. The bells had been cut off years ago.

The other one stopped. The woman under her fur did not.

*Kirri.* Kirri, who had been the big girl at the long table and taught the little ones their letters, and could not spell tide. Who had sworn to send that anklet home in a fish basket the day she had a boat.

The civet looked at her. It was a long, mild, unfocused look, the look of an animal at a smell it half-remembered. Then the head sank onto the paws again. There was no one in the eyes to sink.

Neela could not speak. She had no hands. She could not put a cloth over the bars or a name in the air. She stood there a count of three, which was all she could spare, and then she did the only thing the shape allowed. She went close and breathed out through the slats, once, hard, so that Kirri would have her scent on the wind and no stranger's.

*After,* she told her, with nothing at all. *After the book.*

She went on down the path with her ears flat. She did not look back. A hunter looks back.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The well house stood at the path's end, four posts and old tile, shuttered. The door was plank. The teak bar lay in its two brackets, and over it the hasp hung on its staple with a farm lock through it, shut.

She could not pick a lock. She had no fingers for it.

But the post that held the staple was older than she was. She had pressed her thumb into that wood at eleven, on a dare, and it had come away damp. She hooked one claw behind the staple and eased it. The wood gave with a small sound like a sigh. The staple, lock and all, came out of the post and hung from the hasp. She lowered it to the stone by its weight and did not let it clank. Then she took the bar's end in her teeth, rolled it from one bracket, and let it down onto her shoulders.

The door opened inward on a smell like a held breath.

Inside was a ring of cold stone. The shaft opened in the middle of it, a black mouth with a curb around it. Over it ran a beam with an iron pulley, and a chain ran over the pulley and down. She put her forepaws on the curb and looked.

Far below, a few man-lengths down, a ledge had been cut into the shaft wall, dry and square, well above the black shine of the water. The chain ran down to it. Someone had lowered a box onto the stone and left the links slack. It was sealed all round, with a padlock the size of a fist. From inside, thin and bitter, came the scent of resin-ink.

The black book.

She was so close that she could have wept, if the shape had allowed it. She could reach the chain with her teeth. She could not haul a box that weighed as much as a child up a wet shaft with only her jaws. And she could not open that padlock with a boulder.

*Paws can't open it.* She had known that on the ridge, and Devan had known it, and the plan had stood on it. She had a part and he had a part, and the box lay between them like a hinge.

She lifted her head over the curb and gave the call.

It came out of her throat like a saw drawn once through green wood, rasping and low. She counted to the length of a held breath. She gave it again.

Two. A count apart. The drain is open and the bar is up.

*Come.*

She went to the door to watch the yard, and light swung round the corner of the kennel roof.

A hunter, thin, with a lantern on a hooked pole. He walked the path the way a man walks a round he has walked a thousand times, looking at his feet. He was ten paces off. The light came across the flags toward her in a slow yellow arc.

"Hup," he said, to nobody, and then, "What's—"

She went at him low.

She had no time to be afraid for him. She put her shoulder into his knees and her weight behind it, and he went down backwards with a grunt, and the pole swung. The hook caught her along the ribs. It was a short, flat, burning blow, and it opened her skin in a hot wet line under the fur.

The lantern hit the flags and rolled. She had half a breath to know what that blow would mean. Somewhere over the wall a man in wrapped gloves had just felt the hook land on his own side, with no enemy in reach. She hoped he would not stop.

She drove a forepaw onto the hunter's chest. His head cracked on the stone. His mouth opened, and she took her paw off his throat at once, because she would not be the thing that killed him over a lamp.

He breathed. She smelled it: sour breath, a boy's sweat, no blood but a little. Out cold. She had not killed him, and she was grateful, in the sharp quick way she was grateful for any door that did not need to be shut behind her.

The lantern had rolled against the kennel wall. Its flame lay down along the wick and began to lick the stone, and a drift of dry straw beside it caught the edge of the light. She raked a pile of wet midden straw across it with her paw and pressed it flat. The flame hissed, smoked and went out.

Dark. She stood in it, shaking, with her ribs on fire.

Something moved on the low wall above her.

An otter sat there. Sleek, black-wet, whiskered, sitting up on her haunches the way they do, watching. It was Pavi. The white chin gave her away, and the way she held one forepaw curled, the old break that had never set. Pavi, who had come to the House at eight with a fever and a doll. Neela had braided her hair the night before her first turning, because Ammu was with the little ones that night and there was nobody else.

Pavi was twenty-four now, and on the night watch. In shape, she was the same as the dark rows along the wall that Neela had not understood until this instant. Neela had left a House where watch was kept by hunters. Someone had put the shapes on the walls.

*That is a thing to ask later.*

Pavi looked at the hunter on the stones. She looked at the dead lantern. She looked at the leopard.

Neela did not move. If Pavi gave the cry, the whole yard would wake. An otter has a thin high whistle that carries across a pond. She had heard it once, at nine, in the sheds.

Pavi turned her head away.

She turned it slowly, deliberately, toward the outer wall, and kept it there. Her small round back was a wall of its own. She did not look again.

*Thank you,* Neela told her back. It was a stupid, useless thing to say to someone who could not hear it. It was all she had.

She dragged the hunter by his collar into the shadow of the nearest post, where a passing lamp would see a heap of cloth and not a man. Her ribs sang with every pull. She propped the well-house door with her shoulder and stood in it.

The wall was dark above her.

Then, softly, she heard it. A scrape of cloth on laterite. A weight set down on stone, and a breath held and let go. Boots on the flags, going quickly, going quietly, a man who had wrapped his soles in sacking so that the stones would not ring.

Devan came round the kennel roof with a coil on his shoulder and a pry bar under his arm. His palms were wrapped to the wrist. His other hand was pressed hard against his side, and his shirt was dark and wet under his fingers, right over the old white seam.

He did not speak. A voice carries across a yard at night, and the nearest lamp was thirty paces off. His mouth was shut hard around it.

He saw the dark heap at the post. He saw her. His eyes went to her ribs. She drew a breath, and his face tightened with it, as if the breath were his. She let it out, and his shoulders dropped a half-inch with hers. His fingers shifted on the stain.

"Yours?" he said, almost without voice.

She dipped her head once.

"Then it's mine." He crouched, so that his face was level with hers, and put one wrapped palm briefly flat between her ears. It was warm, and he did not linger. "Good," Devan said. "Hold the door."

He went past her into the well house with the rope. She heard him loop it over the beam and test it, the small creak of the pulley and a breath. She heard him go over the curb. The chain clinked.

She stayed in the door and did what she was best at. She counted.

Four lamps she could see. The nearest hunter was thirty paces off, facing away. The hounds had not stirred. Pavi's round back still did not turn. Down in the shaft, the rope groaned, and then the groan changed as it took his full weight.

Seven breaths. The pry bar scraped on iron below her, and she heard him swear without sound.

Then the wind turned.

It came round the corner of the cook-house roof and across the kennels, and with it came the smell she had braced for since the ridge. It was not two days old now. It was fresh and rank and close, and it was walking.

A foot came down on the flags beyond the cook-house corner. Not the rhythm of a bear's stride. A drag, and a step. A drag, and a step, as one hind leg took the weight a half-beat late. New pain, a month old. But the weight behind it she had heard on the long-ago nights when she was nine and had pretended to be asleep.

The bear rounded the corner with its nose high.

He was bigger than the wall's shadow had a right to be. The shaggy black bulk of him blotted the lamp. The pale Y on his chest hung like a scar, and his small eyes were wet and sharp. He stopped. He swung his long muzzle from side to side and drew the wind in, in great wet breaths, and found the lantern smoke, and the hunter, and the otter, and her.

He knew her. She watched him know her. The head came round and settled, and the hind leg shifted, and the breath that came out of him was not a snarl. It was something close to satisfaction.

Behind her, in the shaft, the pry bar rang on iron. The rope creaked and swung. A man at the end of it, with his back to the sky, was working at a box on a ledge, and every cut she took tonight he would feel.

*Hold the door.*

The other one lowered her head and stepped out of the doorway onto the flags. She put her whole body between the bear and the well.

# Chapter 15: Bear and Lamp

The first claw opened across his back while Devan was reaching for the hinge.

He did not cry out. He had one boot on the ledge and the other braced against the shaft wall. A shuttered lamp hung from a nail beside his head, giving a yellow slit of light on wet stone and the iron box at his feet. The heat started below his left shoulder blade and ran down to the right hip in three lines. A fourth came after, higher, burning like a dropped coal.

*Shallow,* he told himself. *They're shallow.* He had been told they would be.

Above him, through the ring of the well-house floor, came sounds no man makes. A heavy drag across flags. A cough, deep and wet. Claws that scrabbled and found purchase and scrabbled again.

*Three in ten.* He had said the number himself, on a river bank, as if reading a tally.

He put the flat of the pry bar under the hinge plate. Chathu had trusted his lock. It was fist-sized and good, and it hung on the hasp as shut as a jaw. But a man who trusts a lock forgets the hinges. Devan set his boot on the curve of the chain and leaned his whole weight on the bar. The first rivet sheared with a crack he felt in his teeth. The second held. The fourth welt tore wider as he leaned, and he hissed through his nose and leaned harder.

The third rivet went. The lid came up on its ruined hinge like a mouth, and the lock stayed shut on nothing.

A black book lay inside, wrapped in greased cloth. It was thick as his forearm, and it smelled of scorched tamarind. He lifted it out in both hands. His thumbs found the edge of the cloth, and he stopped them there.

He had sworn twice, in the warden's chamber with a lamp between them. If he began to read a page at night, she would close her teeth on his wrist, and he would shut the book without asking why. He had made the oath before he held the thing. It was a different oath now that it was heavy in his hands.

He did not open it. He pushed it inside his shirt, against the old seam, and cinched his belt over it.

Then he took the rope and climbed.

Each pull opened something. He counted hand-lengths as she would have counted them. Nine. Ten. The welts on his back stretched and tore a little at every reach, and the sacking on his boots slid on the wet stone. At eleven his head came above the curb.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The well-house door stood open on the yard. It was full of lamplight, swinging.

Neela was not in the doorway. She was on the flags ten paces out, down on her side, with one forepaw lifted and her lips drawn back off her teeth. Her fur was black with wet from the ribs to the haunch.

The bear stood over her.

It had risen onto its hind legs, the bad one cocked and the good one planted. The pale Y on its chest shone in the lantern light. Both forearms were lifted like a mallet raised to a post, and every claw was open.

Devan did not strike first. He had not struck first in eight years.

He crossed the ten paces in four strides and did not strike first now. He waited for the arms to start down.

They started. He swung the pry bar up and across with both hands, and the iron met the bear's shoulder at the joint, with a sound like a mallet on wet timber. Under the iron he felt a ridge, an old healed seam in the muscle. It was where his own blow had landed a month ago, from one knee, in this same yard.

The bear roared.

It was a huge, cracked, furious noise, and it filled the yard to the wall. The great body came down off its hind legs and swung to face him, one arm hanging wrong. It was the second time in a month Devan had struck the bear. He stood over Neela with the bar low and did not step back.

From the east wall a horn began to blow.

It was a flat, hard hunters' horn, going again and again. Lamps jerked along the wall. Boots came down the stairs. A hound found its voice, and then another.

*If the House wakes, Bhaskaran comes. If it doesn't, he comes at the lamp. Either way he comes.*

He was counting the breaths to that when the east wall bloomed with light.

Six lamps rose over the coping at once, and under each a man came over. Two more climbed from the drain grate, bent double, and straightened with spears. Eight spears, and Bhaskaran with them. They ran in a line across the yard with Bhaskaran at the head, his face a shut gate. Kuttan was grinning, even now.

"Spears out," Bhaskaran said, in his parade voice. "Lamps up. Hold the line. Nobody speaks to the animal."

It was Devan's order, given on the ridge, and Bhaskaran gave it flat as a tally. Eight points of steel came level in a row, and six lamps threw one hard white wash across the flags.

The bear swung its head from the light to the spears and back to the light.

It was a big animal and a hurt one. It dragged the hind leg, and the shoulder hung. It went back one step on the flags, a drag and a step. Then another. A bear knows light and steel at a distance. A man inside it knows more. He had hunted in the dark a long time, and he had never once been seen.

It backed toward the cook-house with its head low, and the light followed it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Someone stood on the cook-house step, holding a small lamp.

He was old and soft, in a sleeping robe with a shawl over his shoulders, and his left foot was set a little crooked on the stone. Devan had seen him once before, at dusk in this yard, reading a page aloud while it burned to nothing in his hands. He knew him now by the same stillness, and by the soft way he held the light, the way one holds a candle in a sickroom. That evening the old man had not shouted. He had only looked down at the ash like a clerk finding a loss in his books. Hunters were coming in at his back, with poles and nets and iron rings on chains.

Chathu looked at the bear, and the spears, and the leopard bleeding on the flags. Then he looked at Devan's belt.

The book was a hard square under Devan's shirt. He had not drawn it. Chathu had seen the outline.

Devan took it out.

He did it without a plan. Between eight spears and a bear, there was no room for one. He drew the black bundle from his shirt and held it up in the lamplight in both hands.

"Call them off," he said.

The hunters stopped. Their poles dipped, and they looked at their master.

Chathu did not look at the poles. He did not look at the spears. His gaze went past all of it to the dark corners of the yard, where something was gathering.

"That's eleven years of feeding, warden," he said, in a soft, patient voice. "Every grain. Put it down, and I'll write the rest off."

"Your terms are late," Devan said.

Chathu's mouth moved as if he were adding. Then, quite simply, he turned on his bad foot.

"Vellan," he said. "The wood. Now."

He went. He went across the back of the cook-house at a rolling, scuttling run, faster than a man of sixty-one with a broken foot had any right to. The bear went after him in its drag and step, and the hunters broke and ran with them, bearing a stunned man between two of them like a sack. The dark under the trees took them, and the lamps lit only empty ground.

"Sir?" Bhaskaran said. "Pursue?"

"No."

Devan stood with the book in his hands and understood what he had just done.

He had known the rule since the border school. He had known it as a line in a book. *A page is a life. Whoever holds it is a master, whether he wants to be or not.* Chathu had known it too. Chathu had looked at the book in a stranger's hands and seen at once what his house would do next. He had run, and he had been right to.

The yard was not empty.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They slid out of the sheds and from behind the kennel roof, from under the wall and the drain. They came as low shapes at the edge of the light, and they did not hurry.

He did not count them aloud. Forty, near enough. There was a long-legged grey animal that stood like a heron's shadow, and a small lean one that was all eyes. There was a dark otter with a white chin and a forepaw held curled to her chest. There were others he had no names for, and he would not look for names. The hounds had stopped. The spears had stopped. The only sound was the lamps spitting.

They stopped where the light stopped, in a ragged half-ring, and sat or stood. None of them looked at the spears.

All of them looked at the book.

Forty to eight. He did the sum Chathu had done, and it ended where Chathu's had ended. Each of them was a trained killer in a shape no spear was made for. They had been fed and caged by the man who held that book, and now the man had gone, and it was held by a stranger with a pry bar and four welts across his back.

Devan did not move his hands. The book weighed more with every breath he held it.

"Lower the spears," he said.

"Sir—"

"Lower them, Bhaskaran. Slow. Point them at the ground."

They lowered, one by one, slowly, like men easing off a sleeping dog's tail. Nothing stirred in the dark. The half-ring stayed where it was.

At his feet Neela got up.

It took her three tries. She stood with her flank against his leg, and he felt her ribs working against his knee. The wet on her fur was not all hers. She leaned on him, and he let her. He could not put a hand to her side with eight men and forty animals watching. He could not wrap a cloth round a hurt he felt as his own. He stood, and she stood, and in the lamp's glare his welts wept into his belt.

"No one speaks to them," he said to the men, quietly. "No one says what they are. You've seen animals tonight, and that is all you saw. If any of you are asked, you were here for a book."

"Yes, sir," said Kuttan, who could not write, and who was not grinning now.

He did not open the book. The back half held resin-ink pages, and he did not know whether the words in it were shapes or cipher. He did not mean to learn. He stood with it shut in both hands while the sky over the cook-house roof went from black to the colour of a bruise.

*Hold it till she turns back. That was the plan.* He had said it on the ridge, with his hands open on his knees. It had sounded like a small thing then.

The grey came slowly. Under the wall, one of the shapes rose and turned. Another followed. Without a sound or a signal they went in a file across the yard, toward the long low sheds by the wood where the resin smell lay thickest, and one by one the dark doors took them. Somebody had taught them long ago to turn back unseen. The training held, even now.

"Spears to the gate," Devan said. "Faces to the gate. All of you."

They turned, stiffly, glad of an order. He heard the boots grind round on the flags.

He took her folded cloth from inside his coat, where it had ridden against his ribs since the ridge. He put his back to the well-house door and held the cloth out behind him at arm's length. He kept his eyes on the line of Bhaskaran's shoulders. He had watched her return once, in daylight, in his arms. Tonight she had the wall of his back between her and the world, and nothing else.

Behind him he heard it. A breath that was not an animal's, long and shuddering. A woman's cough. The cloth slid from his fingers.

"Thank you," Neela said, hoarsely. She did not say it loudly.

"Don't," Devan said. "Eat."

He found the leaf packet in his sleeve, cold rice and curd, and passed it back without turning. After a moment he heard her sit down on the curb of the well, and heard her hands shake on the leaf. He kept his face to the gate.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They filed from the sheds at first light, dressed.

They wore grey work wraps, with wet hair, shaking with the hollow hunger of the morning after, as she was shaking. They came one at a time, then in twos, and stood in the pale yard in rows no one had ordered. There were men and women, tall and short, and one young woman at the front who held her right hand curled against her chest. A white scar ran across her chin.

Nobody spoke. They looked at Devan's hands.

He had been the warden of Kettamala for five years, and he knew what it was to stand in front of people who waited for an order. This was different. At the fort they waited because he had earned it, or been born to it, or because an office gave it. These forty had waited all their lives on a book. Now they stood in the cold, hungry, with the day opening behind them, and every face was turned to the thing in his hands.

*Whoever holds it is a master.* He felt it, plainly, as a kind of lean in the yard. If he spoke now, they would do what he said. He had never wanted anything less.

Neela had come to stand beside him. She was in her day cloth, hair stuck to her neck, and she held one arm clamped across her ribs. The leaf lay empty in her other hand. She was grey under the brown of her skin. She did not look at the book. She looked at the faces, one at a time, and he knew she was counting them and giving each one a name.

"Pavi," she said, very low. The young woman at the front flinched, and her chin came up.

"Chathu is gone," Devan said.

His voice carried across the yard without trying. He did not raise it.

"This was his. It isn't mine." He turned to her. "I said I would hold it until you turned back. You've turned back."

Neela did not move. "Devan."

"I know what it is."

"If I take it, I'm what he was." She said it so quietly the front rows could not have heard, but they saw her mouth. "Whoever holds it."

"Then you'll hold it where all of them can see you do it." His hands were steady. He was surprised. "And if you ever begin to read one of those pages at night, I'll take it out of your hand and give you no reason. You gave me leave. I'm giving you the same."

Her eyes stayed on his face. Her thumbnail dragged along her forefinger, once, the way it did when she had a count she would not say. Then it stopped. She had been hunted all her life by this book. She had wanted it burned, and she had been right to want that. No one had ever held it out to her.

*Whatever is in it,* he thought, *there is one page I want more than I have wanted anything in eight years.* He did not look down at the cover. He did not ask.

He held the book out to her in both hands, in the open grey light, in front of all of them.

Neela put down the empty leaf. She wiped her palms on her cloth, slowly, once each. Then she took it against her chest. Her arms sagged a little and caught.

It was done. His hands were empty, and lighter than he had known hands could be. His welts burned in the cold, and he had never felt so little like a master.

Forty faces watched her. Not one of them moved.

# Chapter 16: Lookout: Seventeen

They bowed.

Nobody gave the word. Pavi went first, the young woman with the white scar across her chin. She folded from the waist and made her curled right hand lie flat on her thigh beside the left. The row behind her followed, then the next. The bow ran back across the grey yard like wind through young rice, until Neela was looking at forty partings and no faces.

It was the bow they gave Chathu at the long table. She knew it in her knees. She had given it every night since she was six, until she learned to wait for his back to turn.

The black book lay against her chest. Greased cloth, scorched tamarind. Its corner pressed the claw lines along her flank, and each breath found the place.

*Which am I?*

A sister would bow back. An owner would let them stay down. She stood between the two and could not feel which one her body meant to be.

"Up," she said.

They did not move. Her voice was hoarse from the turning. Perhaps it hadn't carried.

"Up. Please."

They rose together, all forty, in one breath.

That was the answer, and she hated it. No one rises together for *please*. They rose for the weight in her arms.

"Pavi," she said. "Is the cook-house open?"

"Barred from inside, Seventeen. After the table."

The number landed like a hand on a bruise. Neela kept her face still. "It's Neela. You can use that." She looked along the rows. "Open it. Eat what there is. Everyone."

Nobody moved until Pavi did. Then they all went, quietly, the way they had been taught to go.

Devan had not stirred from her shoulder. He stood with his arms at his sides and his shirt dried stiff across the back where the welts had wept. He watched the last of them cross the yard. Then he gave Bhaskaran two low orders about the gate and the wall. When he turned back, his face held nothing but the question.

"Where would he keep what I can't read?"

"His room," she said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She had been called through that door twelve times, once before each contract of her own. Never once for anything good. The lookout night had come with no summons at all, only a hand on her shoulder in the dark and a cloth tied over her eyes.

By daylight it was only a room. Scrubbed boards. A low desk with an abacus on it. A shelf of small, neat account books, one for each year, each spine lettered in the fine hand she knew. A tin sat on the end of the shelf with its lid pressed down.

She did not look at the tin twice.

Devan took the stool, stood up from it again, and sat on the boards with his back to the wall. He did it without a word. She had seen him sleep that way and had not asked.

Rain started on the tiles, a few fat drops and then a steady drumming.

She laid the book on the desk and turned it so the back half, the resin-ink pages, faced the wall. She did not open it. Then she unwrapped the front. The contract leaves came loose from their stitching in her hands, as she had known they would. Plain ink, in hooks and half-moons like bird tracks in wet sand.

"Daylight," she said. "Aloud. In front of you. Every word, in order, and nothing I skip without saying so."

"In order," said Devan.

She had learned this cipher at the long table, before she had a name to write in it. She found the first leaf. It was an index, in Chathu's hand, the kind a man makes when he cannot bear a thing he owns to be mislaid. Year, place, leaf. She read it aloud as it came, flat, one line after the next, and Devan listened with his head against the wall.

The first heading was *Released.*

"I'll start there," she said. "It's first in the index, and it's mine to check. Ten breaths. Then I'm yours."

He nodded once.

The leaf was short. Twelve names in a column, each with a date, and beside each a tick and the word *released* in Chathu's best script. She read them to him. Her voice stayed steady until the ninth.

"Kirri. Twelfth contract closed two years back. Released."

In the margin beside it, in a smaller hand, ran two words. *Kennel, nine.*

She stopped.

That numeral was familiar. In the dark, on the third cage from the end, she had looked at the civet's face and nothing else. But there had been a tag wired to the bars, with a figure painted on it in white. A nine. She had seen it and not read it.

"Every name has a number," she said. "Beside it. The kennel doors are numbered. I saw Kirri's last night. It's nine." She went down the column with her finger. Twelve names, twelve numbers. "Released means a door. All of them. Twelve."

Devan said nothing for a long moment. Then he said, "Twelve is the number that earns it."

"Yes."

"And they let them out into cages."

"They let them out of the contract," she said. "It's very tidy."

His jaw moved. That was all. Under her ribs a cold place she had been keeping shut for weeks gave way, and she let it. Ammu had asked after Kirri for years. She had written four times and been told Kirri was where she wished to be.

She turned the leaf over.

Below the twelve, set apart by a ruled line, stood one more name and a tally. *Thirty. Thirty-six contracts.* No tick. No *released.* Every few lines the tally had stepped up by one, and nothing was ever written beside it.

"That's Vellan's number," she said. "Thirty. He earned it three times over. He's never been let out. He's still useful." She heard how that sounded and did not soften it. "His contracts run on for leaves and leaves, after this. I'm going to turn past them. I will read them if you ask. Not now."

"Not now," Devan said. He was looking at the name, not her.

She turned past them. They made a thick wad of paper, a man's whole working life. She did not read a line, and she felt every leaf go by under her thumb.

After them the stitching changed. A separate quire, sewn tight, headed with the hooked mark Chathu put in a corner for the accounts. *The Keeper's.* She said so aloud. Then she said, "Tell me the year. Tell me the season."

"Six years back," Devan said. "The cold month."

"Then it's here." Her mouth was dry. "Before I read it, I'll tell you how. I'll cover everything below the line I'm on, with my hand, and slide it down as I go. I won't read ahead. If I read ahead I could change it. This way I can't."

"All right," said Devan.

He did not say *I trust you.* He watched her hand.

Her left palm lay flat over the leaf, uncovering the first line. Rain came harder on the tiles. She began.

"The cold month, six years gone."

She slid her hand down.

"Kettamala, the warden's daughter, the west stair."

She heard him go still. It was not a movement. It was the stopping of one.

"My father was warden then," Devan said, to no one.

"Buyer, the Keeper."

Her hand slid.

"Hand, Thirty."

A long breath, from him or from her. She could not tell. She slid her hand one more line.

The numeral stood there in the plain cipher, as ordinary as her own hand. She knew it the way she knew the scars on her wrists. Her mouth had said it for twenty-one years before she was told to stop.

"Lookout," she read. "Seventeen."

The rain went on. She did not move her hand.

One line was left under it, and she had promised every word. She slid her palm down and read it, in a voice that did not belong to her.

"Closed. Paid in full."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Nobody spoke.

She had read it. Every word, in order, with her own hand covering the next. There was nothing to take back and nothing to soften. The honesty she had sworn to was a clean floor, and it held, and she stood on it with her hand flat on the leaf.

"Seventeen," Devan said.

"Yes."

"You."

"Yes."

He did not stand. He sat against the wall with his hands loose on his knees, and she could not read his face at all. It had gone as still as it did in the kalari when a drill ended.

"Thirty is Vellan," he said.

"Yes."

"Say what you did."

She said it plainly, because he had asked it plainly. "I was brought in blindfolded. They took it off at the foot of a stair. Carved post, a bird. I had a shuttered lamp. I was to open it twice if anyone came into the court below." She counted the next part in her head and said it anyway. "No one came. I didn't go up. I heard it. I counted to three hundred and ten. Then I was blindfolded again and walked out the way I came in. They never told me the name of the fort. They never told me the name of the girl."

"Asha," he said.

"I know that now."

She stopped. There was nothing else that was true.

*I would have told you,* she wanted to say. It was true, and it was useless, and it sounded like every excuse ever made by a person who had stood at the foot of something. She said it anyway.

"I would have told you. If I had known which fort."

"I know," Devan said. He said it evenly, the way he gave facts. It was not forgiveness, and it was not the opposite. It was only a thing he knew.

She could not look at him. She looked at the leaf instead. The rereading had begun in her whether she wished it or not, the way a wall will show the damp through new plaster.

The parrot newel, smooth as a river stone under her palm the morning Rema came. The eleventh step that rang hollow under her boot and made her stop and breathe. Her long way round, eighty-one steps by the east stair, so that her feet would not pass that post. Janu, keeping ten paces from the foot of the stair without knowing she did it. And herself, a bride on her second day, walking wide of the bird without stating a reason. She had thought it a nerve in her. It had been memory, only without a name.

She had held a lamp at the foot of his sister's death. On the first night in his house, she had been afraid of the very place.

And he had asked her once, in a lamplit room, why she took the turn on the eleventh step. *I don't know,* she had said. It was true. She had known only how her body walked.

"The shutters," she said, before she could stop. "Everything you did. You closed them for me, and I thought it was —" She shook her head. The words were no use to either of them.

"Leave it," said Devan quietly.

She left it.

The only thing left to her hands was to turn the pages. The Keeper's quire ended. Behind it lay one more, the last in the front half, and it was not sewn like the rest. It was stitched in green thread, tight and even, round all four edges. Over the stitching, pressed into a lump of wax the colour of new leaves, a bird sat with its wings spread.

A sunbird. She knew that bird from a wedding gift and a cut seal in a man's sleeve.

*The queen's.* The hook mark in the corner of the first page was the same hook. She did not need to read a word to know what the Keeper's money had been for, or whose hand was above his.

She slid her thumbnail under the edge of the wax.

"Leave it whole," Devan said.

Her thumb stopped.

"It says who sealed it. If we break it, it says nothing, and anyone can say we wrote what we found." He still sat against the wall. His voice was low and exact. "Rema can break it. The high court can. Not us."

"You don't want to know what's in it?"

"I want to know." His hands went flat on his knees and stayed there. "Leave it whole."

She took her thumb from the wax.

He got up. It cost him; she saw the welts pull across his back under the stiff shirt. He crossed to the desk and took the leaves from her one at a time. He tapped their edges true against the wood, the way he squared the gate roll at a change of watch, and set them down on the black cover with both hands. Then he stood a moment with his palms flat on either side.

"I need to walk," he said.

"Devan."

"Not far." He did not look at her. "I'll be inside the wall."

"It's raining."

"Yes."

His cloak hung on the peg by the door, where he had put it that morning. He went past it. The door opened on grey water coming down in sheets, and he walked out into it with his back straight as a gatepost. She watched the dark spread through the back of his shirt, across the claw lines she had given him.

Following him was out of the question. She had learned that much about him. A man who answers questions with facts and does kind things unmentioned will also ask, once, for the thing he needs, and say no more about it.

She counted his boots across the yard. Eleven steps. Twelve. Then the rain took the sound, and the door stood open on the empty court.

Neela stood in Chathu's room with the book, and the green seal, and the tin on the shelf, and laid her palm flat on the leaf again. Paper. It was only paper. It said, in a clerk's tidy cipher, what she was.

# Chapter 17: The Broken Beak

The rain made the yard a bad place to be fast, so Devan went slow.

He stood in the middle of the Resin House training ground with the mud sucking at his bare feet. Eight men with spears stood at the gate and kept their faces to it, as he had told them. He raised his right hand to the level of his eyes. He held it there until it stopped trembling, and then he started the first form.

It was the drill he had made for himself at twenty-five, the winter after the ford. Open hand, slow. Palm out, turn, drop. Two fingers to the post and stop a hair short of the wood. Never touch it. A hundred times, until the hand did what it was told and nothing more.

The welts across his back stretched at every reach. Four lines, shoulder to hip, and the fourth burned like a coal. They were hers. She had earned them under a bear, and he wore them in the rain like a borrowed coat.

Palm. Turn. Drop.

The post was grey pala wood, scarred by years of other people's fists. He set his two fingers an inch from the notch below its crown, where a man's ear would be.

He had put them in that notch once. The captain at the lower ford had knelt in the ash of a burned village, with his hands open and his helmet off, and said a word in his own tongue that Devan did not know. Muthu had been holding his own opened arm. There had been a knife-cut already in Devan's ribs, from before the kneeling. Two fingers, one stroke, and the man had folded like a coat dropped from a peg. Devan was twenty-four. He had not slept properly since.

Last night he had not needed fingers. A pry bar, an old seam in a shoulder, a roar. He had known whose shoulder it was before the iron landed.

That was what he made himself look at, standing in the rain with his hand an inch from the wood. He had not hated the swing. He had waited for the arms to come down so that the blow would be fair, and it had felt like a locked door giving. He knew now he could do it again. Not as a man in a fury. As a man with a tally, who had found the last line.

Palm. Turn. Drop.

His hand shook on the fortieth stroke, and he started the count over.

*If you're reading this, I was right, and it's ugly. Say it anyway. The truth protects whoever finds it.*

The note lay in a carved box under his bed at Kettamala. He had read it so often the creases had gone soft. Asha had written it knowing she might not come back to read it herself. *Say it anyway.*

So he could say it. He could take the Keeper's quire to Rema on the day of the assize, and the leaf of Asha's death, and read it out in the hall with the court at his back. *Hand, Thirty. Lookout, Seventeen.* The court would ask who Seventeen was. The crown already held her name on a closing writ. It would not take a clerk long.

Or he could cut one line from the leaf and say nothing. That was the other road, and it was open and well-lit. He looked at it as he had looked at the notch in the post. He would not take it. He would not lie to Rema, and he would not lie to his wife by keeping quiet, and he could see no way to do neither without breaking something. Both were true. Both pulled.

Palm. Turn. Drop.

The rain came down harder, and his fingers stopped a hair short of the wood. He held them there and breathed.

"You'll take cold."

He did not turn. He had heard her on the step of the long shed, the way she set her left foot down a little short because of the claw lines. He felt it as well, a small echo along his own side.

"I'm wet already," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Neela came across the yard with a grey wrap pulled over her head. She stopped at the edge of the drill ground, where the beaten earth gave way to puddles. She was too far to touch and too near to ignore. He had watched her keep that exact distance from a cornered man once, in a lane in Kodiyur, in a story she had told him. She kept it now with him.

"I'm not going to say I'm sorry for the House," she said.

He lowered his hand.

"It isn't mine to give. You'd know it for a cheap coin if I did." Her voice was still rough from the turning. "I won't say sorry for the stair either, not yet. I don't know yet what I owe there. When I do, I'll say it, and it will be the right size."

It was an honest thing to say. He noticed that it was honest, and noticed he could not lean on it. He stood in the mud and let the rain run into his collar.

"What do you need?" she asked.

Not *are you angry.* Not *forgive me.* He had expected one of those, and he had had an answer ready for each. For this one he had nothing.

"Can you look at me?" she said. "I'm only asking. If it's no, say no, and I'll talk to your shoulder."

He had been looking at the post. He turned his head and looked at her, and that was a mistake.

She stood in her wet wrap with her left arm clamped against her flank. Her hair dripped off her chin. Her thumbnail dragged along her forefinger and stopped, and dragged, and stopped. She looked like what she was, a woman who had read her own name aloud as the lookout at his sister's death, and then waited, with her hand on the page, to be sentenced.

He did not know the answer. He knew only that one true answer would be cruel and the other would be a lie.

"I won't answer that," he said.

She took it. He watched it land and watched her not flinch. Nothing in her face closed, and he was glad of it and sorry for it at once.

"All right," she said.

"I'm not leaving," Devan said. He said it quickly, before she could build anything on the silence. "I am not going back to the fort without you. I'm not going to the horses. I'll be in this yard, or the gate, or across the sill of whatever room you sleep in. I need a day. One."

"A day to do what?"

"To not say anything I can't take back."

She was quiet for a time. The rain ticked on the hood of her wrap.

"Have you eaten?" she said.

"No."

"Nor have I. They gave me rice with some kind of tamarind in it. It was too sour." She turned toward the long shed, and then said, still not looking at him, "The master's room has a mat behind the desk. I don't want it. I'll take the floor by the shelves."

"I'll have it moved to the wall. Away from the door."

She did look at him then, a short look, sharp. He had given her the number of doors in the room without being asked. He had guessed it from the way she counted them. She said nothing, and he did not wait for her to.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He did small things that day, because he could do those with his hands.

Bhaskaran got his orders first: leave both ends of the passage open, front and rear, and put a man at neither. Devan found a brass pot in the cook-house and filled it with water from the rain barrel, and set it by the mat with the spout toward the left, because she reached with that hand when she woke. He sent a boy to the sheds for the clay pot of salve the otter-girl Pavi had offered, the green one that smelled of scorched tamarind, and left it on the desk without a word. He did not stay to see her pick it up.

He stood the afternoon watch himself at the gate, with the spearmen, and let the mud take the knees of his breeches.

Forty came past him in ones and twos. They went to the sheds, the kennels, the cook-house. They did not look at him, and he did not look for their names. Once, at the kennel end, a small man came out carrying a bucket of water for the cages. He looked at Devan's bare, shaking hand, nodded as if he understood something, and went on. Devan watched the bucket go and thought: *No one told him to.*

Nobody had asked him anything about the book. They did not ask where it was. He understood then that they did not need to. They knew the room, and the door, and who had gone in.

At the dusk change he walked through the wet to the long passage behind the master's room. He did not go in. He stood where the light from her lamp fell through the door.

Neela was at the desk, in a dry wrap, with the green salve open beside her. Her sleeves were rolled and she was binding her left wrist, as she did when she wanted her hands busy. She had not touched the resin. The flask sat corked on the shelf, next to the tin she had refused to look at. The sun was gone below the wall, and she had not so much as glanced at the window.

It was dusk and she was a woman. She had chosen that, and he understood it was not for his sake. It was a night she wanted to spend as herself.

He reached into his belt pouch and took out the pebble.

It was Kannan's, small and white and cold. Gopan had taught him at sixteen to keep one in his cheek on long cistern watches, so that a man falling asleep could not shape a clean word. He had not needed it for any watch tonight. It was not for the dark. Nothing in the dusk was going to cage her, not with her own name already in his hearing.

It was for his mouth. He was tired and hurt and angrier than he could place. Words were a thing he did badly when they came quickly. Behind the white stone they came slowly, and a sentence that comes slowly can be stopped before it cuts.

He put it between his teeth and the soft of his cheek and felt it settle.

She looked up.

At once her eyes went to his jaw, then to the lamp, then to the unstoppered window. She had counted his sleeping breath, and she counted this too. He watched her work it out. Her fingers stopped on the wrapping.

"I took no resin," she said.

"Mm."

It came out as nothing, a hum, and he almost laughed. It was a poor laugh and he kept it behind the stone.

Her face went through three things. Her lips parted, and he thought she would say *you don't need it* or *take it out.* Then she looked at his hands, at the knuckles skinned from a drill that had touched nothing, and her mouth closed again.

"All right," Neela said quietly. She went back to the wrapping.

He stood in the doorway until the lamp burned low, and neither of them said another word.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He slept across the sill.

Bhaskaran's spare blanket went lengthwise over the threshold of the master's room, from jamb to jamb, and Devan lay on it on his side with his spine against the wall. His sword lay along his front, hilt toward the room. The floor was cold, and the claw lines pulled at every breath. He lay still.

Through the open door he could hear her. She was not asleep. He knew it by the tiny things, a rustle, the soft knock of a nail against wood, the count of her breathing. In the third hour she turned over, and the pot of water clinked against the floor, and she drank. Then she lay still for a long time, and he knew she was watching the ceiling.

He did not speak. The pebble lay against his cheek, and he thought about Asha.

*Say it anyway.*

He would. He had not decided how, or in what order, or to whom first. But there was a thing he had been taught at the border school and had never once doubted, and it was this: a decision made on one page is a decision made in the dark. He had a leaf that said *Lookout, Seventeen.* He knew what she had done in one night, one lamp, three hundred and ten counts. He did not know the other ten times, or the faces, or what she had been told. He would not judge a woman, or a court, or himself, by one leaf.

Rain eased. Somewhere in the yard a hound coughed, and a cistern chain that wasn't there clanked in his head on the hour.

He slept at last, near dawn, and woke to grey light and the creak of a board.

She was sitting up on the mat, with her arms around her knees. She was dressed, and she had been awake a long time. The black book lay on the desk with the leaves squared on top of it, as he had left them.

He took the pebble out of his cheek. His jaw ached. He held it in his palm, and it was warm now.

"Neela."

"I'm here."

"I want the second contract." His voice was rough with the night and came out clean. "And the third."

She did not answer for a moment.

"Not Vellan's," Devan said. "Yours. The next, in the order you took them. Then the fourth, and every one after, to the eleventh. I want all eleven before I decide anything."

"Decide what?"

"I don't know yet." It was the truest thing he had said since the yard. "That's why I want them."

Something went across her face that he could not read, so he let it go. She looked at the book and then at him on the threshold, where he had slept across the one door she could not otherwise count.

"They're not like the first," she said. "The first I told you. I was sick in a drain and ate after. The rest —" She stopped. "I didn't get sick after the rest."

"I know," Devan said.

"You don't."

"No. I want to."

She was quiet. Then she rose, and crossed the room, and stood with one hand on the cover of the black book. He sat up against the jamb, with the blanket round his knees and the pebble in his fist, and watched her draw out the loose leaves one by one.

"Daylight," she said.

"It's nearly daylight."

She turned the first leaf toward the window, where the grey was thinning to white, and drew a breath. He sat very still, and waited to hear what else she had been.

# Chapter 18: Forty Names

She told him the second contract at first light, with the book open on the desk and her hand covering the line below.

"A magistrate in a river town. Bad knee. He sold verdicts, and somebody who couldn't afford the price wanted him quiet. He took the stair slowly, so I waited on the landing." She slid her palm down. "One strike. He looked surprised. The buyer paid in full."

Devan sat against the jamb with his arms across his knees. He did not nod, and he held her gaze.

"The third."

"Two sisters in a coast house. They owned a share of a boat that someone else wanted. I went in by the cook's door." She read the leaf again, because she had promised every word. "Twelve nights I watched them before I went. The elder one kept bees."

That was the fact she had not meant to say, and it was out. She went on.

She gave him the fourth, a boy on a river barge, and then the buyer, who had paid for the boy and been paid for in turn two years later. She gave him the sixth through the ninth. Some had names. Some had only a trade and a town. Her voice stayed flat, because flat was the only way she knew to carry weight without spilling it. She had once seen a girl carry a full water pot the length of the yard by never looking down.

The rain stopped at midmorning. The tiles dripped. Pavi passed the door with a basket of tapped bark on her hip, the old-broken hand curled round the rim, and Neela lost a line and found it again.

By the eleventh leaf Devan had said four words in four hours. *Go on. And then?* Each time, she had gone on.

She shut the book. The room smelled of wet laterite and the bitter edge of scorched tamarind. She put both hands flat on the cover.

"Now I'll argue it," she said.

"Argue it."

"I was twenty-one." She kept her eyes on the cover. "I was brought blindfolded. I didn't pick the fort or the night. I didn't pick the girl. Chathu chose. He took the money and wrote the leaf. Vellan struck. All I did was hold a lamp and open it twice if anyone came, and no one came." She heard her own voice rise a little and brought it down. "If a lamp-holder is guilty, so is the lamp-maker, and the road, and the stair. I won't carry what was put in my hands in the dark. It isn't mine."

It was a good argument. She had made it to herself on the east stair at night for a week, in the voice of someone she would have believed.

"I know," Devan said.

She waited for the rest. He let the silence sit.

"I know it isn't yours the way the strike was Vellan's," he said. His voice was even, the way he gave facts. "I have read the leaf. You've read it to me twice. You were a child on a stair, and a man had your eyes covered." He turned his hand over on his knee. "She's still dead."

Neela opened her mouth. There was an answer in it, and the answer was the argument again, with the same words in a different order.

She shut her mouth.

He did not press. He rose, slowly, with a hand on the doorframe, and stood a moment with his back stiff under the dried shirt. "I'll be at the gate," he said.

She watched him go. Her hand had tightened on the cover until the edge of the board bit her palm. It had not been an accusation. She could not put that down.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The forty were waiting in the yard, as if someone had called a count.

Nobody had. They stood in their rows on the churned mud, hands at their sides, faces turned toward the master's door. They had stood that way when Chathu came out to read the day's work. She walked into the middle of the ground and watched their spines straighten, and she stopped there.

"I'm going to ask your names," she said. "All of you. One at a time. Say what you answer to."

A girl in the front row said, "Twenty-two."

"That's a number. What did you answer to before?"

The girl looked at her. "Before what?"

It went down the rows like that. Most gave a number. A few gave a name, and it was a shed name or a nickname, *Sparrow* or *Long Mani*, the kind one child gives another across a sleeping mat. A thin boy of about fifteen gave her three, one after another, and said, "Whichever you like." Pavi, in the first row, her old-broken hand curled against her hip, said only, "Pavi. Because you braided my hair."

Neela wrote them down. She had no slate, so she took a plank from the carpentry shed and a stub of lamp-black and wrote each name down the board with the number beside it. The letters went crooked. Nobody told her so. Forty heads stayed turned to her the whole time, like a field following the sun.

When she finished, she had thirty-one names that meant anything and nine blanks. She stood a long while over the blanks.

The back half of the book lay open on a mat beside her, with Pavi guarding it. It was the resin-ink pages, in Chathu's careful hand. Each leaf had a slip stitched to its corner, thick paper, a different hand on each. She turned them one by one, careful not to read the entries. She looked only at the slips.

Names. Real ones. A village, a river, a father's trade. *Thulasi, daughter of a salt-boiler, Pazhayangadi.* *Kumaran, called Kuttu, a ferryman's son.* The slip behind Twenty-two said a name in four syllables she had never heard anyone speak. It was lovely.

The girl had come up behind her. She said, very quietly, "Does it say mine?"

Neela put her thumb over the slip.

She knew the rule. The slip was fastened to the page, and the page was the leash. To hand the page back, she had to read the entry aloud, by daylight, in the girl's hearing, and anyone standing near would hear what shape the girl took. To burn it was to burn the slip with it, and the name would be ash.

But the name was not the page. It was a few words on thick paper. She could bend and say it into the girl's ear, here, now, and the entry would stay shut under her hand.

She saw that. Then she saw what came after. A girl with a name would want the rest. She would ask which page was hers, and why Neela held it, and she would ask in front of thirty-nine others. Or she would hear *Pazhayangadi* in her own mouth and think of a road, and run for it before the groves were held, past the hunters in the wood. Neela weighed it against a girl's face and chose.

It was a Chathu kind of choice, made for someone else's good while she watched. She made it anyway.

"Yes," she said. "It does."

"What is it?"
