"I can't give it to you yet." She heard how thin it sounded. It sounded like a tin of sweets. "When it's safe, I'll hand it to you whole."

The girl searched her face. Then she bowed, a small and perfect dip from the waist, and said, "Yes, Master Seventeen."

"Not —" Neela said, and the girl was already gone.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She told Devan her reasons in the gate passage, with the rain-dark wall at her back. She told him fast, as if speed would excuse it.

"Burning a page frees her and marks her rogue. A rogue is hunted by the guild and the crown. Forty rogues in a yard is a hunt with a map." She counted on her fingers. "Handing a page back means reading the entry aloud, so the room hears every shape. I can't do that to forty people in one yard with Chathu's hunters in the wood. They'd hear it. Someone would say a word in a fever or a quarrel, and a girl would be a bear in a kennel by morning."

"Rema is a day's ride," Devan said.

"Rema is a crown officer. She'd take the book to a bench. The bench would take the pages." She was not sure whether she believed it. She believed it enough to say it. "The groves have to be held. Without resin, the ache takes half of them in a month. Without a watch, Vellan walks in at dusk."

"Yes," said Devan. "Those are all true."

"Then I hold the pages. Not for me. Until it's safe."

He studied her for a time. He did not say what he thought.

"If Rema asks who these forty are," she said, "they're the Resin House. I vouch for them, under my own name. A house with a master isn't rogue. The crown can hunt a rogue but not a vouched house."

"A vouched house has a master," Devan said. "The crown will write your name beside forty."

It was a fact, set down flat, the way he set down all of them. She felt it land.

"Then it writes it," she said. "Forty rogues have no name at all. Mine is already on a crown writ. Let it be beside theirs, where I can see what it costs."

"It will cost more than that."

"I know." She held his eyes. "Say it again when it does."

He took that, as he took everything, and set it somewhere she could not see. "Then do it," he said. "I'll hold the gate."

She did it.

It took one morning. She sent six to the groves with spears from the Kettamala men's spare stack, in pairs, with a rule that no pair stood where the wind carried scent to the wood. She sent four more to the wall. She told the cook-house to feed all forty twice a day, whatever was left of the stores, and the cook-house did. She said the kennels were to be cleaned, the straw burned, every cage opened at the front and scrubbed, and eleven people took buckets and went and did it. Nobody asked how many buckets. Nobody asked why.

She had expected to argue. She had braced her shoulders for it, the way she did at a gate.

It was easy. She stood in the sun with her hands empty, and she could not stop turning it over. She said a sentence, and the yard moved. She had never in her life said a sentence that large.

At noon she said, "Someone should wash Kirri," and two girls went quietly to the third cage from the end, one with a rag and one with a basin and a cake of coarse soap. They talked to the civet in low voices. The civet, pressed against the bars, stayed very still and let them wash her face. The silver anklet with the bells cut off lay dull against one thin paw. Neela watched from the end of the aisle, and her chest hurt, and she let it.

*It is not a cage if the door is open*, she told herself. But the doors were not open. She had written no sentence for that.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Devan's horse stood saddled at the gate by the second hour past noon. He had split the Kettamala men, two to go with him, six to stay under Bhaskaran. He told the six their orders, and Bhaskaran took his with a face she could not read and a quick, almost privately bitter nod. Devan did not look at the forty. He looked at her.

"Rema will be at the fort by now, asking where the warden rides," he said. "I'll tell her the truth. Not all of it."

"Tell her what you can."

"I will." He pulled the strap of the girth. His hands were steadier than they had been. "Neela."

"Yes."

He didn't turn from the horse. "Chathu thought the ledger was mercy too."

He said it as he had put the brass pot by her mat, with the spout turned toward her left hand. If he had said it hard, she could have answered hard.

She stood very still. The wind moved the loose end of her wrap.

"I'm not Chathu," she said.

"No."

"I haven't kept a tin of sweets for anyone."

"No," Devan said. He put his foot in the stirrup and rose into the saddle with a small tightening about the mouth, where the welts pulled. He gathered the reins. Then he looked down at her, steady. "I didn't say you were. I said what he thought."

He rode out through the gate at a walk. The two went after him, mud flying behind. She watched until the wood closed over the last of the Kettamala coats.

Under her ribs the claw lines pulled. They would pull for days, and he would feel each pull on his back all the way up the wood road. She had not asked him whether it was a comfort or an insult to be so close.

*I'm not Chathu.* She said it again, inside, and it came out the same and meant a little less.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dusk found her in the master's room, which was not a thing she had decided. The mat was by the wall, as Devan had moved it. The desk held the black book, and the loose contract leaves, and the plank of crooked names propped against the shelf. The tin was still on the end of the shelf. She had not looked at it twice.

She sat with her back to the wall and counted the doors. One. The hearth, if it counted, was two.

The cook-house had sent bread. A bowl of lentils steamed beside it. Somewhere beyond the wall the hounds were settling, and the wind off the groves brought the smell of tapped bark, bitter and sweet. Forty people slept inside that wall because of what she had said. She turned the thought over like a coin and found no obvious flaw in it.

A knock came at the frame, light, two knuckles.

"Come," she said.

The man who came in was older than anyone she had counted that morning. He was forty-one, he had told her at the names, in a voice with no hurry in it. Thilak. He had been a tapper before he was a member, he said, and a member for twenty-two years. His hands were scarred past the wrists. He carried a brass tray in both of them, the way a man carries something he is afraid to spill, and on it stood a small covered cup.

"Your tea, Master," he said.

He set it on the desk, beside the black book, without looking at the book. Steam rose from under the lid, sharp with ginger and something earthy that she knew from the long table. It was Chathu's blend. She would have known it blind.

She waited for the edge in his voice, a tilt that would let her say *don't*. There was none. He said it as he might have said *good evening*. He believed it, plainly and without effort, and so did the room.

Her mouth was open to correct him. She had the whole sentence ready: *Neela. It's Neela. Please.*

She looked at the cup.

"Thank you, Thilak," she said.

He bowed, small and exact, and went out, and drew the door half shut behind him as the master liked it.

She sat for a while without touching the tea. Then she lifted the lid and drank, and it was hot and sweet and tasted of every night of her childhood. She set the cup down beside the black book, and did not know how long she had been sitting there with her mouth still shaped around her own name.

# Chapter 19: Assize Weather

Rema asked where the warden had been before he had the mud off his boots.

She sat in his father's chair in the hall with the tally chest at her elbow and a clean leaf under her pen. She was fifty-eight, with iron-grey hair under a plain white cloth. Devan had never seen her hurry, and he had never seen her late.

"Where does a warden ride when he leaves at first light with his wife and comes back four days later without her?"

He stood on the flags with a night and a morning of road in his legs and the welts across his back dragging at his shirt. Janu had pushed a rice ball into his hand in the passage. He had not been able to eat it, and he held it still.

"The Resin House, in the Blackbark Wood. A day's ride. I stayed two nights."

"Why?"

"The guild's ledger was there. I have seen it."

"Seen it." Rema wrote a word. "And it is now where?"

"Where it is safe. My wife holds the House. The contract leaves will come to your hands when they can be carried without losing them."

"Holds it against whom?"

"The men who ran it."

The pen stopped. "Is there anything in that book touching Kettamala?"

It was the question he had walked the whole road to meet. The Keeper's quire lay in a black book in a room he had slept outside. A leaf in it said *Hand, Thirty. Lookout, Seventeen.* Rema's court would ask who Seventeen was, and he had no answer yet that would not hand a crown clerk his wife.

"I won't answer that," he said.

Rema laid the pen down. She did it gently, the way she did everything. A woman who raised her voice would have been easier.

"A court takes that once, as a courtesy," she said. "Twice, as a habit. The third time it writes it down as an answer, and not the answer you would choose. I am not threatening you. I am telling you how the roll reads."

"I understand."

"I hope so. The assize sits at lamp-lighting. I will swear your wife as household witness to the closing writ, as I said I would. She will need to be here."

"She will come."

"When?"

He opened his mouth and found he did not know. He had said *one day* in the rain, and he had meant it as a thing he took for himself. He had not thought what it would cost the people waiting on the road behind him.

"As soon as she can," he said, and it was true, and it was thin, and Rema wrote it down.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The refusal came in the yard an hour later, and it was not loud.

He had given the order at the stable door, to the duty corporal, a plain one. A patrol of six to walk the lower narrows to the shelf and back before dark. Somebody had camped on the lip above it, with bows and a bronze mirror, and no one yet knew who waited beyond the cut. The mirror had flashed a count to somebody. He wanted to know who.

Six men came out of the barracks and stopped in the middle of the yard.

Corporal Kandan stood in front of them. He was past thirty, with a scar through one brow, and he had been Raiu's comrade. He had his spear grounded and his cap in his other hand, which was a courtesy, and the courtesy was how Devan knew it was serious.

"The corporal begs the warden's pardon," Kandan said. "The men won't walk the narrows."

Fourteen others had drifted to the barracks door to watch. Someone on the wall had stopped moving. Devan heard the cistern chain clank the hour and let it finish.

"Say why."

"The warden bowed to a green sash while the Keeper's neck was on the block." Kandan's voice did not shake. He had practised it. "The warden rode out at first light with his wife to the house that sent the thing that killed Raiu. We've heard where that house is." He drew a breath. "We'll walk for the fort. We don't know that this is the fort."

It was not an unreasonable thing to think. Devan would have thought it himself. A man who bows and rides off and will not say why is a man to be watched.

He had a hundred ways to answer. He could put the six on the wall in irons for the night and send for Unni's gate watch to hold them. He could say *I order it*, and the order would hold, because he was warden and the roll said so. He could close his hand and take Kandan by the collar, once, in front of everyone, and every man in the yard would walk by sundown.

He knew exactly how it would be done. That was the trouble with eight years of an open hand. It had not forgotten anything.

He opened it, and showed them.

"I bowed to Rema because she held a warrant," he said. "I let the Keeper die because I could not stop it without breaking the pass. I went to the Resin House because the men who killed Raiu keep their books there, and I wanted them in my hand and not theirs. I'll tell you no more than that, and you may hate me for it."

Nobody moved.

"If I'm wrong, the court will say so. It sits tonight, and the corporal's statement is in front of it. Rema has read your words and I have not asked to see them. Until the court says it, walk."

"And if you're not wrong?" Kandan said. "Who tells the next man's mother?"

It landed where it was aimed. Devan took it.

"I do," he said. "Every one. Raman's widow, and the next man's mother, and the one after. Not you."

Kandan's scarred brow twitched. Devan went on, without lifting his voice.

"This is what it costs, and I'll name it. It costs you a cold walk to a place where a man of ours died single file. It costs me your good opinion, and I have little enough that I notice the loss. If I could do it myself I would. I can't be at the narrows and the gate and the court." He lowered his hand to his side. "I could put you in irons. I haven't, and I won't. I've struck no one in this yard in eight years, and I'd sooner keep it so. I would rather you walked because you chose to."

The silence went on. A langur barked once on the cliff and was still.

Kandan looked at the open hand, then at the others. One of the six, young, a boy with a cowlick like Raiu's, shifted his feet.

"Six," said Kandan, to the boy and not to Devan. "Water bags. Lamps for the shelf." He put his cap on. He did not salute, and he did not look at Devan again. But they walked.

Devan stood in the empty yard with his hand still open. It was shaking again, a little. He closed it, and the welts pulled across his back as though someone had drawn a thumbnail down them.

Neela would have said something dry about it. He found he was composing her answer, and stopped.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Muthu was in the gate chamber over the padippura with the post chest open beside him. He rose at once, laughing before he was fully upright.

"Warden. You look like a wet mule. I have kept your fort for four days and nobody has died of it, which I count as a triumph. Kandan's six are at the narrows, I take it, because I heard a certain corporal's voice go up."

"They are at the narrows."

"Good. Good man, Kandan. Stubborn. It's the mother." Muthu rubbed his hands. "The chest is as you left it. Nothing went down the pass and nothing came up. Lady Rema asked to read the log and I let her. She read every line, and it took a while."

Devan took the brass post-key from the cord at his own neck. He had worn it since the day he took it from Muthu's belt, with a grey packet broken open on the long table and a captain who had written to Kodiyur every month for six years. He held it out across his palm.

Muthu looked at it.

The laugh was gone. It went all at once, the way a lamp goes when the oil is out. Devan had seen it go in the kalari pit when they were boys, at the moment of a lie, and he had never forgotten what it looked like.

This was not a lie. He had settled that in the weeks since sealing day. The quiet was not Muthu telling a falsehood. It was Muthu holding his breath between two doors.

"The log's yours," Devan said. "So is the chest. I should have given it back sooner."

"You took it for a reason."

"I did. The reason has gone with the day." That was nearly true. He did not say *the reason is a slip you carried in to the Keeper, and told me of only in the pit.* He said instead, "You ran this fort honestly while I was gone. I'm not so sure of many things that I can afford to lose one."

Muthu closed his hand on the key. The knuckles went pale.

"If anything is ever put in your hand that you can't carry," Devan said, "bring it to me first. Before you carry it anywhere."

For a long moment the big man said nothing at all. Below them in the court a mule coughed.

"Aye," Muthu said, very quietly, and that was all.

It was a risk, and Devan counted it. If Muthu wrote tonight, the household would learn within a week that the warden had gone to the Resin House and left his wife there. That was the price of the key. He paid it open-handed, and went down the gate stair without looking back to see which door Muthu chose.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Eravi caught him in the passage by the dispensary, stooped, with a stained apron tied over his shirt and his spectacles pushed up into his hair.

"Warden, a favour. A small one." He held up both hands as if Devan might refuse before hearing it. "I'm short of willow bark and the poppy, and the pass apothecary's stock is damp since the rains. There is a tapper I know up the wood road. Honest man, keeps a dry store. I'd ride up tomorrow at first light, back in two days."

"The wood road," Devan said.

"Only as far as the first clearing. I wouldn't go near the House, of course, not with all that's —" Eravi waved a hand. "I'm too old to be caught up in anyone's war."

His cuff brushed Devan's wrist. It smelled of green salve, scorched tamarind and pine sap, the smell that had followed Fourth Guard's pot into every room Neela slept in. Eravi's own receipt, probably, or near enough. Devan registered it as one registers weather.

Half his mind was in the hall and the other half on a road a day's ride north. A hurt woman's name had been lying in his chest since noon like a stone in a boot, and he could not find where it pressed.

"Go," he said. "Take Sankaran as far as the ford."

"Kind of you. I'll be back before you've noticed." Eravi smiled and bobbed, and went into his dispensary and shut the door.

Devan walked on. It did not occur to him until much later that he had not asked the tapper's name.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The assize sat at lamp-lighting, as Rema had said.

She had the hall set out with trestles and a green cloth. Janu had polished the eight pillars, because Janu cleaned when she was afraid. Rema sat at the head with the tally chest, and the clerk's chair was empty beside her. Devan took the warden's stool at her left hand. He had washed and eaten at last, under orders. His wife's seat, a wooden bench at the foot of the table, stayed bare.

Rema read Kandan's statement into the roll. *The bride has learned the name of every man's mother. The warden bowed.* She read it level, with no commentary. Then she looked at him over the page.

"The corporal says you bowed. Did you?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Because the writ had your hand on it, and the Keeper was your prisoner. I had no right to stop you and no wish to be seen trying." He kept his voice level. "I thought I had a better use for the pass."

"Is that the whole reason?"

"It is the one I'll give."

Her mouth moved very slightly. "That is two," she said, and wrote.

The lamps burned lower. A clerk's boy brought the wine. Rema called the first witness, Unni, to speak to the sealing day, and the young man began in the careful voice of one who wanted the whole hall to forgive him for being there.

Heat came across Devan's ribs without warning.

It began under the left arm and ran across and down, shallow and quick, as though a hot wire had been drawn along the old seam from the ford. He did not move. His breath stopped on the inbreath and then he made it go on. Under the shirt the skin rose in a long welt, tender at the edges, and the old white seam, where the knife had gone in years ago, burned beneath it like a second line.

*Neela.*

It was not a thought. It was a sentence his body said by itself.

He put his hand flat over the place. The cloth was dry. No blood. A scrape, then, or the edge of something, or a graze. A cut so shallow it could be nothing. A cut deep enough would have taken his breath. But she had been hurt, and the House was a day's ride in the dark.

He stood.

The witness stopped. Rema's pen stopped. Somewhere in the long hall a stool scraped back.

"Warden?" Rema said.

"I ask leave of the court."

"To do what?"

"To go."

"Where?"

His hand was still pressed to his side. Rema's eyes went to it, then to his shirt, then to his face, and came back with the patient look she gave an honest ledger that did not balance.

He thought: she is too clever for this. He thought: I cannot say *the bond cuts me when she is cut*, not before a crown officer, not with a clerk's boy at the door. He thought of saying no, and of saying yes, and found neither would serve.

"I won't answer that," he said.

"That is the third time today," Rema said. She did not raise her voice. "I am keeping count."

"I know."

"The court may not grant leave. There is no blood on your shirt, Warden, and I am not blind."

"Then the court will say so," Devan said, "and I will hear it from the road."

He put his stool under the table. Nobody stopped him. At the doorway Muthu was on his feet with half his face open, and Devan shook his head before the man could speak.

"Hold the gate," he said. "Nobody follows."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He did not take a lamp. He took the grey mare, because she knew the wood road in the dark, and he took no one, because no one else could be told why.

The padippura swung wide for him on Unni's word. The night watch did not ask where. The cistern chain clanked behind him at the hour, and then the dark came down on the road, and the smell of wet laterite, and the langurs fell silent on the cliff as he passed.

He was not ready. He had asked for a day in the rain, and he had meant it. He had not decided what he owed the woman who had read *Lookout, Seventeen* and waited to be sentenced. He had not decided what the court would hear, or in what order, or from whom. The eleven leaves lay in him unsorted, and the hard thing had not settled.

None of it mattered.

The welt on his ribs was shallow. He rode as if it were mortal.

# Chapter 20: Nellimala

The arrow came out of the grey with no sound before it, and it opened her across the ribs.

Neela went down between two nightbark trunks and counted. One, for the burn along her left side. Two, for the thorn scrub on the wood side, where a bowstring had spoken once and then kept quiet. Three, for the pair at her back, Vasu and Kuttan, who were already shouting and had their spears up at nothing.

"Stay on the trees," she said. "He's gone."

He was. It was a hunter's way: one shot, then away through the scrub before the light was good enough to give him a face. She pressed a hand to her side and it came back red to the second knuckle.

It was not deep. It was the second arrow of the night. The first had come at lamp-lighting from the same wood and only scored her, a line she had wiped and forgotten. She had read it as a test of the pairs, and she had been right. Then she had left the east pair standing through the dawn change, when men have stood longest and see worst. She had been awake for a day and a night, and she had counted wrong.

"Hold this." Vasu thrust a strip of his own sleeve at her. She folded it and pushed it under her wrap.

Hooves came on the wood road below, slow and uneven. Kuttan swung his spear toward the sound.

"Down," Neela said. "That's the grey mare. She favours the off hind."

Devan came up the grove path on foot, leading her. He was mud to the thighs. The road had been running water for a day and he had ridden all night through it, and his face was the colour of the clay. He stopped ten paces off. His left hand was flat against his own ribs, in exactly the place hers was.

For a moment neither of them spoke. The grove smelled of wet bark and bitter sap.

"Show me," he said.

"It's a scrape."

"Then it's a short look."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She took him to the tapping shed, because it had a door she could see from any corner. Pavi brought warm water without being asked, and the green salve in its clay pot, and went away again with her curled hand held close to her chest.

Devan stopped at the threshold. "Which side do you want me?"

It was a strange thing to be asked. She looked at the room. The door, the shuttered window, the drying racks. "Right. So I can see the door."

He took her right. He did not ask her to strip. He lifted the hem of her wrap the width of the wound and no more, and held it there with two fingers while he worked. The water was warm because he had had it warmed. She had once told him cold water on open skin made her teeth hurt. She had said it in the dark, in the middle of something else.

"Breathe out," he said, and pressed the cloth.

It stung. Under her ribs the claw lines from the bear pulled, and her breath went thin. His hand paused.

"Go on," she said.

He went on. His shirt had ridden up where he leaned. Across his left ribs, over the old white seam from the ford, lay a raw red line that matched hers to the finger's width, a half-breath behind. His mark was cleaner than hers and did not bleed. He cleaned hers.

"That's mine," she said.

"It's ours." He turned the cloth. "I felt the first one at lamp-lighting, in front of a crown officer. This one I felt coming up the hill."

"Rema?"

"Is counting." He said nothing more about it.

She watched his hands. There were seven pale scars across the right knuckles and a thin line at the thumb. They moved with the same care he gave to hurt horses. Eleven leaves lay between them, all of them read aloud and all of them still on the desk across the yard. She had not spoken of the stair. He had not spoken of his sister. The silence sat in the shed with them like a third person who had been asked to wait outside and had not gone.

He pressed the salve on with his thumb, light as breath, and laid a clean pad over it. "Hold," he said. She held. He wound the long cloth round, twice, snug and not tight, and tied it on the side where it would not rub her arm.

"You rode all night for a scratch," she said.

"I rode all night for a woman who counts the doors in a room." He rinsed the cloth. "I wasn't asking what it was worth."

She had nothing dry to answer with. She said, "Thank you," and it came out plain.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Pavi came back to the shed door in the sun, not quite in it.

"Master. There's a thing on the gatepost."

"Neela," she said, from habit.

"Neela." Pavi tried it as if it might not hold her weight. "The hunters put it there in the night. It was still warm when I found it. Nobody saw who."

It was a flat tin, round, no longer than her hand, wrapped in a cloth against the damp. She knew it before the cloth was off. She knew the dent in the lid. Eleven times before eleven contracts it had come to her mat, and each time she had learned a little more of what it was for. A twelfth had been held out to her in the kennels, and she had not touched it.

She lifted the lid. Sweets lay in rows, dark jaggery gone glossy, sesame seeds pale on top. Whoever had shot at her in the dark had stood at that gate and set this down while her hands were busy with a wound. And the gatepost had stood unwatched while she counted wrong.

A gift is the start of a bill. She had understood that by the fifth tin. She had not guessed the bill might be tucked under the sweets.

A folded leaf of thick paper lay beneath them, and a note beside it in Chathu's fine hand.

*Child. The book is not yours to carry. Bring it home, and the account closes. Under the sweets is the first of it. The rest I keep for your coming: who sold you, and every roll I did not burn. Refuse, and Thilak walks the north tapping path at dusk, as he has for twenty-two years. The hunters know the hour. Come home. C.*

Devan had come to the door behind her. He did not read over her shoulder. He stood where she could feel him, a pace off and to her right.

She turned to Pavi first. "Kuttan goes to the north path now, and he doesn't come back without Thilak. Tell Thilak I want him under this roof before the light goes. Tell him it's about tea."

Pavi ran. Then she unfolded the leaf.

It was not her slip. It was the rest of the roll leaf her slip had been cut from. Along one edge ran the fastening-holes where it had been stitched to a page now ash, and along the other ran a torn edge, ragged and brown. Four lines stood on it. Two were in the members' cipher, which she had read aloud for days. Two were in the masters' hand, which she could not.

She read the first two with her finger beneath them.

*Intake.* And below it, *Sold at six years.*

Her mouth went dry. She knew those words. She had known them since the mud of the Resin House yard, and had told Devan in his chamber what the slip behind her page said, without being able to say the rest. The rest was in the masters' lines. Two lines of strange letters stood under the words she knew, small and tidy, and she could not make them say anything.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They went to the master's room because it had a table.

The black book lay where she had left it. Beside it, under a river stone, lay her torn slip, with half its entry gone at the tear. She laid the new leaf against it, edge to edge. The brown tear met the brown tear and closed like a key in a lock. Neither of them spoke.

"The masters' cipher is on a separate leaf," she said. "At the back of the contract leaves. I found it two nights ago. A grid of cipher letters set against plain ones, and the masters' marks below. I have not had an hour to learn it."

"Then learn it now."

She found the grid. She laid the key leaf beside Chathu's and put her finger on the first strange letter of the first line. Down the left of the grid ran the cipher letters, across the top the plain ones. She found the cipher letter, followed the row until it met the plain one, and wrote it on the back of her hand in lamp-black. One. Then the next. Her lips moved. It was slow, and it was dull, and her finger shook only once.

The first word came out as four letters. She checked it against the grid a second time, because she did not believe it.

I. N. D. U.

"Indu," she said.

Then she went on, because she had to, and the rest came out of the grid a letter at a time. *Of Nellimala. Coast side of the Monkey Pass.* And on the second line, *Village burned the second harvest.*


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It did not feel like anything. That was the first surprise. She had expected a door to open, and it was a word. It sat in her mouth like a pebble from somewhere she had never walked.

The second surprise was that the rest came after it, quietly, and fell into place.

"The goat path," she said. "Left of the split boulder. I walked it without a map." She was speaking to Devan and to the table. "I told you I couldn't say how. My feet knew it. They'd known it since before I had a number."

"Below Kettamala," he said.

"A day below, on the coast side. Nellimala." She turned the leaf over and the back was empty. "And lantana. All my life I have hated the smell of lantana. I used to cross a yard to be upwind of a hedge. The village is under it. It must be." She laughed once, short. "A hedge. It was a hedge."

"Neela."

"Indu." She said it to see if it would stay said. "Chathu gives it me now. After twenty-one years in his drawer."

Devan's face had gone still, the way it did when he was working out a cost. "He kept it until he could spend it."

"He kept it for the twelfth contract. Release meant the page burned and the birth name returned. That was the promise." She set the note on the leaf. "It was never going to come. Now he hands me the first of it. He's put my name in one pan of the scale, and the rest in the other, and Thilak on the beam." She could hear how level her voice was. "He knows what each of them weighs."

"Yes."

"Thilak brings me tea. He calls me Master and means it."

Devan did not say *I'm sorry*. He said, "What do you want to do with it?"

It was a question, and he waited for the answer as if there might be one. She looked at the black book, and at the tally book she drew from her wrap in its oilcloth. It was the size of her hand, forty leaves, and it had been in her wrap since the night they took the ledger.

"Read," she said. "First I want to read something."

She opened the key leaf's last page and laid the tally book beside it. "I've read this twice. I read the place names, and I read the sums. The marks beside them were only ink to me. They're the masters' marks, and they don't live in the receipts."

"They live in the key."

"They live in the key's last leaf." She laid her finger beneath a small inked bird with folded wings. She had passed it three times in the margin of Ittan's book and taken it for a clerk's habit. The mark leaf had a line for it. She read it slowly. "A sunbird alone. By leave."

"By whose leave?"

"The masters' receipts use it for the party that grants the passage." Her finger moved down the page. "A hook for a thing delivered. A slash for coin paid. And the bird, for permission given. Ittan copied his shorthand from ours, so his columns are ours." She opened the tally book flat. "He keeps two columns. On the left, his own passes, the extras, with the hook. Those I've read. On the right, a second column. Not every line in it carries the bird. Some do, tiny, folded, and with the key I can see where I'd missed them."

Devan sat down beside her. She did not notice at first that he was not across the table. He had pulled the stool to her left and put the book between them, and he looked at it as if it might bite.

She read down the right column. Dates, boats, a count of men, a place. *Three boats of Thurai men, the long coast road, by leave.* *Eleven boats, the lower ford, by leave.* The sums beside them were small and tidy. The oldest leaves were written in an ink gone brown, copied from some earlier book in a younger hand. She turned back toward the front and the years dropped away.

"Twenty-one years," she said. "Count with me. The roll says sold at six. I was twenty-seven this spring."

Devan counted on his fingers. "I was eleven," he said, softly, to himself.

Her finger stopped.

*Nellimala. Second harvest. Two boats of Thurai men, the coast road to the foot of the pass. By leave.*

It stood in the right column, in the brown ink, under its bird. It had been there when she read it the first time. She had read the name and gone on to the sums, because it was a name like any other, and nothing in her had so much as flinched.

She said it aloud. Devan said nothing. She read the line again, and then the roll leaf, and put them side by side so the dates touched.

"The village was burned by raiders," she said. "That's what I thought. That's what I told you."

"They did."

"By leave." Her throat was dry. "Someone gave a bird to two boats and let them up the coast road to the foot of the pass. And a little girl in Nellimala was six."

The fire spat in the hearth. Outside, someone dragged a bucket across the yard. Devan sat with his eyes fixed on the small ink bird, and she saw him go the way she had gone, one step at a time, down a road with no turning.

"The crown permits raids," he said at last. His voice was even. It was what he did with facts that cut. "Not Ittan's extras. These are the other thing. The ones he didn't sell."

"My village was in the other thing."

"Yes."

"It wasn't a failure of the border. It wasn't the cost of a bad year." She turned her hand over on the table, palm up, and looked at it. "It was agreed."

He did not touch her. He moved his own hand to the table's edge, near hers, with the palm open as it had been in the yard. She looked at it. After a moment she laid two fingers across the old scars on his knuckles, no more than that, and felt them under her skin, seven small ridges and a thin line.

He turned his hand a little, so her fingers lay in the hollow of it.

"It's the same bird," Devan said. "It's on my own death. It's on your village."

She did not answer. She left her fingers where they were.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She rose and crossed to the window. The shutter was stuck with damp. She put her shoulder to it and shoved it wide, and the sun came through in a flat white slab and lay across the ledger, the roll leaf and the open tin.

"Say it," she said.

"What?"

"The name. In daylight."

He lifted his eyes from the page.

"Indu," Devan said.

He said it carefully, as if he was afraid of putting weight on a thing so new. Her shoulders came down from where they had been since the arrow, and the sun warmed the backs of her hands. It was a name, said in the clean light with the shutter open, and the sound of it went through her and left nothing behind but the sound.

"Indu, of Nellimala," he said, and then waited.

"Again."

"Indu of Nellimala."

She closed her eyes and let it sit. When she opened them, he was still looking at her, steady, and had not moved his hand.

"Both," she said.

"Both?"

"I'm going to keep both. Indu was what the village called a girl who is gone. Neela is what I did with the years after." She breathed. "I'll answer to either. I'll not answer to Seventeen."

"Neela," Devan said. A pause, quite small. "Indu."

"Yes."

Kuttan's voice came across the yard, hoarse with running. She went to the door. Thilak stood behind him, tapper's knife still at his belt, breathing hard, bewildered, alive.

"Under this roof till I say," she told him. "Pavi will find you tea."

Then she picked up the tin. It was no longer warm. She held it a moment, the dented lid, the dark sweets in their rows, and thought of eleven tins eaten and a twelfth refused, and a man who had wept at her mat and kept her name in a drawer. *Who sold you.* *Every roll I did not burn.* There were forty women and men in this wood who did not know where they were born. Chathu had just offered her the key to forty drawers, and she was about to put it in the fire.

She crossed to the hearth and tipped the tin.

The sweets slid out in a sticky dark rush. The fire took them with a spit and a hiss. Jaggery bubbled, the sesame seeds jumped, and a thick sweet smoke rose into the flue, so rich it caught in her throat. It smelled of every night of her childhood. She stood in it and did not step back.

When the smoke thinned, she dropped the tin in after them, and then Chathu's note on top.

Behind her, Devan had not spoken. She turned. He was looking past her, at the tally book, at the small ink bird folded in the margin of a line she could not stop seeing.

"Everything I meant to carry to the high court," he said, very quietly, "has the queen's bird on it."

# Chapter 21: The Sunbird Column

Devan laid the three things in a row on the master's table, and they would not make one line.

The tally book lay open on the left. In the middle was the roll leaf with Neela's village on it, and on the right the Keeper's quire, unsewn from the ledger, with Asha's leaf on top. The shed fire had burned down to a red seam. Across the yard a bucket clanked, and Pavi laughed at something, and the sound was so ordinary it hurt.

He had been staring at the same ink bird for an hour. Neela had gone to settle Thilak under a roof with a door she could count. He had told her he would sit with the book, and she had looked at his face and left him to it.

There were two columns in Ittan's hand. He had read the left one already, the hooks. *Delivered.* Extra tokens, sold on top, to Thurai men who came up the coast road with coin that was new and silver and never handled. The right column was the bird. *By leave.* A boat, a ford, a count of men.

He put one finger on each column and made himself look at what lay between them.

The hooks were thin in the oldest leaves, a few to a page. Further on they thickened, like weeds in a field somebody had cleared and left. Ittan had not built the raids. They had been there before him, agreed, with a bird beside each. He had stood in the cleared field and planted his own.

So there were two kinds of crossing. The queen's: limited, counted, through the passes she chose, in exchange for no fleet landing. Devan had not known the shape of it until Nellimala. Now he could see the whole bargain, and he felt like a man who has walked a long time along a wall and finds it has no door. Then Ittan's: extras, uncounted, sold on top of hers and paid into his own purse for a wife's physicians.

Asha had found an extra.

He made himself say it in order, in his head, as he would have said it to a patrol. Six years ago his sister found one token whose wax was too good. She wrote *Need a second to be sure* and tied it in red thread. She was right, and it was ugly, and she meant to say so. Ittan heard. A man who sells what the crown has not sold cannot afford a warden's daughter with a slate full of names. He sent a letter to a kind old man in the Blackbark Wood, and a bear went up the west stair.

Then, eight weeks before his own wedding, Devan had written to the council that the raiders' tallies carried true wax. He had asked to see every seal register. The registers led to Ittan's box. Within days the queen's green wax had come down on a payment writ, and a contract had been opened on him.

Ittan bought Asha's death. The queen bought his.

They were two crimes, and a single hand could not have written both. It was the plainest thing he had learned in six years, and it had come out of a margin.

He sat back. His ribs ached where hers did. The dressing under his shirt was clean, and the line had faded to a pink thread, but he still felt the wire of it.

What could he prove?

He made himself list it, the way Asha used to, with a stub of chalk on her slate. Ittan's tokens: yes. Thirty-six blanks, the wax matched to Asha's token and the scouts' tally, the tally book in the Keeper's own hand. Asha's murder: the leaf in the Keeper's quire, *Buyer, the Keeper*, hand and lookout named, in the guild's own record. Rema's court could hold that. It was a conspiracy, and it had a body and a chain.

The treaty was another matter. So was the writ on his own life. Those had the sunbird on them. A warden's word would not carry them, nor a ledger from a house of killers, brought in by the man whose wife was named on a leaf. They needed the high court in full session, with the green-stitched quire opened before every bench. Rema could not do that. Rema could not even say it aloud and keep her post.

That was the case. Not the whole of it, but one he could stand behind. Yesterday he had gone to the Resin House with his sister's name in his chest like a fist. He would go home with a list.

It did not feel like less. It felt like walking on a floor.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Neela came in with the light behind her and a clay cup in each hand.

"Thilak's tea," she said. "He made me drink some first. He says no master has ever refused his, and he's not starting with a new one." She set a cup at his elbow. It was ginger and something bitter, and he drank a mouthful out of manners. "He's afraid. He won't say. He asked if I would burn the black book now."

"What did you say?"

"That I read it first." She looked at the three things on the table and then at him. "You've been busy."

"I've worked out what I can prove."

He told her. He kept it short, the way he would have briefed Muthu: the two columns, the cleared field, the thirty-six blanks. She followed the line from hooks to Asha without stopping, and he saw where it hurt her, which was the same place it hurt him. When he reached the end she put down her cup.

"And the other half?"

"Needs the high court sitting whole. The bench, the senior clerks, the other wardens. Not Rema alone." He turned the roll leaf a little, so the tear caught the light. "I want to write it down. All of it, in plain words, so a clerk can read it aloud without a key. What each leaf says, who wrote it, where it was found. And I cannot do it. I can't read the cipher, and half the ledger is in it."

She did not smile, but something eased. "You can't read it."

"No."

"You're asking me to read it to you while you write."

"I'm asking for help." The words came more easily than he had feared. "I'm no scribe either. Asha wrote my letters. I'd write *the thing* where I mean the name of it. I need someone to say it's wrong."

"I'll say it's wrong." She took the stool across from him and pulled the quire toward her. "Say it plain. 'Lookout, Seventeen' is plainer than anything you could do to it."

"I know."

"And I need something from you." She said it just as straight, and he saw what it cost. She kept her eyes on the leaf. "The groves. Hunters shot at me twice. Eight men stand on the wall of this place and I don't know yet whose they are. Bhaskaran's six can hold the pairs, but they don't know the trees, or which of the members I'd let carry a spear. I need you to stand a night with them before we leave. Tell them where the sun comes into the wood. You're the only one they'll take it from without a fight."

He looked at her. He had the fort, the court, a roll in Rema's hand with three of his refusals on it. She had forty people and a ledger and no wall of her own.

"Tonight," he said.

"You'll lose a night."

"I've lost worse." He thought about it. "I'll show Bhaskaran the east pair's blind side. You'll tell me the rest."

"Done." She drew a breath. "That's what we do, isn't it. You can't read. I can't hold a line. I ask you for a wall and you ask me for a cipher."

"It seems a fair trade."

"It's a good one." She looked, briefly, almost shy. Then she tapped Asha's leaf with one finger. "Now. Plain words."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They worked through the lamps. She read, and he wrote, and every few lines she stopped him. *Say who* and not *a hand*. *The Keeper, not the one who bought*. His letters stood in rows, large and square, a soldier's hand, and the pages grew in the lamplight. By midnight he had the first quire in order, and Ittan's tokens fell into place like a ledger of his own.

He came to the last leaf and put the pen down.

"This one," he said.

She did not look up. She knew which one.

*The cold month, six years gone. Kettamala, the warden's daughter, the west stair. Buyer, the Keeper. Hand, Thirty. Lookout, Seventeen. Closed. Paid in full.*

"I have to tell you what I'm going to do with it," Devan said. "Before I do it. I won't have you hear it in a hall."

Now she looked up. The lamp threw a gold line down one side of her face.

"The contract goes to the court whole. The lookout line goes with it. Every word. I'll not cut it, or blot it, or leave it in the quire and say nothing, because a case with a hole in it is the kind a queen's clerk drives a cart through." He kept his hand flat on the table so it would not move. "I will not strike out your number."

She was quiet for a moment. He could hear the cistern chain, faintly, in the wood's silence, and then remembered it was a sound from home and he was hearing it only in his head.

"You'll name me," she said.

"The leaf names you. It says Seventeen. Rema already holds your name from the closing writ. A clerk who can count will put the two together before the sitting is over." He did not soften it. "I could have kept it from them for a while. I'd have won you a month. And I would have built the thing I mean to bring against the queen on a lie, and she would find it."

"A month isn't nothing."

"No. It's a month of you waiting for it to break."

She turned her cup between her palms. A tendon moved in her wrist, over the pale scars.

"I'm not asking you to," he said. "I want you to know that. If you ask, I'll hear it. I won't pretend I haven't thought of it. But I've thought of it for three days, and I can't find the place where I'd strike it out and still be the man who wrote *Buyer, the Keeper* underneath."

"The crown hunts a rogue it has a name for."

"Yes."

"The queen could send her sashes for me by the next moon."

"Yes."

"You'd have that on your hands."

"I'd have it on mine," Devan said. "It will be on yours too. That's the cost. I'm not hiding it in the margin."

For a long breath she said nothing. Then she gave a short, dry, unhappy laugh.

"I spent twenty-one years being a number nobody read aloud," she said. "Now you're going to read it in a hall."

"I'm going to read it with you in the room, if you'll be there."

"I know what you'll say. It's *true*, and we need the truth whole." She put the cup down hard enough to slop it. "You're right. That's what's hateful. If it were only cruel I could argue it. Mine is a true leaf, and I held that lamp. Whoever sits on that bench ought to hear that Seventeen was there." She rubbed her eyes with the heel of her hand. "And I don't want to be read. I want to be Indu, in a village, with a hedge. I want that more than any court."

"Both of those are real."

"Both are real," she said. "I won't ask you to strike it. I'll not do that to Asha."

It was a gift, and he knew it. He felt it settle somewhere under his ribs, next to the ache that was hers.

"It isn't the same as standing beside it," he said. He could not stop himself. "I'm not asking that, either."

"No." She met his eyes. "It isn't the same. I haven't got that far yet."

"You don't have to."

"I know. I'm telling you where I am." She said it without heat, as she'd have given the count of a wall. "I won't bargain you out of it. I won't promise I'll walk into the hall with my head up when they read it. Ask me that in a day, or when it's in the lamplight."

He nodded. He wanted to say something that would make it easier, and had enough sense to know that every such thing was a small lie.

"Thank you for telling me which," he said.

"Thank you for telling me first." She gave a crooked half-smile. "You could have just done it, and let me hear it from a clerk. I'd have hated you properly."

"I'd rather you hated me properly in advance."

That got a real laugh out of her, brief, startled. It broke the edge off the room.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She walked out with him to the grove before the wall changed. The east pair stood where they had stood the night before, and he showed Bhaskaran's men the dead ground under the tallest trunks, where a bowman could kneel and not be seen from either post. Bhaskaran grunted, and moved his pair, and said nothing, which from Bhaskaran was a speech. Neela named the trees. She named three members who could be trusted with a spear and two who could not yet. Vasu counted her hands with his eyes and then looked away.

When the sky greyed, they stood together at the gate. The dressing under his shirt had gone warm, and so, he thought, had hers. The wall behind them was only a wall.

"I'll leave the six, with Vasu over them," she said. "Pavi will hold the shed. Thilak stays under a roof." She counted them off in the air, with her thumb. "And I'm riding back with you."

He had been about to ask. He closed his mouth.

"The roll leaves are packed. The ledger rides in my saddlebag, so no one else carries it." She paused. "And I'm coming to Kettamala because there's someone there I owe. Before the court, and before anyone reads a word."

He looked at her. She was watching the road, her chin level, her mouth set the way it was when she had put a number on something and would not say it twice.

He thought of a dozen names. He did not say any. There was one he did not let himself think, because then he would also have to think what he'd say to it, and she had not asked him.

"I'll saddle the mare," he said.

"The mare favours her off hind."

"I know," Devan said. "I'll walk her up the hills."

She almost smiled. Then she turned and went to fetch her cloth, and he stood at the gate in the cold with the case he had made folded in his coat. It was larger than he had expected and lighter than it should have been, because someone had read it with him.

# Chapter 22: The Hollow Step

Neela did not put the ledger down.

She had carried it up from the Blackbark Wood in her saddlebag, thumped against her hip for a day and a half of wet road. When the grey mare stopped under the padippura, she slid off with the bag still on her shoulder. Unni took the reins. She gave him her thanks and his mother's name, the salt-fish seller, and he went red to the ears.

"Rooms are aired," Unni said. "Janu says you're to eat first."

"Janu says a lot of things."

"Yes, wife of the warden." He ducked his head. "She says them louder when you're thin."

Devan had already gone to find Rema and the pit and, she suspected, a wall to put his hand against. He had looked at her once at the gate. It was the look of a man leaving a lamp lit in a window and walking away from it.

The kitchen was warm, and it smelled of coconut oil and curry leaf. Chinna sat on her stool by the hearth with her bad hip stuck out. Janu stood at the long table with her sleeves rolled, banging a lid onto a pot.

"Sit," Janu said, without turning. "Look at you. You've gone down to a lamp-wick. Two nights in a wood with men, and nobody thought to hand you a plate."

"We had rice."

"Cold rice is not rice." Janu turned, wiped her hands, and looked her over. She was sixty-one, small and square, with a brass key on a cord at her neck, and her eyes missed nothing. "Sit."

Neela sat. The saddlebag stayed on her knee, both arms over it. Janu's eyes went to the bag and away again, which was her way of asking nothing.

The plates came. Rice, ghee, fish curry, a dish of plantain, buttermilk with curry leaf floating on it. Janu scolded as she set each one down. The guests ate too little, the warden ate nothing, and the stew had been made for forty and would feed six. Then she set down another plantain.

Neela picked up a ball of rice. It was warm. She held it while the kitchen noise went on around her: Chinna's grumble, a girl scraping a pan, the iron cistern chain clanking its hour out in the yard. Her gaze fell on the key at Janu's neck.

*She holds it six years and cannot go in.* Devan had told her that, and Neela had seen the shut door at the end of the south passage.

She put the rice back on the plate.

"Not hungry," she said.

Janu stood very still. For a moment Neela thought she would be scolded properly, and wanted it. Instead Janu said, "Whatever it is will be a worse thing on an empty stomach," and put the plate where Neela could reach it. She did not push it. She stood with her arms folded, and then she went to see about the fire.

Neela sat with the book on her knee and a full plate going cold. In the end she ate three mouthfuls. They tasted like ash.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The dispensary was a low room off the north court, hung with bundles of bark and leaf. It smelled of camphor and boiled cloth, and under that of something else.

Eravi was stooped and slow. He unwound Devan's dressing from her ribs with fingers that shook a little and never slipped. He studied the graze for a long breath.

"Clean," he said. "Cleaner than I'd have done. Your husband has a gentle hand for a man who breaks people."

"He's had practice with horses."

"So I hear." He smeared salve from a clay pot. Cool, then a sting. "You'll keep this. The skin's closing well."

She watched his hands, because she watched everyone's. They were spotted, and the cuffs were stained green. His satchel sat on the stool beside her, flap open, and the smell came off it like heat off a stone. Scorched tamarind. Pine sap. Nightbark.

She kept her face as it was. There was a tapper on the wood road, he had told Devan. A man for willow bark and poppy. A man for a bad hip. There were tappers everywhere in the hills, and resin got into everything they touched, and an old physician might carry its smell home on his coat the way a cook carries onions.

She counted the bundles on the beam. Eleven. Nine of them were willow.

"How many does the House hold now?" Eravi asked, winding the cloth.

"The House?"

"Janu likes to know how many mouths." His eyes stayed on his work. "Forty, I heard. I only ask for the beds and the pots."

"Who's asking, you or Janu?"

"I ask for everyone. It's the trouble with the work." He tied the knot and patted it, kindly, as he might have patted a child's hand. "Oh, and forgive an old man, I nearly forgot. The day you read the pages back. If I knew which, I'd keep the dispensary open. When it falls, I mean. A day of that sort, there will be tears and fainting, and nobody sends for the physician until the floor is wet."

She had said that sentence to four people. Devan. Pavi. Bhaskaran in the grove, and Thilak over tea. None of them would tell a fort physician, and Devan had been at her side every hour since.

"Thank you for the dressing," Neela said. It came out level. She stood and eased her tunic down over the cloth. "It's very neat."

"It's my work."

"It is." She lifted the saddlebag onto her shoulder. At the door she looked back, once, at the old man scrubbing his hands in a basin, humming something that was not a tune. His satchel sat on the stool, its flap hanging open on the dark inside.

She walked out into the court and filed it. *How many. Which day.* Two questions, side by side, with nothing under them. She would not answer them yet, and she would not forget them.

She counted nineteen steps to the arch without meaning to.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The sun was going down behind the west wall when she came to the stair.

She avoided it. Since her first week at Kettamala she had taken the east stair, eighty-one steps by the wall-walk where the west way took forty-four, and she had climbed the west only when forced. She had told herself it was the view. She had counted the west stair once, in the dark of her own head. She had shouted from the head of it on the worst night of her life. Once she had come down it on purpose, under Rema's eyes, and her knees had known the difference.

She had touched the newel once, for Rema's benefit, and not let herself feel it.

The lower court was nearly empty. A boy was carrying water, one slow bucket at a time. Beside the first tread stood the newel, a carved parrot as long as her forearm, its beak broken short and worn smooth as a river stone.

She put her bare hand on it before she could decide not to.

The stone was cold. At her throat, where the resin scars lay in their pale ridges, there was a faint burn, the first warning of dusk. She had smeared nothing. She breathed through it, in and out, and it passed, as it did on the nights she kept her own shape. She stayed a woman. She stood a woman with her palm on the bird.

She climbed.

One, two, three. The treads were worn in the middle by feet. Pala wood in the rail, smooth under her fingers. Four, five, six. Nothing in her stirred but the count. Seven, eight, nine. The light went amber on the laterite. Ten.

Eleven rang.

It was not loud. A hollow note under her heel, like a knock on a shut door in an empty house. The drain slot ran beneath it, and the tread over it gave back the sound of the space below. She stood on it and did not move.

She had stepped on that note before. She had known it at once on the first day, and told Devan *I don't know* with her thumbnail dragging along her finger. She had said it for two months.

Eleven. She went on to forty-four and stood on the landing. The passage ran off toward the south range, dim, with a window at its end. She looked at it and did not walk it. Then she turned and went down again, counting aloud under her breath, and stopped with her hand on the newel.

The memory came the way water comes up in a well, from under.

A hand on her shoulder in the dark. The cloth over her eyes, with its smell of scorched tamarind. A walk she did not remember, some of it uphill, some of it stone. Then the cloth coming off, and the world very sharp and very black.

She had stood where she stood now. A stair rose in front of her. A brass lamp was in her hand, a shuttered one, small and warm as a held bird.

*Hold it. If anyone comes into the court, open it twice. Do not speak.*

She had held it. The thin bar of light had leaned across the bottom step and the carved bird, a parrot, with its beak broken short. She remembered thinking it looked insulted. She was twenty-one and she had been given a job, and she was good at her jobs.

Then a big man's weight went past her on the stair. Soft, for a thing that size. Up and up, a step at a time, into the dark. She had heard the eleventh tread ring under him, very faintly. She had held the lamp.

And a voice, above her. A woman's, young, quick, not frightened yet. It came down the stairwell with a laugh just fading out of it.

*Who's there?*

Neela stood below with the light in her hand.

*Who is that?*

The lamp did not shake. She had made sure of that. She had been told to hold it, and she held it, and the bar of light lay across the bird, and she said nothing.

There had been a sound, after. She had not needed to hear it twice, and she did not call it up now. Afterward she had counted. Three hundred and ten. Then the hand on her shoulder, and the cloth.

That was all of it. It was the same night she had seen in the book's lamplight, in the master's room in the wood. She had told Devan about the blindfold and the lamp and the count. She had left out the voice. She had not decided to. It had simply never come up out of the well until her hand was on the bird.

*Who's there?*

She had known the answer. Someone had stood at the bottom with a light, and the light was hers.

Neela took her hand off the newel. It left a damp print on the stone. She watched it until it dried.

"Ask me now," she said to the bird, to the stair, to a girl of twenty-two with a laugh in her voice. Her own voice came out flat and small. "Ask me who it is."

Nobody asked.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Boots crossed the court behind her.

She knew the tread. It stopped a long way off, and she was grateful for that, and angry at being grateful. When she turned, Devan stood by the well post at the edge of the lower court, twenty paces away, with a lantern unlit in his hand.

He did not come nearer. His shirt was damp from the pit, and there was a smear of red earth up one forearm. He looked at her face, then at the newel, then at her face again. She saw him understand it, all of it, without a word said. His mouth flattened. He set the lantern on the well curb.

"You're going to tell Janu," he said.

"Tonight." Her mouth was dry. "I thought I'd have a day. But she'll be along with the lamps."

"At the hour." He nodded toward the cistern. "She comes round the court at dusk. She always has."

"She stops short of the stair."

"Ten paces." His eyes didn't leave her. "Six years."

Neela folded her arms and held her own elbows. A boy crossed the court with a bucket, saw them, and went wide.

"I won't ask you to help me," she said. "I'd like that understood. I'm going to say it, and it'll be the wrong words. If you step in and say them better, I'll never know if I could."

"I know."

"If you say a single thing for me, I'll hate it."

"Then I won't." He said it slowly, as if setting down something heavy. "I'll stand where you can see me. That's all. Here, by the post, or closer if you tell me. I won't speak for you. If you stop, I won't finish it. If you want me gone, say *go* and I'll go, and I won't ask why."

She looked at him. He looked back, with that dry tired steadiness, a man who could have crossed the court in five strides and was keeping his boots where they were.

"And if I want to hit something?" she said.

His mouth moved, a very little. "The newel's yours."

It was a small joke, and it cost him. She heard that. Something in her chest that had been clenched since the kitchen let go a notch.

"It's insulted enough already," she said.

"Asha thought so too."

Neela breathed in. She breathed out. "Stay there."

"I'm staying."

"Don't come nearer."

"I won't."

She turned back to the stair. Her whole body wanted to run. She stood with her hands open, and above her the light went from amber to a thin rose, and the first lamp showed in the south range.

The cistern chain clanked its hour.

Across the court a door opened. Janu came out of the kitchen passage with a lamp in each hand, brass, lit, swinging a little as she walked. She was already scolding someone behind her about the oil. She came down the flagged way along the north side of the court with her small quick step, and her eyes went up the stair, and she stopped.

She stopped ten paces from the foot, where she always stopped. She did not seem to know she did it.

Neela's hands were cold. She had held a lamp steady for a long time. She had learned how.

"Janu," she said.

The lamps stopped swinging.

# Chapter 23: Ten Paces

Janu stayed where she was. She stood with a lamp in each hand and looked at Neela's bare palm, still pale where it had pressed the stone.

"You're at the stair," she said.

"Yes."

"You never come to the stair." The scold was in her voice, but it had thinned. "Eravi's done your dressing? Good. Come in. The plantain will go leathery."

"I need to tell you something first." Neela's voice came out level, and she hated how it sounded, like a clerk's. "You'll want to stay where you are."

"I'll decide where I stand in my own court."

"Then stand there."

Across the flags, Devan did not move. Neela kept her eyes on Janu's face, on the creases at the corners of the mouth, the brass key on its cord.

"Six years ago, the night Asha died, I was here," she said. "I stood at the foot of this stair. I held a lamp."

Janu's face stayed as it was. The flames in her two lamps stood straight and still.

"A shuttered lamp," Neela said. "I was told to open it twice if anyone came into the court. No one came. Someone went up the stair past me, and I held the light. I didn't move. I didn't call out. I never went up."

"What lamp?"

"I was part of it. I'm not saying I knew her name. I'm saying I was there, and I was part of it, and it was me."

The cistern chain hung quiet. A swift went over the court, high, going home.

"Why?" Janu said.

It was a flat word. She might have been asking why the oil was short.

Neela had thought it through on the climb, on the way down, and all the way across the wet road from the wood. She had seen where she would put it. Late. She would put it last, after the plain thing was out, and she would not lean on it. Now it was asked, and she answered.

"Because I was sent. I was sold at six to a house that kills for coin. They raised me. They put a lamp in my hand and a name in my head, and I did what I was put there for. They made me, Janu." She heard it come out and stopped. "That's the true answer. It isn't a reason. I was twenty-one. I was good at my work, and I did it well, and I never once asked whose stair it was."

She stopped there. She didn't say *I was a child*, because she hadn't been. She didn't say *I didn't know*, because she had known enough.

Janu set the lamps down.

She did it carefully, one and then the other, on the flags, the way she set a full pot on the table. She straightened. Then she walked.

Neela counted without meaning to. One. Two. The small quick step she knew from the kitchen, the step that went round a house and missed nothing. Three. Four. Janu's eyes were on the stair. Five, six, seven. Eight. Nine.

On ten she stopped, an arm's length from Neela, at the foot of the stair for the first time in six years. Neela braced to be struck.

Janu's hand went past her and slapped the newel.

The flat of it, hard, on the carved bird. The sound cracked across the court. Then again, and again, with a small furious noise in her throat. Her palm must have been stinging. She struck the broken beak with the heel of her hand, as if it were a child that had hidden something.

"Stupid thing," she said. "Stupid, *stupid*."

Then her hand closed on the stone and held on. Her shoulders went down.

"I was asleep," Janu said.

Neela said nothing.

"She said keep her supper warm. She was late. She was always late. She was on the wall, or in the barracks with her slate, or down the village, and she'd come in at all hours with her cap in her hand saying she'd eat standing." Janu's eyes were on the bird. "I put the rice by the kitchen fire. I sat down beside it to wait. Only for a moment, and I slept. I woke at the cock. The pot had burnt dry and the fire was ash." She breathed. "I came out for water in the grey. I found her here."

"Janu."

"I'll not hear that it wasn't my fault." The words came sharp, and Neela closed her mouth. "I know what people say. The kitchen's across the court. The doors were shut. No one could have heard. I've said it to myself six years, and it's true, and it makes no difference. I ought to have been awake."

"I wasn't going to say it."

"Good." Janu turned her head at last and looked at her. Her eyes were red-rimmed and dry. "You'd have had a hard time saying it. Of everyone in this fort."

Neela took that. It was fair. She stood and let it land.

Ten paces. She thought of Janu's tread going round the court every dusk and stopping short of the stair's first step. She thought of the key on its cord and the shut door at the end of the south passage. Six years of keeping the house in order so nothing else would break.

"One thing," Janu said. "I'll ask you the one thing, and you'll tell me true."

"I will."

"Was she afraid?"

The court was very quiet. Neela had known the question was coming, though not from where. She had known it from the moment her hand touched the newel, and she had no soft answer ready. She hadn't tried to build one.

"She asked who was there," she said. "From the top of the stair. She had a laugh in her voice. It was just fading, like she'd been in the middle of something. She wasn't afraid yet. She called down, *Who's there? Who is that?*"

Janu's hand tightened on the bird.

"I was holding the light," Neela said. "She could see it. I had it on the bottom step. And I didn't answer. I stood in the light with my mouth shut and she asked twice, and I said nothing."

For a long moment Janu was silent. A muscle moved in her cheek.

"She was frightened of the dark," she said at last. "From a tiny thing. I'd leave a lamp in her room and she'd count the stairs going down so she'd know where the floor was." Her voice was steady, which was terrible. "She'd have seen a light at the foot and thought it was a friend's."

"Yes," Neela said. "I think she did."

She added nothing. She had learned that much from the master's room and from the deck of a boy's barge and from eleven tins of sweets: the extra word was where the leash went in.

Janu took her hand off the newel.

"I don't forgive you," she said.

"No."

"I don't know that I can. I'll not lie to you. If I say it tonight I'll mean it for an hour, and then I'll find I don't." She wiped her palm down her wrap, once, the way she wiped it before kneading. "I'm sixty-one. I haven't the time to say what I don't mean."

"I wouldn't take it if you did."

"Good." Janu looked at her, and then past her at the dark stair, at the forty-four steps going up to a passage with a window at the end of it. Her mouth worked. Then she bent and picked up one of her lamps.

"Come and eat," she said.

Neela could not move.

"The plantain's drying. I didn't stand over that fire for a woman who'll let it go cold." Janu held out the lamp. "Take this. The court's dark. Mind the step by the well."

Neela's hands were cold. She took the lamp. The brass was warm from Janu's fingers, and the flame stood in it small and upright and steady. She watched it for a breath because she couldn't look at Janu.

Janu bent for the second lamp. She straightened with a hand to the small of her back.

"You'll tell the rest," she said, not turning. "Not tonight. But you'll tell me, in order, with nothing left out. I'll know when you leave something out."

"All of it."

"Hm." It was the sound she made at a pot she had decided to trust. She went away across the flags with her small quick step, and passed the corner without a pause.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Neela stood holding the lamp until the kitchen door shut.

Then she breathed in, and out, and a sound came out of her that was not a word. She set the lamp on the bottom step, next to the bird, where it laid a bar of yellow across the stone. She stared at it. She picked it up again and put it on the well curb instead, as far from the stair as the curb would let her.

Boots crossed the court. This time they didn't stop twenty paces away.

Devan stopped an arm's length off, where Janu had stood. He had said he would stay at the well post, and he had. He hadn't spoken, or moved, from the first sentence to the last. She could see what it had cost him in the set of his shoulders.

"You didn't speak," she said.

"You asked me not to."

"I know. I didn't think you could do it."

"Neither did I." His voice was rough. "I counted the court stones. There are eighty-six between the post and the kitchen door."

A laugh got halfway out of her and turned into something wetter. She pressed the back of her wrist to her mouth. He didn't reach for her. His hands were open at his sides.

She looked up and saw that he was looking at her.

It struck her how long it had been. Not the glance across a room, or the nod at the gate. He had looked at her, but the way a man reads a page with his thumb over one line, taking in everything else and keeping clear of the part that hurt. Since the ledger's last quire he had found the table to speak to, or the road, or her shoulder.

Now he was looking at her face. He wasn't hurrying to finish.

"You're looking at me," she said.

"Yes."

"You haven't, for three days. Not properly."

"I didn't know where to put my eyes." He said it plainly, as he gave the count of a patrol. "I looked at you and saw a lamp on a step. I couldn't make it stand still. I knew it was the same woman. I couldn't find how."

"And now?"

"You told her what you did before she asked what you'd done." His eyes held. "You said *they made me* only when she asked. You said it, and then you said it was no reason. I stood there and listened to you not defending yourself." He drew a breath. "I can look at that."

She wanted to say something dry. She had nothing dry in her.

"Don't thank me," she said instead. "Don't tell me it was brave. She still doesn't forgive it."

"I wasn't going to." His mouth moved, very slightly. "I was going to say you have red earth on your sleeve from the newel, and I'm going to let you keep it."

She looked down. There was a red smear on her cuff, from the bird. She rubbed at it with her thumb and made it worse, and she laughed again, shorter, and found she could stand up straight.

"I haven't got it all out," she said. "The rest of what I owe. But that was one."

"It was one."

"Janu's right about the plantain."

"She usually is."

Neela turned toward the kitchen, and her eye caught the side of the stair. A low arch of laterite where the treads rose over the wall. Under the arch, half hidden by the lip of the eleventh step, was the iron grate she had seen on the day she walked round the newel.

She had meant to ask about it then. She had told no one. She had kept it behind the thing she couldn't say.

"Devan." She crouched. "Come and look at this."

He crouched beside her. She put a finger through the bars to the bolt heads. They were bright. The edges of each were still sharp, cut clean with a file, and not a flake of rust on them.

"The step rings hollow over a drain slot," she said. "I knew that. I've stood on it. But this isn't the drain slot, it's a grate in the wall under the stair. The wall is six years old, or more. This iron isn't." She turned a bolt with her thumbnail. "Rust takes a year in this damp. Two winters on laterite and these would be orange. It's been put in lately, and by someone with a file."

He said nothing. He had gone very still, the way he had in the lamplight at the Resin House with the leaf under his hand.

"Asha was found on this stair," Neela said. "A bear's wounds. Inside walls no bear can climb. Every man in this fort can tell you how she was found. I've heard it told at the well and let them tell it, and I knew what kind of bear. I didn't let myself ask how it got in." She looked up. "Someone opened a door."

"The padippura was held. Four men."

"The padippura was held that night, yes. And this is not the padippura."

He rose. He said nothing for the length of one slow breath. Her eyes went, before she could stop them, across the north court to the dispensary window, a small square of yellow where the old man worked late. She looked away. Nobody had said anything yet. She did not mean to be the one.

Devan had followed the glance. He did not follow it with a word.

"Not a shout across the court," he said, very low. "Someone in this house talks to the guild. Gopan lights the stair lamps at this hour. He'll come to us."

They did not wait long. The old keeper of the cistern came across the flags with an oil can in one hand and a bunch of wicks in the other, as he did each dusk. He had been in the post nineteen years, and he walked as if the court were a shelf he had stocked himself. He reached the stair lamps and set down the can.

Devan crouched at the grate again, so that he and Gopan were of a height and the court saw two men at a lamp.

"Gopan," he said, barely above the oil's gurgle. "This grate. How long has it been iron?"

Gopan squinted. He grunted down onto one knee and laid two knuckles against the bars.

"That's not mine," he said slowly.

"Whose is it?"

"I couldn't say. The old gate was wood, strapped with iron, and rusted to lace. It's the slop-gate, the old postern. Opens on the outside path, under the wall." He rapped a bar. "Never opened in my time. I used to go round by the outside path in the damp months and feel it over for swelling." He frowned at the bolts. "Wood's gone, I see. New iron. Somebody's had a smith up."

"I gave no order for it."

"No, Warden. Nor your father." Gopan said it with the plain honesty of a man who had never been asked to guess. "But I'd not have seen a smith. The lip of that step hides the grate from above unless you kneel, and outside there's a bank of lantana grown over the path, thick as a hedge. I've been meaning to cut it these ten years. And the fever year I kept the chain from a cot, and the sergeant's lads walked my rounds. Before the lantana closed the path, I put my hand through to the lock, felt it hanging where it always hung, and thought no more."

"The lock?" Neela said.

"Big as my fist. Same one." He felt past the bars for it, and his knuckles knocked iron. "There. Old lock, moved to the new door. Same key still fits it, I'd wager. Why would a man buy iron and keep the old lock?"

"Because he wanted nobody to look twice," she said. She spoke to Devan, but her eyes were on the bolts. "The postern was there six years ago. It was there that night. New iron means someone still uses it, and has kept it oiled and quiet, and kept the same key."

"Then who has the key?" Devan said.

Gopan looked up at him, and then at her. His old eyes crinkled with something like surprise at being asked at all.

"I haven't," he said. "Nor the sergeant. Nor Janu, for all she keeps every other." He pulled himself up by the arch with a creak. "There's only the one that ever carried it. Only the physician keeps that key."

The chain clanked above them, the hour, once and then again.

Neela didn't look at Devan. She saw the dispensary, the bark on its beam, eleven bundles and nine of them willow. She saw the satchel on the stool with its flap hanging open on the dark. A pair of green-stained cuffs, wrapping a dressing. A kind old voice asking which day.

She straightened, and the bright bolts were at the height of her hand. Across the court, the dispensary window burned small and yellow.

# Chapter 24: The Physician's Key

Devan had been under the west stair for four hours when the old man came across the court.
