He knew the step before he saw the lamp. It was slow and a little dragging, the walk of a man who has been on his feet since the grey of morning. It came from the north court, past the well, and stopped at the stair's foot. Someone held his breath there.

Devan did not move. He sat on the bare laterite in the black space under the treads, back to the wall, with the iron grate three paces from his knee. The eleventh step sat above him. The dark smelled of wet red stone and old drain. He had picked the place because it was the one spot in the fort where a man could watch the grate and not be seen from above.

A key turned. The lock was fist-sized and well oiled, and it gave without a sound. A hand's breadth of iron door swung inward.

*Outside,* Devan thought. *Come on, then.*

Beyond the bars the lantana rattled. Something small came down the cut path under the wall, careful, with a boy's breath. A runner, hooded, no taller than Kannan's shoulder. The pass village, by the mud on his shins.

"Late," the old man whispered.

"The road was wet, master."

"It always is. Take this. Say it was left by a tapper's wife. Tell him—" The voice stopped. When it came again it was flat and low, a man reciting. "Tell him she's here. The House has no master in it. The forty think she's one of them, nothing more."

Clay touched iron. A jar, passed through the bars.

Devan stood.

He did it slowly, so that his boots made no sound, and stepped out of the shadow into the thin light from the court. "Eravi."

The old man did not cry out. He went still with his arm through the gap and his hand empty. The boy snatched the jar. For a moment his face showed under the hood, round and terrified. Then he was gone, crashing through the lantana. He ran the way a hare does, in lines, and the sound faded downhill.

Devan let him go. He had thought about this for a long time under the stair. He wanted the hand that passed the jar. A boy could be taken at the first ford and know nothing.

"The salve," Eravi said at last. His voice was very dry. "Fourth Guard left a pot. I only..."

"You said *she's here.*"

The arm came back through the bars. The key hung from his fingers, and he looked at it as if it belonged to another man.

"Yes," Eravi said. "I did."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He told it sitting on the lowest tread with his hands between his knees. Devan stood. He kept his own hands loose at his sides, where he could see them.

Twenty years, Eravi said. He had come to Kettamala at forty, from a coast clinic that had thrown him out for a drunken wrong dose. The fort's purse was small and the physician's budget smaller. A tapper's widow sold him poppy and willow bark at half what the factors asked, and the House stood behind her. They asked only for the weather in the hills. Who was sick. Whether the pass was open. News, in place of coin, and what harm was in that? A physician who talks to the sick hears everything. He went on saying yes, and a year became ten.

"And six years ago?"

"A man came to the sick-bay door in the cold month." Eravi's mouth worked. "A big man, with a gentle way of speaking. He said he was the House's. He said there was a theft to put right, a stolen token. He needed the old slop-gate open for one night, and the old lock oiled, and he would be gone by the cock." He raised his eyes. "I said it was a smuggler's door. I said it to myself for a week."

"It was the night my sister died."

"Janu found her. They brought me to her in the grey." The words came out in a thread. "I was the one who wrote that she died of a bear. I wrote it, Warden. You took it from my hand. And I never once let myself..." His shoulders jerked. "I kept the key."

Devan heard the pass-village hounds far down the hill, and above him the cistern chain, patient. He thought of the report in Eravi's neat hand, the one he had folded and kept, the one that had made a bear of what was not.

He thought: *he bled me twice and despaired.* In the fever, before the storm, with the cat behind the curtain. Eravi had counted his pulse with the same shaking fingers, and Devan had trusted them.

His own hands were still. He watched them be still. He had sworn on this stair, the day of Asha's death, never to be the man who held a throat. He looked at the old throat above the stooped collar and kept his fingers where they were.

"The rest," Devan said.

"Three things, before tonight." Eravi counted them on his fingers, and the shaking made the count untidy. "When you lay sick, I sat by your bed. Your sword was on the floor on her side, with the hilt turned to her. I told them that. I thought, a husband's habit. Weeks later I stood in the passage outside your sickroom while she gave the captain his orders. A cart, the second dawn. I told them that." He looked at his feet. "And on the last day I took the oil. The west stair lamps. I tipped the cups and left the wicks white. A physician carries oil to the sick, and nobody looks."

"Three, and tonight's jar makes four."

"Yes."

"Then tell me why you asked my wife which day she would read the pages back."

Eravi's face worked. "A note came in the salve pot, last week. It asked how many the House holds, and the reading day. I asked her. She didn't answer, and I wrote back that I didn't know." He wet his lips. "It's the truth. Tonight's jar was the first thing I sent that was true and whole. I thought, if I give them what's already known..."

"You thought you'd be paying less."

"Yes."

So Chathu did not have the day. He had the rest. Devan filed it, cold and exact, and moved on.

"I was told it was to dark the stair," Eravi said. "They said a lamp-fall would be a mischief, a thing to frighten you. They said you'd be hurt, no more. Not the boy."

"Raiu."

"I sat with his body in the morning." Eravi's face crumpled, and he did not hide it. "Four furrows. I counted them."

The chain clanked. Devan breathed out through his nose.

Every step of this had a price, and none of the men who took the steps had paid it. Raiu had paid, nineteen and unruly-haired. So had Asha. Janu had slept six years with it on her chest, and Neela had come to this foot of the stair with her palm on a carved bird. Eravi had bought cheap bark.

"You'll be held," he said. "In the gate chamber, with Bhaskaran's man at the door and a lamp. You'll write down everything you've passed on, in order, with the dates. You'll write it for Rema, because she keeps records, and the crown will read it."

"Yes."

"No one lays a hand on you. If I hear that anyone has, I'll make the sergeant answer for it." He heard how level it was. "You'll eat. You'll have your bed."

Eravi looked up, wet-eyed, almost afraid. "Why?"

"Because the next man who needs a physician will be a soldier of mine, and you're the only one I have." He held out his hand for the key. "And because I'm not going to be the thing you were afraid of."

The old man put the key in his palm. The iron was warm.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The gate chamber over the padippura held a table, a lantern and three men who had not slept. Bhaskaran stood at the door with his arms folded and his face shut. He had ridden back from the Resin House at Devan's stirrup that morning, and had not said ten words since. Muthu sat on the bench by the shuttered arrow-slit with a cup of cold water he hadn't touched. His big shoulders were round. He had gone quiet, as he did when he had been caught, or when he was hurt.

Eravi settled at the table with a lamp and a stack of clean paper. Bhaskaran's lad took him in hand without a word.

Devan stood at the door and kept his voice low. "The jar will be at the pass village by now. Chathu hears tonight that the House has no master in it."

"Then I ride," Bhaskaran said.

"At first light. Not before." Devan had weighed it the whole way up the court. "I rode that road in the dark three nights back and nearly drowned the mare. The ford is up, and the cut below the cairn has no footing. A rider lost at midnight warns nobody. Your best man, at grey, to Vasu. Bar the gate, trust nothing from the pass village, bring the six in if they can come."

"It's a night's start for him."

"I know." Devan did not look away. "Have the horse saddled in the yard before the watch changes."

Bhaskaran gave one hard nod.

Devan waited until the door had shut. Then he said, in front of Bhaskaran, "Muthu."

"Warden."

"For six years I let you carry grey letters I believed were to your mother." He kept his voice flat. It was the only way he could say it. "And when I learned they weren't, I read every one of them as betrayal. I took the post-key off your belt and wore it on my own neck. Each time you went quiet, I took it for proof."

Muthu didn't look up. A muscle moved in his jaw.

"A man in a grey cloak asked you, the winter Asha died, to write each month to keep me safe. You believed him. The slip that threatened Ittan's wife came later, and you carried it to him, and you told me nothing until the pit. That was a wrong, and you know it. But I held mine longer. The man in my house was sitting on my own bed with a basin, and I looked past him at you." He drew breath. "I should have asked you straight. Instead I watched you. I'm sorry."

The lantern hissed. Bhaskaran looked at the wall.

Muthu put the cup down on the bench. When he spoke, the old loud laugh wasn't in it, but his voice was his own. "You've got the words in the wrong order, Dev."

"I've been practising."

"It shows. You sound like you're reading a charge." He rubbed his face with both hands. "Two grown men, thirty-two and thirty-five, and one of us has to be talked into saying sorry. I'd have done the same, mind. I know what I look like when I go quiet. My mother told me when I was nine." He lowered his hands. "Don't do it again. Ask me. If you ask me, I'll tell you, and if I can't, I'll say I can't, like you do. Fair?"

"Fair."

"Then I'm taking it." Muthu stood, rolled his neck, and held out a hand. Devan took it. The grip was crushing. "And don't think this means I'll let you off the round on the seventh day."

"I'd expect nothing less."

"Good." The corner of Muthu's mouth moved. "Now get out. You look like a man who's been under a stair."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The shutters creaked, the third slat last, as they always did.

Devan came in with a plate. Janu had left it at the chamber door under a cloth, plantain and cold rice and a twist of salt. He hadn't asked how she knew. Neela sat on the bed's edge in her sleeping wrap with her knees drawn up, awake, a lamp on the sill.

She was human. No resin smell hung in the room. Her nails were bitten short, and she hid them when she saw him look.

"Eravi?" she said.

"Held. He's writing it all down." He sat beside her and set the plate between them. "He took the oil from the stair lamps. He told them where I keep my sword. He told them about the cart." He heard the roughness in his own voice. "I didn't touch him."

"I didn't think you would."

"I thought about it."

"Thinking is allowed." She picked up the plantain and, to his surprise, ate half of it. She chewed slowly, watching him. "You haven't looked away once since you came in."

"I'm trying not to."

"You said you counted the court stones."

"Eighty-six." He took the plate off the bed and set it on the floor. His hands wanted to be busy. "Neela. I heard what you said at the stair. What she asked you. *Who's there.* I stood by the well and heard every word."

Her face went still.

"I've heard her on that stair in my sleep for six years," he said. "I never knew the words. I thought it was a scream, or I thought it was nothing. Now I know she laughed first. She wasn't afraid. She asked." He swallowed. "And I know whose light she saw."

"Devan."

"I know, and I'm here." He turned so that she had to see all of his face. "I knew when I stood in the court. I've killed a man who knelt, and I went eight years without striking a living thing, and then I struck a bear twice for you, and none of that is what I'm telling you. I'm telling you I've heard it all and I choose you. Knowing. Not before I knew, not instead. With it."

She breathed out. It came ragged.

"Don't forgive me yet," she said.

"I won't."

She looked up sharply.

"I won't," he said again, gently. "I'll tell you why. If I said it tonight, you'd carry it like a coin you hadn't earned, and every time I was quiet you'd think I was counting. I don't want you to pay me for staying. I'll choose it. Tomorrow, next winter, on the day Janu does or doesn't. Forgiving may come in a year, or it may not come. The choosing doesn't wait for it."

For a moment she only looked at him. The lamp moved in her eyes.

"That's the worst possible thing you could say," she said, "and it's the only one I could stand."

"I know."

"You don't get to be that sure."

"I'm not sure of anything else." He lifted his hand, slowly, and laid two fingers against the scar at her throat. The ridges were warm. "I'm sure of this."

She turned her face into his palm.

It was not like the first time, with rain on the roof and her scars still new to his eyes. She was twenty-seven and she knew her own mind, and she put her hands to the knot of his shirt herself. He let her, and he held still while she did it. The wrap fell from her shoulders. Lamplight lay along the ridges at her wrists, and she watched him look at them, and he didn't look away.

"Tell me if I'm wrong," he said.

"Stay where you are, and you won't be."

He stayed. Her mouth found his collarbone, then the old seam at his ribs, and she traced the white line with her tongue until he said her name and could not manage the rest. She laughed at that, low, against his skin. He drew her up and kissed her properly, and the bed took their weight with a long wooden complaint. Her bitten nails scored his shoulder. He tasted salt and plantain and her. Her thighs closed on his hips, and for a while neither of them kept count of anything.

Afterward she lay with her cheek on his chest and her palm flat over his heart. The shutter slat was still. He felt her breath settle.

"Nobody's watching," she said, drowsy. "Nobody's at the window. For the first time since the wedding."

"The grove has a watcher."

"The grove has a watcher who can't see through a latch." Her hand closed. "Stay till morning."

"You don't have to ask that."

"I want to hear you say it anyway."

"I'm here," Devan said. "Till morning and after."

She slept. He didn't, for a long time. He lay with her breath moving under his hand and thought about a ledger and an old man's key and the small cost of a night without resin: the ache that would count against her tomorrow, and the day after. He had filled her kindi, spout toward the pillow, as he did every night. It was the one thing in this house he could do that was nothing but care.

He thought of the rider, too, and the road. First light was the best the wood allowed. He told himself so twice, and slept at last without believing it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He woke to a horse on the wood road.

The sky behind the shutters was the colour of ash. First grey. Neela stirred against him, and the sound came again, a rider pressed hard, hooves slapping wet clay and then stone, and the cistern chain clanked as if it too had been startled.

He was at the window with his mundu knotted before he decided to be. Through the slats he saw the road come out of the wood and drop to the padippura, and a single rider on it, low over a mare's neck, lathered to the girth. The sentry's horn gave one long note and two short. Unarmed, one man.

Bhaskaran's voice rang across the court. The gate groaned. Below, a lad stood by the stable arch with a saddled horse and a message not yet spoken, caught in the very act of going. By the time Devan reached the stair, the rider from the wood was down in the yard with his hands on his knees, hauling air. Mud to the thigh. A face Devan knew: Vasu, whose mother kept ducks at Kalladi.

"Warden." He straightened with a gasp. "The House. They've shut the gate on us."

"Who has?"

"Hunters. They came back out of the wood in the night, dozens, with nets and poles, and the gate was barred before the tapping bell. Bhaskaran's lads are outside the wall, in the sheds, six of us and Pavi." He wiped his mouth. "They're on the walls, Warden. All of them. Chathu's hunters, with the forty standing in among them, and I couldn't see..."

He stopped.

Devan looked at the lad by the arch, and the horse that would have carried a warning through the dark road and arrived behind the hunters. He had chosen the grey over the night. Whatever the night would have bought was gone now, and the choice had been his.

"What couldn't you see?" he said.

Vasu looked at him, and then at the stair, where Neela had appeared at the top in her wrap, one hand on the rail, her face quite white.

"Anything," he said. "I couldn't see what they'd done with them."

# Chapter 25: The Gate Shut

Devan left the assize table in the middle of a sentence.

Neela watched it from the hall door. Rema had called the sitting at first grey, the moment Vasu's news was in, and had just asked a clerk to read Eravi's dated account into the roll for the second time. Devan stood, said "Forgive me," and walked out past eight pillars and forty-odd faces. He didn't hurry. A man who runs from a hearing gives it a story. He gave it a back.

Rema looked up from the warden's chair. She did not call him back. She dipped her pen.

"The court notes the warden's absence," she said, and wrote it, and the scratch of the nib followed Neela out like a hand on her collar.

Within the hour they were on the wood road, the morning barely begun. Devan, Neela, Bhaskaran and eight Kettamala spearmen rode two by two on wet clay, the grey mare favouring her off hind under Neela. The ledger rode in her saddlebag, wrapped in greased cloth. It pressed her knee at every stride, a block of other people's names.

Nobody spoke for the first long stretch. The wood closed over them, black trunks and dripping leaf, and the langurs in the high branches went silent as the column passed.

"Thirty-one real names on that plank," she said at last. "Nine blanks."

"I know."

"I told Twenty-two I couldn't give hers yet. I said a name means reading the entry aloud, and I couldn't do it safely." She stared between the mare's ears. "I wanted the reading day to be right. Daylight, the holder and the owner, no clerks. I wanted each of them to choose who sat in the room. That took until after the assize."

"It was a good plan."

"It was slow."

He didn't answer that, and she didn't ask him to.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They reached the last rise in the long gold light of late afternoon. The Resin House sat in its clearing below, laterite wall and tapping sheds, the bitter smell of scorched tamarind coming up the slope on the wind. The gate was shut. The sun stood a hand above the trees.

The walls were full.

She counted without meaning to, because counting was what she did when she was afraid. Hunters first: eight at the gatehouse, more along the north wall, nets coiled on their shoulders, hooked poles ready. Then the others, standing among them in the open. Grey wraps and cropped heads, thin wrists. Men and women she had braided hair for and shared a bench with. They stood where the hunters had put them, a pole's length of air at each shoulder.

Thirty-nine.

She went along the wall again and got the same number. One short. Beyond the wall, in the lee of the tapping sheds, five men in Kettamala grey huddled round a cold fire, and Pavi's curled, old-broken hand showed among them. They were outside their own wall, and they looked up at her like drowned things. Pavi was the one of the forty Chathu didn't hold.

"Eight hunters at the gate," Bhaskaran said quietly. "More inside. We are eleven."

"And five outside," Devan said.

"Five who can't climb it."

Neela rode down alone to the edge of bowshot and reined in. She said the names. It was a habit, and now it was a plan. "Thilak. Madhavi. Kuttu. Meenu." Some of the faces flinched. One or two looked at their feet. Meenu, who had cried at the roll leaf, was pressed against the stone as if it might open. Twenty-two stood second from the end, looking straight at Neela with her mouth shut.

Then a voice came down from the gatehouse, thin and cracked.

"Master!"

Thilak, forty-one, tapper before he was an odiyan, stood at the parapet with a hunter's pole laid across the back of his knees. He had brought her tea every evening she held the House and never once asked her for a thing. His hands gripped the stone. She saw why he'd been chosen to shout. No one else on that wall would say *Master* to her and mean it as a plea.

"You kept the book!" His voice had gone high and steady, a man reading from a slate. "So did he. He says he'll burn ours."

*Ours.* It took her a breath. Not the pages. It fell into place cold and exact: Chathu had kept the other thing. He had kept the trees.

"Thilak," she said.

"I'm to say it twice." His eyes held hers. They said what his mouth couldn't, and she took it, the only gift he had. "I've said it."

The gate wicket opened above the arch. A man came out onto the gatehouse walk, one foot uneven from an old break, one hand resting lightly on the wall as if on the head of a child.

"Seventeen," Chathu said.

He didn't raise his voice. He never had. It came down warm and soft and tired, the voice of a man calling a girl in from the rain. Even the hunters on the wall seemed to lean into it.

"I won't ask you to come in. I know you can't hear that from me. Let me say a smaller thing." He spread his hands. "The groves for the book. That is all. You keep the thirty-one names you've written on your plank, and the nine you haven't. You keep your husband. You ride home. I keep the House, and the trees stay standing, and the resin comes as it always has."

"And if I don't?"

"Then it doesn't." He said it sadly. "I have a fire in the oil store, child, and a man at the nearest grove with a torch. I'd rather not. The nightbark takes forty years to bear. But you know what a moon without resin does. They ache first. You'll ache. You've had the first of it already." His eyes went to her hands on the rein, to the nails she hadn't hidden. "Do you imagine I haven't counted yours?"

She hadn't thought he would. Her hands tightened, and she made them loosen.

"I call that a fair price," Chathu said.

He believed it. That was the worst of it. He was doing his sums, as he always had, and she was a debt on the page.

Neela sat very still. She thought of the plank in the carpentry shed and the lamp-black names, and of Twenty-two asking, *Does my roll name me?* She thought of the reading day she had put off. Until the assize. Until the right day. Until it could be done safely and with consent and by daylight, with no clerk in the room.

She had called it protection. It had been a delay, and Chathu had gone straight through it like a man through a door left on the latch. While she waited to be right, he had come back out of the wood with his nets and his poles. She had left the forty their names unread, and he had walked in and found them unowned.

*I handed him back what I'd taken.*

She couldn't argue with that. She looked at it plainly and it did not get smaller.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Something moved at Chathu's shoulder.

A man had come up beside him on the walk, big and bearded and slow. He wore a plain brown wrap and came on a short stiff stride that hitched on the left leg, a drag and a step, though he was not in his shape. He didn't look at the road. He sat on the stone coping with his back to the drop, drew a knife, and began to carve.

Vellan.

Neela had seen him from a dozen angles. In the Resin House yard when she was nine, vast and quiet. At the meeting stone in daylight with a wooden bird in his hand. In the ledger, in Chathu's neat black ink: *Hand, Thirty.* She had never seen him look so ordinary. A man whittling in the sun.

Beside her Devan went still.

She did not turn. She felt it all the same, along the length of the saddle, a change in the air at her left side. His reins stayed slack. His breath went in and did not come out for a long time.

He knew who it was. He had seen that face by day only once, from a ridge, too far to read, and he knew it, the way you know a stair in the dark.

Vellan's knife turned. A curl of pale wood fell. He lifted the thing in his hand: plum-sized, a bird, one wing folded, the head turned to listen. He blew the dust off its beak.

Then he looked down at her.

Not at Devan, who had every right to a look. Not at Bhaskaran with his spear, or the Kettamala horses. At her, only, with a flat tired steadiness that held no threat and no regret and no apology either. It was the look of a man checking that a thing he had set down was still where he'd left it.

She thought: *He's waiting to see which of us breaks.*

She would not break first. She sat the mare and looked back. In the end it was Vellan who dropped his eyes, to the bird, and went on carving.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Seventeen," Chathu said again. "Hear the rest."

"I'm listening."

"At dusk I'll put resin on every one of them on this wall. Not the way you do it, with a hand and a choice. The way the House does it when it must." He said it without relish, the way he'd have said *the rice is late*. "They'll turn on the stroke of sunset, and it will tear them. You've felt that. Some will bleed a week. Then the gate opens, and they go into the wood. Animals with their minds whole and every hunger of the shape in them, and the nearest living things on the hill will be you and the man beside you."

A breath went along the wall. Neela saw Meenu's mouth open and shut.

"Don't think they'll refuse you the kindness," Chathu went on. "I was the first witness at every one of their turnings. I'll stand on this wall with the word in my mouth, and any of them who will not hunt you, I'll cage where he stands. And my hunters hold the wood road behind you. There is no riding home tonight, child. There is only the hill."

He leaned on the coping. "You have their pages. I know you do. They are in the bag against your knee. At dusk you can read them. One by one, in the dark, in their hearing. That is a witness's power, and it is yours." His voice softened further. "Cage them, and they stop. They'll be in the kennels with the room I have ready. Or don't, and they'll find you. That's all it is. A choice. I only describe it."

"You'd do that to them."

"I'd do it *for* them. To keep a House together. To keep them fed." He looked genuinely grieved. "You'll learn it, if you keep the book. Nobody holds a page and stays clean of it. I was twenty-six when I learned."

"Chathu."

"Until dusk," he said, and turned, and went in. The wicket shut. Vellan stayed a moment on the coping and then rose, with that hitch, and followed him.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wall did not empty. The hunters stayed, and the thirty-nine stood where they were put, the sun going down behind the trees.

Neela turned the mare. Her legs felt hollow. Devan fell in beside her, and they walked the horses up the slope to where Bhaskaran's men had drawn together, out of bowshot, on a bare shoulder of ground above the road.

She looked back once, down the way they had come. Two hunters stood in the road where it left the rise, nets over their shoulders, where an hour ago there had been no one. As she watched, a third stepped out beside them.

The sun was still up. The thirty-nine were within hearing. She made herself look at the plain thing: she could ride down now, break the seals, and read the pages back in daylight, as she had always meant to.

She turned it over and it did not hold. A handover was read in full, and a full reading said every night-shape aloud. Every hunter on that wall would hear them, thirty-nine words handed to men with nets and rings. And Chathu would still stand there, the first witness at every turning, with his own mouth. Pages in their hands would not unmake him. At dusk he could cage whoever he pleased. She would have made herself a master for nothing, and told his hunters the shapes besides.

So it was the binary he had given her, with the sun already going.

She waited for the other thing.

She knew exactly what it would be. *I asked you to read them the day we rode out.* He had asked, on the ridge road, without heat. *Read them now, before the assize.* She had said no, and given a good reason. He could say it now, and be right, and she would have to carry it. She wanted him to. It would have been something she could be angry at.

He said nothing.

He sat the horse with his hands on the pommel and his eyes on the wall, his face grey under the road mud. A small muscle worked at the corner of his jaw. She found she was counting again, under her breath, the hunters on the north wall, because her mind wanted something to hold. Her lips moved. She lost the number at the third pole.

"Nine along the north," Devan said, very low.

She stopped.

"Eight at the gate," he said. "Two on the corner post. That's nineteen." His eyes didn't leave the wall. He was counting for her, in her order, gate first and then the north, and he had learned the order from watching her lips. "Go on."

She went on. She named the posts and he gave the numbers back, steady as a man calling out soundings. When she reached the end of the wall she was breathing evenly, and the number she had was a real one. He turned his horse a half step, and the animal's shoulder came between her and the place where Meenu stood against the stone.

"You could say it," she said.

"I could." He said it quite evenly. "It wouldn't undo one hour."

That was all. She looked at him, at the hands loose on the pommel, and her throat closed.

"Whatever I do," she said, "it lands on you too."

"Yes."

"If I read them, I'm a master. Your wife is a master, and the crown has my name beside forty." She heard herself and made herself go on. "And if I don't, they'll hunt us, and you'll have to fight my sisters and brothers, and you have already struck a man for me this winter, and you can't tell me what you'll be after that."

"I can't."

"And you're not telling me what to do."

"No." The wind lifted the wet hair off his brow. "What you do is yours. I'll carry my half, whichever way."

She nodded, because her throat would not do anything else.

Behind the wall a bell began to ring. It was the tapping bell, thin and sweet, calling the long table to its meal. Out on the wall a hunter laughed at something, short and bored, and the thirty-nine did not move. The sun touched the tops of the nightbark.

She had less than an hour. She had a bag against her knee, with forty names in it, and a man beside her who would not tell her what to do.

*Cage them with their pages,* Chathu had said, *or be hunted by them.*

She did not yet know which of those she could survive being.

# Chapter 26: Human at Dusk

Neela's hand went to her belt for the flask, and found nothing.

Devan saw it from a horse's length away. She did it the way a man pats a pocket he knows is empty. Her fingers closed on cloth and stayed there.

They had climbed the bare shoulder above the road, and the sun was sinking into the tops of the nightbark. Bhaskaran's men sat their horses in a tight ring behind them with their spears grounded. Below, in the clearing, the tapping bell had stopped.

"It's in the store," she said. "Behind the tapping floor. I put it in the week I took the House."

"Inside the wall."

"Inside the wall." She was looking at her own fingers. "Everyone's flask went in the same box, Thilak's and Pavi's and mine, with the ration written on a tag. So nobody could say I kept more than they did." She laughed once, short. "It seemed clean at the time."

"Pavi is outside. Pavi could—"

"Pavi tried. At noon, before we came, by the drain. It was pinned again and two hunters sat on the grate." She turned her wrist and showed him the inner side, the old ridged scars pale in the failing light. "The last thumb of Ammu's resin went on the ridge, scouting the well house. There's no more. I'm a woman tonight, Devan. Thirty-nine of them will be animals, and I'll be a woman on a hill with no claws."

He had known it for a little while. He had worked it out when she stopped at the rim of bowshot and did not reach for anything. Knowing it did not make it lighter.

"Yes," he said.

She said nothing else for a time. The wind brought the smell of scorched tamarind up from the clearing, and her nostrils flared at it as if it were a hand on her throat.

"I did this," she said.

"Chathu did."

"I put the flask in the box. I put off the reading. I put their names on a plank in lamp-black and told them it was safe." She still hadn't looked up. "I've become him, Devan. I kept them and I called it care, and the day it counted I wasn't there to read the pages, because I wanted to be right. That is what he does. He is always about to be right."

Devan thought about arguing. He had the facts for it, and they were good ones. She had stood on a wall under hunters' arrows and carried a ledger through the ache. She had asked thirty-one people their names.

He put them away. She hadn't asked for comfort. She had said a thing aloud, which was the hardest sort of work she did, and the worst answer was to take it from her.

"Maybe," he said. "That's yours to carry or put down. What you do next is still yours. I won't tell you it isn't."

She breathed in, slowly. The tension in her shoulders did not go, but it moved.

"You're not going to say I'm not like him."

"I don't know that. I know you counted the hunters out loud for an hour so you could breathe, and he never needed to."

Her mouth twitched. It wasn't a smile, but it was near one.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She went to the saddlebag like a woman walking to a door she dreaded.

The ledger came out in its greased cloth, the black book with the contract leaves loose at the stitching and the resin-ink pages sewn in behind. She unwrapped it on a flat rock, in what light was left. Bhaskaran's men turned their heads away without being told. She turned the pages with her thumbs. They were stiff and tacky and wrong to the touch, like skin that had been wet and dried.

Near the front of the back half she stopped. She did not need to read the name. She knew the place by the number: *Thirty*.

"Vellan's," she said.

She took the page between two fingers at the stitching and tore it, slowly, so the thread gave in a line. It made a small dry sound. She laid the page flat on her palm and held it out to him.

He did not take it.

"Chathu says I'll cage the forty tonight," she said. "He's not sure which one I'll choose, and I won't be sure either, so I may as well be honest about it. If the House comes down the hill at me and I'm out of ground, I don't know what my hand does." She kept it steady. "I don't want this one in my hand. Vellan will come. He'll come for me, or for you, because he's the one thing Chathu can still point. If it's his page or one of our lives, I don't want to be the one who decides."

"Neela."

"It's yours." She said it quickly, as if speed would make it cheaper. "He killed your sister. Whatever's written there, that's the oldest right on this hill. If he comes tonight, the choice belongs to the man with the most claim to it. I'll not take it from you and I'll not make it for you." Her eyes finally came up. "And I trust you not to read it. Not until you choose."

He looked at the page. A strip of tacky paper with a name at the head, a single line of black ink beneath it, and a number. He looked at her face instead, and kept his eyes there, and did not let them drop to the line.

It was the cruelest thing she had ever given him. It was also the most trusting. She knew both, and he saw that she did.

"The bargain stays," he said. "You told me, if I begin to read, you take it. I told you the same. That holds."

"It holds."

"Then I'll hold it." He took the page by its edge. It was warm from her palm. He folded it once, along the tear, and tucked it inside his shirt against his ribs, over the old white seam, where he'd feel it with every breath.

She let her hand drop. It shook, and she pressed it flat against her thigh until it stilled.

"That's the most I've ever given anyone," she said.

"I know what it cost."

"No." She almost laughed. "You don't. But thank you for saying it."

He took Kannan's white pebble from his belt pouch and put it in his cheek. He had no resin to guard against and no sleeper's mouth to fear. But the page lay against his ribs, and he had sworn on a stair never to be the man who held a throat. A pebble slowed words. It was the only lock he trusted tonight.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Bhaskaran took it badly, and then he took it.

"You want us to ride *off*," he said, low. "With the wall full."

"East along the ridge to the cairn, where the camp was. Horses and spears." Devan took the pebble out to say it and set it back. "Face the way you ride. Nobody looks at the wall after the sun touches the trees."

"They're not our enemies."

"They'll turn. Whoever sees it becomes a witness, and carries a word in his mouth for the rest of his life. Every one of them would know exactly who you are." He held the sergeant's eyes. "I won't hand nine good men that. If you hear a thing you can't help, you hear it. You don't turn round."

Bhaskaran's mouth worked. He looked past Devan to Neela, who stood by the rock with the ledger in her arms, and some of the hardness went out of him.

"The hunters hold the road," he said. "Mine are on the ridge track, then."

"Yes. Take Pavi and the five from the sheds with you if you can reach them." Devan did not say *if they live*. "Go."

They went in a clatter of wet harness. Bhaskaran was the last to turn his horse, and he said nothing, only touched two fingers to his brow, as he had at Raiu's pyre.

Then the hill was theirs. Neela, himself, the rock. The long gold light went red along the ridge.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It began as the sun went.

He had set himself with his back to the Resin House and his face to the wood. Neela stood at his shoulder facing the same way, arms wrapped round the ledger, eyes fixed on the trunks. He could see, without looking, that she was counting them. The rim of the sun slid below the nightbark. Then he heard it.

The first cry came thin and high. It might have been a girl's. A second followed, lower, ripped off short. Then another and another, from every part of the wall, a rolling chorus that was not a battle cry. He had heard those. This was the sound a body makes when it is turned inside out against its will.

"Don't," Devan said, as she flinched toward it. "Don't turn round."

"I know."

"I'm not saying it for them. I'm saying it for you."

"I *know*." Her voice cracked on it. "I can hear Meenu."

He could hear her too. A thin shriek, and a breath, and a longer one. Meenu, who had cried at the roll leaf. A name, now, as well as a number.

He stood and listened, because there was nothing else to do. The pebble was hard against his teeth. After the first sixty heartbeats the cries changed. They went down into something worse, grunts and a wet tearing, claws on stone, a hunter shouting a short sharp order and getting no answer. Over it all a bitter wind off the nightbark carried the smell of fresh blood.

"A forced turning tears them," she said. Her teeth were set. "The scars at the throat and wrists. It opens them all, and they'll bleed for days."

He took the pebble from his cheek.

"This is cruelty," he said, to the dusk, to the trees, to nobody. His voice was quite level. It was the voice he used for reading a charge. "I want that said, once, in the open. It isn't discipline, or mercy, or a hard sum. Chathu knows what it is. Thirty-nine people are bleeding on a wall to make them dangerous. They didn't agree to it, and he's lying to himself if he calls it a kindness. I won't call it anything else, and neither will you."

She turned her head and looked at him. He hadn't thought he had said it for her. But he saw, in what was left of the light, that she had needed to hear a man say it who had a right to.

"Thank you," she said.

"It's only the word for it."

"Yes," she said. "I haven't had one."

He put the pebble back.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


When the cries had thinned to scattered whimpering, he turned and unslung his pack.

It was an old canvas thing, still smelling of the kalari's sesame oil. He had packed it at Kettamala without thinking, with a flint and a water skin and a twist of rice. He knelt on the wet ground and unbuckled the flap.

"What are you doing?"

"You'll be running."

"I'll be—"

"Fast. If the ache lets you. Which it won't, for long." He took the ledger from her arms and set it in the pack, the black book wrapped in its greased cloth, and buckled the straps over it. "You shouldn't carry other people's names on a hill. I will. I'm bigger."

"You'll be slower."

"Yes."

"Then they'll catch you."

"They'll catch both of us, one way or another." He stood and shouldered the pack. It was heavy, as it ought to be. He took the lamp from the saddle and trimmed it with his thumb, and the oil smelled of sesame, the kalari, the early mornings. He hung it from the strap at his chest, unlit. "I'll light it when you say. A lamp makes a witness of the person who holds it. Mine, not yours."

She stared at him. In the dusk her face was bare, with all the guarding gone.

"The pages?" he said. "The forty?"

"Here." She touched her ribs. She had folded them flat in oilcloth and bound them with a strip of her own wrap. "Where I can reach them. Not Vellan's." She swallowed. "I won't read them, Devan. I mean it. Not unless there's nothing left."

"I heard."

"I still want to be able to."

"Then keep them," he said. "A thing you can't reach isn't a choice."

She nodded, jerkily, and dragged her sleeve across her face.

His hand had gone to the folded page inside his shirt, to the edge against his ribs. It pressed there, warm. Six years he had wanted a sentence, and now it lay a finger's breadth from his heart, and he did not know if he'd have the strength to leave it folded.

*Asha,* he thought. Only that. He did not say it. His lips moved around the shape of it behind the pebble, and he hoped she didn't see.

She did. Her hand came down over his, flat, over the page.

"I'm here," she said.

"I know."

"If you start to read," she said, "I'll take it."

"And if I don't let go?"

"Then I'll hold on till you do."

It was not an answer he could improve on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The gate opened.

They heard it before they saw it: the bar lifting, the long drag of wood across stone, and then the hush that comes before a flood. The lamps along the wall had gone out. In the dusk the gateway was a black mouth.

"Wood," Devan said. "Not the road."

"The road's held."

"Then upslope. Keep the trees close. Let them come to us through the trunks, not the open." He felt her take her hand away. She had her feet planted, and he had never seen her so entirely alone in her own body. "You'll feel it first. Anything that bites you opens on me. I'll feel it a breath behind. Don't spare yourself on my account."

"I won't. I'll spare you on mine."

"Neela—"

"Don't tell me what it costs. I've counted." She was almost smiling, and it made the rest of her face worse. "Every bite on me, a welt on you. Every scratch. A mortal wound drags you down beside me. I've known it since the yard, and I'm going to bleed on you anyway, because I've nothing else to run on."

"Then bleed," he said. "I'm here."

They came out of the gate in a rush.

First the sound, a pouring of padded feet on packed clay. Then the shapes, low and quick and many, spilling past the hunters as water goes round stones: a sleek otter-dark one, a long spotted back, a jackal's thin legs, things with horns and things with teeth and things he had no name for. Their eyes caught the last red light. Thirty-nine, he counted, because she had taught him to count. And behind them the great slow shadow with a hitch in its stride, coming through the gate last of all.

Neela's breath went in.

"Run," Devan said.

# Chapter 27: Before Dawn

Neela ran with a lamp in one hand and forty names strapped against her ribs.

Devan had pushed the lamp at her in the first twenty strides. "I need both hands," he said, and she understood what the hands were for. She held it by the ring now, unlit. The oilcloth packet shifted under her wrap at every step. She did not touch it. She ran upslope between black trunks with his breath at her shoulder and counted: strides, roots, the slap of the pack on his back.

The wood filled up behind them like a bowl.

They came in ones and twos. A long spotted back slid between two trees on her left, belly low, and was gone. Something heavy broke through the ferns. Eyes showed green at knee height, paired, then paired again. They did not rush. They closed wide and patient, the way hounds do when the slope will do the work for them.

Every one of them knew her. That was the horror of it. The mind sat whole behind each eye, and the hunger belonged to the animal, and the legs belonged to whoever had been smeared and sent.

"Thilak," she said. It came out ragged. "Thilak, it's Neela."

A heavy shape checked at the edge of the dark.

"You brought me tea every evening I held the House. You never asked me for a thing." She ran on and kept talking, because the other choice was to go quiet and be caught. "Madhavi. Kuttu. Meenu, I heard you on the wall. I'm sorry. I'm here."

Nothing answered. Nothing could. The shape stood a breath longer than a hunter should, then turned and went crashing back downhill.

She nearly stopped. Devan's hand caught her elbow and kept her moving.

"Again," he said.

"He can't hear you out here!" She flung it at the trees. "Chathu's on the wall. His word doesn't reach this far. Nobody is caging you tonight."

In the House she had been Seventeen, and they had all been numbers. Now she dragged names up out of the plank in the carpentry shed, lamp-black on pale wood, and threw them into the dark one at a time. "Thulasi. Kuttu. Madhavi."

They broke off by ones. A low shape veered away. Then a pair, side by side, as if they had been waiting for permission. Others came on with their heads down, and she saw it in how they ran. It was not hunger. It was fear of a man on a wall with a word in his mouth. She could not be angry at that.

"Twenty-two." A pale narrow shape paced her on the right, close enough to touch. "Your roll names you. I won't read it in the dark. At noon, in the sun, if you want it. You and me and whoever you choose."

The shape ran beside her for ten strides. Then it dropped back, and the dark where it had been felt wider.

The old tappers' verse came to her unasked, the line Ammu had said was a door. *The name given back at the closing of day.* She had heard it as a thing between two people at a dusk turning. She had never thought it could be done with her mouth, to a wood full of strangers, with no one answering.

Then the root. She never saw it. Her foot went, the ground came up, and the lamp rang off a stone. She rolled, and something took her calf.

Teeth, quick and narrow. It let go as soon as it had tasted. Blood ran warm into her wrap, and the pain came after, hot and bright.

Devan grunted mid-stride and slapped his own left calf. Under his palm a welt was rising, hot and shallow, the skin lifted in a weeping line that did not open.

"That's mine," he said.

"I know." She tried to rise and the leg folded. "Go on. I'll—"

"No."

He had the pack swung to his chest and the buckles snapped before she finished. Then his arms were under her, and he heaved her onto his back as if she were one more bundle. "Hold the lamp. Hold me."

She hooked her arms round his neck, the lamp ring biting her fingers. He ran. She called names over his shoulder into the dark.

A heavy brindled shape came out of the ferns on the left. She knew the gait. She had called its name three times.

"Madhavi," she said. "Madhavi, it's me."

It did not break off. It closed low and fast, and Devan turned his body to take it on the side away from her, and the jaws fastened high on his thigh. He went down on one knee with a sound she felt through her own ribs. Cloth tore. Blood ran down his leg, a real wound, deep and wet. A welt came up hot on her own thigh, and she gasped at it, and the blood was all his.

The teeth did not let go.

Her free hand found the packet under her wrap. Oilcloth, the stiff edges of forty pages, and in them Madhavi's whole night-shape in a witness's hand. One sentence, aloud, in the beast's hearing, and the jaws would stop for good. It sat against her ribs like a second heartbeat.

She took her hand away.

"Madhavi," she said, and her voice shook. "I'm not going to do it. I won't. Not to you. Let go."

A breath. Two. The brindled head jerked, as though something behind the eyes had pulled hard. The jaws opened. The shape fell back with his blood on its muzzle and stood a moment in the ferns, and then it turned and went.

"Up," Devan said through his teeth. He was already getting up.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She lost the hours after that.

She remembered his shoulders working under her, and the pack knocking the ledger against his ribs, and her cheek wet with his sweat. She remembered calling names until her voice went to a rasp and then calling them in a whisper, because he could run and she could still do that. Twice he stumbled. Once he went to one knee, and a long cold breath of fur passed within a hand of her face. He got up, and it did not follow.

When he finally set her down, his hands were shaking.

"I can walk," she said, and found she could, if she leaned. They went on together, two bleeding people on a hill, his arm round her and hers round him. His thigh still bled through the torn cloth, and the blood ran down his leg and over her welted calf where she pressed against him. The welt on her thigh wept clear.

The dark was thinning. It was not light. It was a greyness between the trunks, as though the night had worn through at the elbow.

She knew the tree before she knew where she was.

It stood alone in a ring of trampled fern, blackbark, old as the House, its trunk split at the base into roots like knuckles. Something pale lay among them. A rag of blue cotton, rained on and dried and rained on again, with a cord pouch beside it, held down by a flat stone.

Ammu's Valli cloth. Muthu had laid it there.

"This is where she—"

"I know," Devan said.

They had both stopped. Neither had chosen it. She thought, with the part of her that still counted: *The wood brought us here.* Then she heard the hitch.

A drag and a step. Slow, unhurried, from behind the trunk.

The bear came round the tree without a sound beyond that. He filled the gap between two roots. His coat was matted with the night, and in the grey his small eyes held nothing at all, neither hate nor pity, only a patient weariness that she knew. It was the look from the wall.

She had time to say, "Devan, go—" and then the forepaw swept her off her feet.

She hit the roots on her back where Ammu had died. The air left her. A weight came down on her breastbone, and claws pricked through the wrap, and the smell of him closed over her like a wet wool blanket. Scorched tamarind. Old blood. A man's sweat under an animal's musk.

He did not bite. She lay under his paw and understood, all at once and very coldly, why he did not.

Devan stood over them.

He had put the pack down. His right hand was inside his shirt, and it came out holding a folded strip of tacky paper. The lamp had rolled against a root. He snatched it up, struck flint twice with fingers that would not hold still, and the wick caught. The little flame put gold on his face and a shaking gold on the page.

He opened it. He held it to the light.

"Off her," he said.

The bear did not move. The paw pressed and the claws went in a hair deeper.

She could see the page from below, upside down: a name at its head in Chathu's neat black hand, a line beneath it, a number. One sentence, aloud, in the bear's hearing, before the dawn. She watched Devan's lips shape the heading. He had not said anything yet, but he was reading. He had begun.

Six years, she thought. Six years of Asha's notes tied in red thread, and a stair he had sworn on. The oldest claim on the hill.

She had stood at the foot of that stair with a shuttered lamp. She had counted to three hundred and ten.

"Devan."

He didn't look at her.

"You haven't struck first in eight years." She said it between breaths, because the paw left her little else. "You swore it to me twice, in our bed at Kettamala, that if you began to read I'd take it, and you'd let go. It's not a throat. It's a year. A year of his mind going, in a cage behind the cook-house, and you'd be the one who put him there."

"He killed her." His voice was very low and perfectly even, the voice he used for a charge. "He's standing on you. He could finish it now."

"He hasn't. Because you've got the page lit. That's the only reason I'm breathing, and I'm still asking."

"Neela—"

"You said if you begin to read, I take it." Her hand was free. She had known it was free since she fell. "I said I'd hold on till you let go."

The flame leaned in a draught. The bear's breath rolled over them both. Somewhere out in the grey, something moved and was still.

She reached up. It was a long way. Every claw lifted and settled with the stretch of her arm, and she did not look at them. She closed two fingers on the edge of the page, and then her whole hand.

His grip was iron for a moment, a man holding a sentence the way a drowning man holds a rope.

Then it opened.

He did not speak. He stood with his empty hand in the lamplight and let her have it. Through the tacky paper she felt a fine tremor that was not hers.

"Thank you," she said, and found her voice had gone.

"Don't." He knelt. His face looked scraped to the bone. "Tell me what to do with my hands."

"The book. Open the book. Hold the light."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He dragged the pack to him and unbuckled it one-handed, the lamp in the other. The ledger came out in its greased cloth. He laid it open on the roots beside her head, front half up, the loose contract leaves lifting in the draught. She turned the leaves with the hand that wasn't holding Vellan's page, and the bear's weight rode her breath.

She knew where it was. She had turned past it once, in the Resin House, unread.

*Thirty.* Chathu's cipher, small and tidy, plain ink on a plain leaf. No resin in it, no witness's hand. It couldn't cage a mouse. It was only what the House had written about a man.

She read it aloud in the plain tongue, slowly, so that every word would arrive.

"Thirty. Release not to be granted. Sell to the crown when spent."

Nothing happened.

She said it again, because it was a short line and she wanted the bear to have it twice. "Thirty. Release not to be granted. Sell to the crown when spent. That's in his hand, Vellan. It's been in his hand since your third contract. He wrote it in ordinary ink, so he'd never have to say it."

The paw stopped pressing. It did not lift, not at once. It rested on her, a weight with the intent drained out.

*Three times over,* she thought. *He earned it three times over.* A man who believed the blade was never guilty, only the hand that threw it, and who had waited years to be set down. The hand had written his price in advance.

The small eyes closed. When they opened they were looking at the page in her fist.

The paw lifted away.

The bear backed one step, then another. He lowered himself among the roots as if his legs had been cut. He put his chin on his forearms, six feet from her face, and did not move again.

She lay where she was. She did not trust herself to get up.

"Is he—" Devan began.

"Waiting."

"For what?"

"Light."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The grey went to pearl. A bird started up somewhere, and then another. The trunks came out of the dark one at a time like men stepping from a crowd.

Round the edge of the clearing she saw them. Low shapes at the fern line, a dozen, then more, standing in the paling dark and not coming nearer. The thin-flanked ones, the antlered, the spotted. A pale narrow one who held her eyes a moment too long. They had run all night on a hill with their minds whole, and now they stood and watched what a man and a woman and a bear were doing under a tree.

Then Vellan began to change.

She had seen it a hundred times from the other side. She had never watched from outside, with a lamp on it. The coat drew in like a tide going out. Bone slid under skin with a sound like green wood. The great shoulders narrowed, the muzzle drew back, and a big bearded man lay shuddering among the roots, bare to the waist, the old resin scars pale at his throat and wrists. He coughed. He got his hands under him.

Devan had not looked away. He had not been able to. The lamp was in his hand, and his eyes were on it all, the whole slow wrench of it, and she saw the instant he understood what he was seeing. His shoulders went still.

*Witness,* she thought. *Oh, Devan.*

Vellan raised his head. He was grey under the beard. He looked at Devan's face, then at the lamp, then at the page in her fist. His voice, when it came, was the low grumble of the yard.

"Well," he said. "You saw."

"Yes," Devan said.

"Then you've got it. Any dark you like."

"I know what I have."

Vellan's mouth moved. It might have been a joke. He didn't finish it, and he didn't ask for anything. He sat back on his heels with one hand flat on the root where a blue rag lay, and for the first time since she had known him he looked at no one.

All round them the light was coming. Along the fern line the shapes began to stir, then to go, one by one and then in a rush, loping downhill toward the House and the sheds behind the wall, where the dawn would take them out of sight of every eye. She counted them out. She could not help it. Thirty-one, thirty-two. Behind the trunks, far off, the first of the cries began, the thin ragged turning-back that always came with the sun.

None of them was caged. She had not said a word of any page. She said it again to herself, slowly, to be sure it was true.

Devan sank down beside her. He still held the lamp, and the flame had gone pale in the daylight. He set it on the root, and it ran with him, smoking, a small stubborn light. Blood had dried black down his leg.

"I stood there," he said. "With it open."

"I know."

"Another breath—"

"You didn't."

"You took it." His eyes closed. "I let go."

"You did."

She lay with her head on the root and looked up through the black branches at a sky going pink. Forty pages sat flat and warm against her ribs. One more, tacky and creased, lay curled in her fist. In the clearing behind her a man who had killed Asha knelt in the dirt and did not run.

The night was over. Nothing in the wood had been caged.

She had until the House woke to decide what she was going to do with the forty pages and with Vellan.

# Chapter 28: Daylight Reading

Thirty riders sat their horses on the apron outside the Resin House gate, and every one of them had his back to the wall.

Neela stopped at the edge of the trees. Her calf had stiffened on the walk down into a hot line of pain, and Devan's weight was on her shoulder as hers was on his, two limps making one bad stride. Vellan came last, bare to the waist, with nobody holding his arm.

"Garrison grey," Devan said. "That's Muthu."

The captain swung down and came across the trampled ground. He didn't laugh and he didn't greet them. He looked at the two welts, the blood dried black on Devan's leg, the bear-sized man behind them, and he took it in the way he took in a bad ford.

"Rema posted her order on the hall door the morning you rode," he said. "Plain, so no man could say he misheard. No soldier of Kettamala to give aid to any rogue odiyan, on pain of the queen's displeasure." He rubbed his jaw. "Bhaskaran's holding the ridge road behind us. I brought thirty. Told them to face the road and not turn round, whatever they heard."

"They'll lose their stripes for this," Devan said.

"Some of them. I asked for volunteers and got more than I could mount." He pulled a folded square from his sleeve. Grey wax, unbroken. "My month's letter. Wrote it at the second watch, for the first runner on the goat path. Unni watched me do it and said nothing, which was kind of him."

An iron basket stood by the gate where the tappers burned their rag ends. The hunters had kept it lit all night. Muthu held the letter over it a moment and looked at Neela.

"I spelled your name right," he said.

"Thank you."

"Burned it anyway." He dropped it in. The wax ran grey over the coals and the paper browned and curled. "I'm talking, Neela. Have you noticed? Loud as a drum. I want it noted."

It was almost a joke. She nearly laughed, and the sound cracked in her throat instead. For six years he had carried two loyalties in one pocket. She watched the smoke lift off the last of the second one.

Above them the wall had filled with hunters. Hooked poles, nets, iron rings on chains. Pachan stood below the gate arch, tall and stooped in his grey wrap, with his eyes on his boots.

"Pachan," she said.

He looked up.

"I'm not asking you for anything. The book is down here in a pack, and nobody owns anyone in it by dawn. You can take that to the wall and hold it for him, or you can put down the pole."

A pole came off the wall and rang on the stone. Then another. Then all of them, clattering down like dropped spits. Pachan lifted the bar himself, and the gate dragged open.

"Face the road," Muthu told his men, very quietly. Thirty heads stayed forward.

Neela went in alone first. She would not cross that sill with a soldier at her back.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The forty stood in the yard at noon, and the sun stood straight over them.

They wore blankets and sacking and one another's shirts. The torn resin scars at their throats and wrists had been bound with strips of tapping cloth, and the cloth had bled through. Some looked at her. Most looked at the ground. Pavi had ridden back from the cairn camp at first light, and she stood at the edge of the crowd with her curled forepaw hand pressed to her chest.

Chathu sat on the cook-house step, quite upright, with his hands in his lap. Two of Muthu's men had walked him there. He had taken in the empty hunters' wall and said nothing.

"You two," Neela said to the soldiers. "Out to the apron. Face the road with the rest."

They looked at Devan. He nodded. They went, and the gate shut behind them. Thilak and Kuttu came and stood either side of the step, and Chathu did not turn his head to either.

Devan stood at the gate with the pack at his feet. Vellan sat against the well wall under the eye of nobody. Thilak had put a cup of ginger tea beside her boot without a word.

"I'm going to read your pages aloud," Neela said.

The yard hushed.

"All of them. Aloud, in full, in daylight, in front of whoever you choose. Once I've read yours, you take it, and from then on nobody can read it against you. Not me. Not him." She didn't point at the step. "That's the one way out of the leash that Chathu never told you about."

"What's the price?" That was Kuttu, thin and angry.

"Whoever hears your page hears your shape. The reading makes them your witness. If I read it here, the whole yard has it." She made herself say the rest. "That's thirty-nine new people with a word in their mouths, and I don't get to pick who they are. The soldiers are outside, and they stay outside. But everyone in this yard is a witness by the end. You can ask me to read yours alone, in the shed, to one person you name. You can ask me to wait. If you ask me to wait, I hold it, and a holder is a master, and I don't want to be that to anyone. But it's yours to say."

"And if we say nothing?" Kuttu asked.

"Then I stand here until you do."

Nobody moved for a long moment. A hunter had left a tapping bell on the ground, and it rocked in the dust and went still.

Twenty-two stepped out first, narrow and pale in a man's shirt. "Shed," she said. "Her." She pointed to Pavi. "Nobody else."

"Noon sun, I said."

"The shed has a skylight."

They went in together. When they came out, a quarter hour later, Twenty-two held a strip of tacky paper in both hands and kept saying a word under her breath, four syllables. *Kamalakshi.* She said it like someone trying a stair in the dark.

After that they came. Six asked for the shed. The rest stayed in the sun and heard each other's animals read out in Neela's flat, careful voice. *Thirteen. Boar. Witnessed at first turning, and sworn in resin-ink by Chathu of the Resin House.* A boy flinched at *wolf.* Nobody laughed at *hare.* She read the intake rolls fastened behind the pages, and she watched what they did to faces.

"Thulasi," she read. "A salt-boiler's daughter, of Pazhayangadi."

"My mother boils salt," Thulasi said. Then she sat down in the dust.

"Kumaran, called Kuttu. A ferryman's son."

Kuttu said nothing. He took the page. Twice more as the afternoon went on, she heard him say it to himself, flat and wondering: *Kumaran.*

Her voice went twice. Thilak brought more tea, and she drank it and went on.

When she reached him, he stood with his big hands hanging. A tapper before he was ever a beast, twenty-two years in the House. *Four. Boar.* She finished the roll, and his village, and the name of the man who had signed him over for a debt.

"Burn it," Thilak said.

"The roll burns with the page. You won't get it back."

"I know where I'm from. I know who sold me." He held out his palm. "I don't want it written down anywhere for someone to carry."

She gave it to him. He took it to the gate basket and fed it in, page and roll, his village and the debt together. The ink curled green and black. He stood watching the smoke with his shoulders loose.

She had not known a man could look lighter for losing something.

Madhavi came last of the forty, brindled shape and bitten tongue. She looked at Devan's thigh, then at the ground. "I'm sorry," she said, to the dirt between them.

"It's closed," Devan said. "Take your page."

She took it, and she did not look up.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Vellan's she read standing, because she did not trust her knees.

She had torn it from the ledger herself, on a rock, and it had lain against Devan's ribs most of a night. She unfolded it in the sun with the whole yard watching.

"Thirty. Sloth bear. Witnessed at his first turning, and sworn in resin-ink by Chathu of the Resin House."

Vellan sat very still against the well. When she reached the roll she read it plainly. "Vellan. A joiner's son, of Venmanal. Sold at nine."

"I knew that," Vellan said.

"I know." She walked across the packed earth and held it out. "It's yours. Read against you by no one. Burn it or keep it, but you decide."

His big hand closed on it and stopped. He looked up past her, to the gate. "Not me you should be giving it to."

"You were holding it all night," she said, over her shoulder, to the man at the gate. "I'm done holding it."

Devan came forward. His limp showed with every step. Some of the forty drew back, and Thilak's weight shifted. Pavi caught his arm without turning her head.

"Nobody touches him," Neela said. She said it as a request, and she heard how it sounded. It was her voice, not a master's. "He walks out of here. He owes every one of you, and he knows it, and you can tell him so when you find him. Today he walks."

Devan stopped an arm's length from the man who had killed his sister. She could not read his face. She had thought she could read it by now.

"You saw me," Vellan said. "Dawn. You've got it."

"I have."

"Say it, then. It's all I'd have done." Vellan's mouth did something that was nearly a smile. "Not a bad swap, for the stair."

Devan said nothing for the length of a long breath. Then he turned his back.

He did it slowly. He put himself between the bear and the gate, and he put his bare shoulders and his bitten leg in reach of a man with a joiner's hands. The yard saw it. She saw it. Vellan's eyes widened, and he had no mouth for a joke now.

"Go," Devan said, to the gate.

Vellan got up, which took him two tries. He folded the page small and put it in his waistband. He stood a moment by the well wall as though he had left something there. Then his hitching stride crossed the yard behind Devan's back, and out through Pachan's gate, and across the apron past thirty soldiers staring at the road, and into the wood.

Neela counted his steps. At forty-one she stopped.

"Seventeen," Devan said, still not turning.

"What?"

"The steps. After I said go." He turned at last. His face stood open as a shutter, as it had been under the lamp. "I learned it from someone."

She tried to answer, and it didn't come. She stood and looked at the man who had held the one sentence he had wanted for six years, and had put it in her hand, and had just let it walk. He looked at her in turn, at the stack of empty cloth on the ground where forty pages had been, and the ink on her fingers.

He didn't say it. Neither did she. Her eyes burned, and he nodded once, as if she had.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Kennel nine had straw changed that week, and a clean cloth in one corner.

The aisle was cool and dim, forty-two cages in a row under the oldest roof, forty-one of them filled, and Muthu's water-carriers had been through. Pavi walked behind her with the ledger's back half. Devan stayed at the door. Neela went down on her knees at the iron bars.

The civet lay on the cloth with its chin on its paws. A silver anklet circled one thin hind leg above the foot, its bells cut off long ago.

"Kirri," Neela said. "It's Neela. Ammu's chechi, I think she'd say. I've come to read your page."

The small eyes moved, slowly. They found her face and went away again.

She read it through the bars. *Number Twelve. Civet. Witnessed at first turning, and sworn in resin-ink.* Then the roll, in the plain tongue, at the speed of walking. "Kirthika. A boatwright's daughter, of the coast. Sold at five."

A boatwright's daughter. She read it again, in case it might matter.

Ammu had written four times. Neela told her that, in a low voice, because someone ought to. Ammu had sat in her inn at Kettamala and hummed a fishing song and written a fifth letter that she'd never had time to send.

Kirri's whiskers trembled. The long grey body stayed where it lay.

There was no use waiting for more. She had known it, the way she'd known the hour at which her own second wrist would burn. A caging could not be undone, and a page in the straw could not undo it. She folded the sheet once and slid it through the bars. It lay on the cloth by the silver anklet, in reach of a paw that would never close on it.

"It's yours," she said. "Nobody reads it but you."

The civet blinked once. Then it curled round and slept.

Pavi put a hand on her shoulder. Neela stayed on her knees until she could trust her face, and then she got up and went to ten. There were forty more pages in the book, and she read them one after another through the afternoon, cage by cage, until the light went from the slats. Each time she laid a page down in the straw.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At dusk, Muthu's men took Chathu out through the gate.

He did not struggle. His hands were bound before him, and a strip of clean cloth hung at his neck for after dark. Devan had been very exact about that: gag him at sunset until the sun comes up again, and nobody speaks to him but by daylight. He walked with his uneven left foot on the packed earth and stopped by Neela.

"Seventeen," he said. He said it kindly. "There's a balance, child. Twenty-one years of feeding and keeping and—"

"My name's Neela." Her voice was steady. "And Indu. I keep both. The account's closed."

He looked at the back half of the ledger on Pavi's arm, empty of pages, stitched thread and stubs. "A book is a thing that holds," he said, almost to himself. "Take it away and see what's left."

"We'll see," she said.

They put him on a horse for the Justice at Kettamala. Pachan stood in the gate and watched him go, his long face working. When it was done he turned to Neela with his hands open.

"Someone should keep the kennels," he said. "And the groves. If anyone wants them kept."

"Keep them. Water them every day. Nobody rations resin. No one is the master of the groves. You tap, and each member takes a season's flask from the store tonight, and next season you ask them what they need."

He nodded slowly. "And who's it for, if it's nobody's?"

"Everyone," she said. "That's the whole trouble with it."

They left in the dusk, the forty, in twos and threes, with their bound wrists and a flask apiece. They rode east along the ridge to the old cairn camp, where Bhaskaran's men would hold the stones and look the other way until the court had spoken. Some of them spoke to her as they passed, and some did not. Thulasi put her page to her mouth. Kuttu said the word *Kumaran* once more and grinned like a boy.

Neela cut the back half of the ledger from its cover with a tapping knife. It came away from its stitching with a soft sigh. Before she wrapped it she turned it over once more, and her thumb found the stubs near the end.

Leaves had been cut from the binding with a clean blade. There were a great many. By each stub, in the margin, someone had inked a sunbird no bigger than a lentil, with its wings folded.

She counted them twice. Then she closed the book on them and said nothing. Devan was at the gate with the pack, and she could not make the number mean anything yet.

The emptied back half she kept, wrapped, and what she had meant to keep: the contract books, loose-stitched, and the last quire in its green thread under the wax of a spread-winged bird. Devan took them from her and buckled them under his flap. He did not ask what else she had seen.

"A day's hard ride to Kettamala," he said. "The assize is still sitting."

"I know."

She knew what the court would want, too. A leaf in the black book, in Chathu's hand, with a number at its foot. *Lookout, Seventeen.* She had read eighty-two pages today and given every one of them away. She could not hand that one on to anyone.

Devan did not look at her. He did not ask. She stood with her ruined hands in the dusk, the ledger gone from her, and understood that she would have to decide it on her own.

# Chapter 29: The Assize

Rema sat in his father's chair with the tally chest open at her feet, and Devan stood where the accused would have stood and thought: *eight pillars, and not one of them will hold me up.*

His thigh had stiffened on the ride. Every step across the hall flagstones tugged at a welt that wasn't his, a hot line on his left calf, and it did not matter. He had come in with a canvas pack and the court's whole attention, and he meant to spend it well.

The hall was full. Janu stood on the hearth step with her hands wrapped in her apron. Muthu had the door, and Kandan of Mathoor stood three men down from him with the scar through his brow drawn tight. Bhaskaran had put the garrison along the wall in their grey, and two of his men held the dais step. Rema's escort was long gone down the goat path. She sat alone with her pen.

Neela sat on the household bench, bound hand in her lap. She had washed. She had put on the day kasavu with the torn border, mended. Her eyes stayed off him, and he understood why. Last night he had told her what he would and would not do.

"The leaf names a lookout," he had said, in the dark, with the pebble in his cheek and no reason for it. "I'll put it before Rema. I'll say whose number it is. You needn't stand. I'll say it, and the cost is mine."

She had said nothing. Her breathing had been very even, and he'd known that breath.

Rema laid a board across her knees. Fifty-eight years old, grey at the temples, plain brown wrap. She wrote like a clerk who loved her ink.

"This is an assize of the queen's border, sitting at Kettamala for the high court's record," she said. "I write what is said and what is shown. I decide nothing today but what I will carry south. Warden. Begin."

He opened the pack.

Asha's token came first. The split-ash stick lay on the table in its oilcloth, the red thread still twisted round it, council wax gone dull. Beside it he set the beeswax impression he'd lifted from Bhaskaran's tally. Dot and split, at the sprig's stem. Then the scouts' satchel tally, with its clove-sweet wax. Then thirty-six blanks from Ittan's chest, in a row, each wrapped, each sealed in the same mark.

"Asha, my sister, found the first of these six years ago," he said. "She wrote on a note beside it that she needed a second to be sure. She was dead within the month. Last spring a second came off a raider's body in the lower narrows. It matches. Today I give you thirty-six more, found in a false-bottomed chest on the Keeper's own cart, with no clerk's number on any."

"And what would they have done?" Rema asked, writing.

"Opened the pass. Not to a gate clerk. A gate would never stamp them. To a widow holding the seal in trust, with the Keeper at her elbow to advise." He kept his voice flat. "My marriage writ names her. The same clause. The Keeper proposed the match."

He set Ittan's tally book beside the blanks. Forty leaves, dated sums, pass names. He did not open it for her. Rema had spent two days in it already, alone in the south range, and her pen told him she knew where it would read.

"And the guild contract," she said.

He took the leaf from its fold. It was cut from the Keeper's quire, hooked in the margin, written in Chathu's tidy hand. It was not the other quire, the one stitched in green thread that he had carried in under his flap. That one was a different matter, and he left it buckled. He laid the leaf on the table face down.

"I'll have the man who wrote it speak first," he said. "By daylight, as the charter requires."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They brought Chathu in from the south range with his hands tied before him and the gag cloth hanging at his neck. It was an hour past noon. Devan had counted every tick of the sun to be sure.

The old man walked with his uneven foot on the flagstones and stopped at the foot of the dais. He looked at Rema's board, and at the false-bottomed chest, and last at the hall. His gaze went along the grey line of the garrison without hurry, taking inventory.

"Master of the Resin House," Rema said. "You are not charged here. You are heard."

"I thank the court." The fatherly voice. Soft, unhurried, a man at a ledger by lamplight. "I will speak in what I know, which is accounts. A man who keeps accounts keeps them true. It is the only mercy in my trade."

"Then give me the account," said Rema.

"The cold month, six years gone, by the Keeper's hand." He did not say *Ittan*. He said the office, as if the man had already ceased to be a person. "Four slashes paid, one hook delivered. The buyer was the Keeper of the Passes. The subject was the warden's sister of Kettamala. The service was a bear's."

The hall made a sound. It was not a gasp. It was lower, the noise of sixty men shifting their weight.

"She was found by the stair," Chathu went on, "as the contract required. Closed. Paid in full. I have never in forty years carried a balance past the day it fell due, and the Keeper was as exact."
